Direct Testimony of Billie S. LaConte

1.  INTRODUCTION, QUALIFICATIONS AND SUMMARY
QPlease state your name and business address.
ABillie S. LaConte, 12647 Olive Blvd., Suite 585, St. Louis, MO 63141.
QWhat is your occupation and by whoM are you employed?
AI am an energy advisor and Associate at J. Pollock, Incorporated.
QPlease state your educational background and experience.
AI have a Bachelor of Arts degree in Mathematics from Boston University and a Master’s degree in Business Administration from Washington University.  Since graduating in 1995, I have been engaged in a variety of consulting assignments, including energy procurement and regulatory matters in both the United States and several Canadian provinces. More details are provided in Appendix A.  A list of my appearances is provided in Appendix B.
QON WHOSE BEHALF ARE YOU TESTIFYING IN THIS PROCEEDING? 
AI am testifying on behalf of the Georgia Association of Manufacturers (GAM).  The members of GAM are customers of Georgia Power Company (GPC).  They consume large quantities of electricity, primarily for manufacturing under a variety of tariffs.  GAM members purchase nearly 11% of GPC’s retail energy sales.  
QWHAT ISSUES ARE YOU ADDRESSING IN YOUR TESTIMONY? 
AI shall discuss GPC’s requested return on equity (ROE) and capital structure.
qAre any other witnesses appearing on behalf of GEORGIA ASSoCIATION OF MANUFACTURERS?
AYes.  My colleague, Mr. Jeffry Pollock, will address issues regarding the Alternate Rate Plan (ARP), cost recovery, revenue allocation and other tariff terms and conditions.  Dr. Roger Tutterow will address the state of manufacturing in Georgia.
QAre you sponsoring any exhibits with your testimony? 
AYes.  I’m sponsoring Exhibits BSL-1 through BSL-6.   These exhibits were prepared by me or under my supervision and direction.  In addition, I have prepared Appendix C which provides more detailed information on the various ROE methodologies utilized by analysts to estimate the ROE.
qDoES the fact that you are not addressing all issues in this proceeding constitute an endorsement of the positions of Georgia Power Company, or other parties, on these issues?
ANo.  The fact that I am not addressing every issue should not be interpreted as an endorsement of the proposals in this proceeding.
Summary
qPlease summarize your findings and recommendations.
AMy findings and recommendations are as follows:
Return on Equity
The 11% ROE recommended by GPC’s ROE witness, Mr. James M. Coyne, is based on improper application of widely used and accepted methods, as well the Risk Premium and Expected Earnings methods, which are not widely used. 
Correcting Mr. Coyne’s ROE analyses reveals that GPC’s cost of equity does not exceed 9.83%.
Mr. Coyne further inflates his derived ROEs by including a flotation cost adjustment.  It is unnecessary to include flotation costs because GPC does not directly incur these costs and Mr. Coyne’s flotation cost adjustment is not based on the flotation costs incurred by GPC’s parent company, Southern Company.
GPC’s proposed 11% cost of equity is excessive relative to the returns authorized by other state regulatory commissions nationwide in rate case decisions in 2021 (9.53%) and 2022 year to date (9.47%) for vertically integrated electric investor-owned utilities.  In fact, authorized ROEs have averaged below 10% since 2013.  
Capital Structure
GPC’s proposed 56% common equity ratio is clearly excessive.  Its proposed cost of common equity of 11% is 747 basis points more expensive than its long-term debt cost (3.53%).  The excessive equity ratio results in a higher cost of capital and higher rates than a utility with a more leveraged capital structure.  
On average, the companies in the proxy group with credit ratings similar to Georgia Power’s A- credit rating collectively had an average 53% common equity ratio in 2021, which is 300 basis points lower than the equity ratio GPC is proposing in this case.
GPC’s capital structure is inefficient because it fails to employ an appropriate amount of leverage.  Accordingly, for ratemaking purposes, the Commission should adjust GPC’s common equity ratio so that it is more in line with the average of other vertically integrated electric investor-owned utilities and should not exceed 53%.
2.  Evaluation of Mr. Coyne’s Return on Equity Analysis
qhave you reviewed the basis for Georgia power COmpany’s proposed 11% Return on equity?
AYes. Mr. Coyne’s proposed ROE of 11% is excessive because it is based on erroneous assumptions, which I discuss in-depth later.  Further, he improperly uses a flotation cost adjustment to increase his requested ROE.
qwhat methods does mr. Coyne use to support his recommended 11% return on equity?
AMr. Coyne based his recommendation on four methodologies: Discounted Cash Flow (DCF), Capital Asset Pricing Model (CAPM), Risk Premium and the Expected Earnings method, using a proxy group of companies that are similar to GPC.  Appendix C provides a detailed description of the DCF, CAPM, and Risk Premium methodologies. 
qwhat are the results of mr. coyne’s analyses?
QMr. Coyne’s analyses result in an estimate of GPC’s ROE within the range of 8.99% to 13.55%.   
qdid Mr. Coyne make any adjustments to his return on equity analysis?
AYes.  He made a flotation cost adjustment to the results derived under the various methods, which is discussed more fully later.
qwhat is your assessment of Mr. Coyne’s return on equity analysis?
AMr. Coyne’s ROE estimates are too high for numerous reasons. His CAPM, Risk Premium, and Expected Earnings analyses use unrealistic assumptions and his flotation cost adjustment is unnecessary because it does not reflect the actual flotation costs incurred by GPC.
Proxy Group
qwhat is a proxy group?
AA proxy group is a group of companies involved in operations similar to the utility being analyzed.
qwhy is a proxy group relevant in determining an appropriate return on equity?
AA proxy group is relevant because it provides a group of companies that are comparable in risk to GPC, hence estimating the cost of equity for the proxy group represents the economic opportunity costs that have an impact on the ROE for GPC.
qdo you agree with the group of companies that mr. coyne included in his proxy group?
A Yes. Mr. Coyne’s criteria for inclusion in his proxy group are based on industry standards and the companies in his proxy group are generally comparable to GPC.
Mr. Coyne’s DCF Analysis
qdo you have any concerns regarding mr. coyne’s discounted cash flow analyses?
ANo.  Mr. Coyne’s DCF analyses are based on sound assumptions regarding stock prices, projected dividend yields, and projected growth rates for each company in his proxy group.  Therefore, the results of his DCF analyses, which produce a range of ROEs, from 7.99% to 9.99%, are reasonable and representative of the cost of equity for GPC. I have selected the result of Mr. Coyne’s 180-day mean average DCF (9.09%) because the time period for the stock price better captures recent conditions in the stock market over a longer term, including the 30-day and 90-day data.
Flaws With Mr. Coyne’s CAPM Analysis
qwhat is the capital asset pricing model?
AThe CAPM is a risk premium method that is used to estimate the ROE.  It states that the expected return of a security equals the risk-free rate plus a risk premium.  Simply put, investors require a premium over the risk-free rate if they are going to invest in a riskier security.  
qwhat capital asset pricing model methods did Mr. Coyne use?
AMr. Coyne’s CAPM analysis uses various estimates of the market risk premium (MRP), based on projected data.  
His first CAPM analysis use a projected risk-free rate of 3.4%, and the average of his projected long-term market risk premiums based on expected market returns (15.87%, 14.03% and 15.63%).  His average expected market return is 15.18%, which produces an 11.78% MRP.  His second CAPM method uses the current risk-free rate (2.72%) and the same projected MRP which produced an 12.46% MRP.  Mr. Coyne used two beta estimates.  The first is based on the average beta as assigned by Value Line for each company in his proxy group, or 0.88.  The second is the average beta for the proxy group using Bloomberg data, or 0.85.  Using this data, he estimates the return on equity for each company in his proxy group.  
qwhat is the estimated return on equity using mr. Coyne’s capital asset pricing model methodology?
AMr. Coyne’s estimated ROE is 13.6% using the projected long-term risk-free rate and 13.5% using the current 30-day average risk-free rate.
qdo you have concerns with Mr. Coyne’s capital asset pricing model return on equity estimate?
AYes.  Two of his projected MRPs are based on the projected growth of stocks in the S&P 500.  However, he includes companies with negative growth and growth above 20% which results in a distorted MRP. Both of those growth estimates are unsustainable and should be removed.  Correcting the projected MRP reduces the average MRP to 8.28% (from 11.78%) and lowers the estimated ROE to 10.57%.
While I agree with Mr. Coyne’s use of a projected MRP, he failed to estimate the ROE using a historical MRP.  Therefore, his estimated CAPM ROE is significantly overstated.
qis it a common practice to also use the long-term historical market Risk Premium to estimate the capital asset pricing model return on equity?
AYes.  A long-term estimate of the historical MRP is a commonly used method which is based on actual, historical MRPs over several decades and provides a reliable estimate of the expected MRP.  Long-term data provides the MRP throughout many economic cycles, providing a reasonable expectation of future performance.
qwhy does long-term historical data provide a good approximation of future risk premiums?
ADr. Roger A. Morin, whom Mr. Coyne quotes in his testimony, states in New Regulatory Finance “it is reasonable to believe that long-run average realized returns provide an unbiased estimate of what were expected returns.”  Further, Dr. Morin states “a regulatory body should rely on the results of both historical and prospective studies in arriving at an appropriate risk premium...”
qhave you calculated the return on equity using the capital asset pricing model and long-term historical market risk premium?
AYes.  The historical MRP (1926-2021) is 7.40%, based on Kroll’s 2022 SBBI Valuation Yearbook (formerly published by Duff & Phelps).  Using the projected 3.4% long-term risk-free rate, Mr. Coyne’s 0.87 average beta and the 7.40% historical MRP, the estimated ROE is 9.84%.
Impact of Correcting Mr. Coyne’s DCF and CAPM ROE Analysis
qif the various flaws in Mr. Coyne’s return on equity analyses are corrected, how would this affect Georgia Power COmpany’s estimated return on equity?
AGPC’s ROE would be significantly lower after correcting the errors in Mr. Coyne’s analysis and removing his flotation cost adjustment.  Based on the results of my revisions, it is clear that GPC’s cost of equity should not exceed 9.83%. The derivation of the 9.83% is shown below in Table 1.

My revised ROE reflects the inclusion of Mr. Coyne’s 180-day DCF analysis, my CAPM results (1) using the corrected projected MRP and (2) using the historical MRP.  Additionally, the flotation cost adjustment was excluded because GPC does not issue stock.
qwhat is the impact on georgia Power Company’s revenue requirement using your revised return on equity?
AReplacing GPC’s requested 11% ROE with the revised 9.83% ROE reduces GPC’s revenue requirement by $299 million in 2023. Exhibit BSL-1 provides the detailed calculations.
Flaws With Mr. Coyne’s Risk Premium Analyses
qdo you have any comments regarding the risk premium method?
AYes.  The risk premium is not as widely accepted as the DCF and CAPM methodologies when estimating the ROE for a regulated utility.  Mr. Coyne’s Risk Premium method analyzes the relationship between historical authorized ROEs and the risk-free rate.  The Risk Premium method is sometimes used by Commissions as a “check” on the reasonableness of the DCF and CAPM results.  Therefore, while the Risk Premium method may not factor in directly when estimating the ROE, it may contribute by measuring the reasonableness of the DCF and CAPM results.
qis mr. coyne’s risk premium analysis valid?
ANo.  Mr. Coyne’s Risk Premium method estimates the ROE based on the historical relationship between allowed ROEs in electric utility rate cases and the risk-free rate at the time the ROEs were authorized, from 1992 through February 2022.  Using this data, Mr. Coyne created a regression analysis to estimate the risk premium.  Mr. Coyne’s regression analysis purports to demonstrate that there is an inverse relationship between equity risk premium and interest rates.  However, his regression analysis does not take into account other factors that can affect the equity risk premium (e.g.,  different Federal monetary and fiscal policies, and economic risk, such as employment, consumption and growth).  These factors can have an impact on authorized ROEs due to their effect on market risk, which may cause regulators to adjust their authorized ROEs.  The change in interest rates is one of many factors that may affect a utility’s authorized ROE.
qhave you revised mr. coyne’s risk premium analysis?
AYes, using data provided by Mr. Coyne, I used the long-term average equity risk premium of 6.06% and long-term risk-free rate of 3.4% to derive an estimated ROE of 9.46%.  The long-term risk premium estimate is calculated using authorized ROEs and the long-term risk-free rate at the time the ROE was authorized.  The long-term risk premium estimate recognizes that the risk premium can fluctuate depending on market conditions and investor expectations.  Therefore, using the average risk premium over this time-period is a reasonable method to estimate GPC’s cost of equity.  
qhow do the results of your risk premium analysis compare to your corrected return on equity?
AWhile the 9.46% risk premium ROE is slightly below my revised average 9.83% ROE, it provides support that my revised ROE is not too low and provides a reasonable estimate of GPC’s ROE. 
Flaws with Expected Earnings
qplease describe the expected earnings methodology.
AThe Expected Earnings methodology estimates the cost of equity using projected returns on book equity.
qwhat is mr. coyne’s estimated return on equity using his expected earnings methodology?
AMr. Coyne’s Expected Earnings analysis results in a 11.03% and a 11.08% ROE, based on the mean and median of the results, respectively.
qwhy do you disagree with the expected earnings methodology?
AThe Expected Earnings methodology is not a reliable method to estimate the ROE because it represents a forecast return on book equity and not a required return or cost of equity.  
qwhat do you mean by the estimated earnings method represents a forecast return on equity and not a required return or cost of equity?
AThis methodology uses forecasted returns on book equity.  This is not a reasonable proxy for investors’ expected market returns.  It is a book accounting return and does not reflect investors’ market expectations.  FERC rejected this methodology in 2019, explaining “Because an investor cannot purchase a utility’s common stock at book value and must instead pay the prevailing market price for common equity, the utility’s expected earned return on book value is indicative of neither what an investor can expect to earn on an investment in the utility’s common stock nor what return an investor requires to invest in the utility’s common stock.”  
As such, Mr. Coyne’s Expected Earnings method is not a reliable proxy for the estimated ROE for GPC and should be rejected.
Flotation Costs
qwhat are flotation costs?
AFlotation costs include two components.  The first component is the actual cost paid by a company to the underwriter for issuing stock.  The second is indirect and represents the potential dilutive impact due to the issuance of new stock.
qhow do flotation costs affect the return on equity determination?
AFlotation costs increase the ROE.  For example, Mr. Coyne made an upward adjustment of eight basis points.
qshould georgia power Company’s return on equity include a flotation cost adjustment?
ANo.  First, Mr. Coyne’s estimate of flotation costs was based on the companies in his proxy group, not on any actual flotation costs incurred by GPC.  This is because GPC is a regulated utility that does not issue stock and, therefore, does not incur flotation costs.  The flotation costs are incurred by GPC’s parent company, the Southern Company.  Therefore, a flotation cost adjustment is not necessary.
qif georgia power company is responsible for the southern company’s flotation costs, should the commission approve mr. coyne’s adjustment?
ANo.  As noted above, Mr. Coyne’s adjustment is not based on actual flotation costs incurred by GPC and, therefore, is unnecessary and unwarranted.  If the Commission allows GPC to recover flotation costs, it should be based on a reasonable projection of flotation costs that GPC’s parent company will incur during the ARP period.
Summary of the Flaws With Mr. Coyne’s ROE Analysis
QPLEASE SUMMARIZE YOUR critique OF Mr. Coyne’s Return on equity ANALYSeS.
AMr. Coyne’s recommended ROE of 11% does not recognize GPC’s reduced risk due to the regulatory mechanisms it has in place which reduce regulatory lag and income variability, as discussed more fully in my next section.  Further, his reasoning for adjusting GPC’s ROE due to flotation costs is inaccurate and should be rejected.
Mr. Coyne relies on four methods to estimate an ROE for GPC.  His CAPM analysis includes incorrect estimates of the projected MRP and excludes the historical MRP, which results in overstating the ROE.
The Risk Premium analysis uses a regression analysis that relies solely on the relationship between interest rates and the equity risk premium.  It does not include other factors that could impact the risk premium, such as Federal monetary and fiscal policies.  Ignoring other factors that affect the equity risk premium produces inaccurate ROE estimates and the results should be rejected.
The Expected Earnings methodology is not a common method used to estimate the cost of equity for a regulated utility.  The utility’s expected return on book value is indicative of neither what an investor can expect to earn on an investment in the utility’s common stock, nor what return an investor requires to invest in the utility’s common stock.  Therefore, it should be rejected.
Lastly, the flotation cost adjustment is misplaced because GPC does not issue stock.
3.  FACTORS impacting THE RETURN ON EQUITY
Qwhat factors should the commission consider when determining an appropriate return on equity for GEorgia Power Company?
AFirst and foremost, a fair and reasonable ROE should be based on results derived from accepted methodologies, such as the DCF and CAPM methods, and using the Risk Premium method as a check on the reasonableness of the DCF and CAPM results.  Further, the inputs and assumptions used in applying the accepted methodologies should consider current financial and economic realities, such as:
GPC’s financial and business risk; and
The ROEs authorized by state regulatory commissions.
I discuss each of these considerations in detail below.
Financial Risk
qwhat is financial risk?
AFinancial risk represents variability in income. Quantifying financial risk includes an analysis of a utility’s financial statements and cash flow/leverage analyses.  To the extent such variability is small, or has been reduced by other means, the risk to GPC’s shareholders is lower.
qwhat factors can influence the level of financial risk for a particular utility?
AFinancial risk is highly influenced by the regulatory climate.  All other things being equal, a utility is less risky if it is regulated by a Commission that:
authorizes rates for a multi-year period;
authorizes separate piecemeal cost recovery clauses and revenue decoupling that adjusts rates automatically outside of base rate cases; and 
employs other constructive ratemaking practices.
Qwhat do you mean by piecemeal cost recovery clauses?
APiecemeal (or single-issue) ratemaking refers to a utility being allowed to implement adjustment clauses that permit it to change rates outside of a base rate case, while ignoring the utility’s earnings. Utilities are able to mitigate risk by implementing an adjustment clause that allows rates to change on an expedited basis, outside the context of a traditional rate case in which all costs and revenues would be considered.   
qdoes georgia power Company currently have any adjustment clauses in place that reduce its variability in income and lower its financial risk?
AYes.  GPC has numerous adjustment clauses in place that reduce its financial risk, such as Fuel Cost Recovery, Environmental Compliance Cost Recovery, Demand Side Management rider, the Nuclear Construction Cost Recovery, and an Interim Fuel Rider.  GPC’s cash flow is improved due to its adjustment clauses and positively affects its credit quality, which reduces its financial risk.
qhow does lower risk impact Georgia power Company’s expected cost of capital?
ALower risk leads to lower financial volatility.  GPC has several tools available to reduce its regulatory lag, including the aforementioned adjustment clauses. Therefore, investors’ required return for GPC will be lower.  Hence, these risk-reducing measures support a reduction to GPC’s requested ROE of 11%.
qdoes the proposed alternate rate plan affect Georgia power COmpany’s risk profile?
AYes.  The proposed ARP is a three-year plan in which rates are based on test-year ending July 31, 2023, modified for a levelization adjustment. The ARP provides GPC with rate stability and predictability, reduces regulatory lag by allowing the Company more-timely recovery of costs, and is considered favorable by the credit rating agencies.  
qhow will the proposed three-year alternate Rate Plan affect Georgia power Company’s risk profile?
AThe proposed three-year ARP will lower GPC’s risk because it will reduce its business risk (regulatory lag) and its financial risk (revenue volatility).  The ARP has lower risk than a traditional ratemaking plan because rates will be adjusted to recover GPC’s levelized revenue deficiency over the three-year period.  Under normal ratemaking conditions, GPC would file a rate case every year to increase its rates. Having an ARP lowers the risk faced by GPC because it provides predictable and reliable rates, reduces regulatory lag and also reduces revenue volatility.  
ROEs Authorized By State Regulatory Commissions
qWhat returns on equity have state regulatory commissions recently authorized for regulated ELECTRIC INVESTOR-OWNED utilities?
AFrom roughly 2010 to 2021, average authorized ROEs ranged from 9.53% to 10.42%.  However, in the most recent years this number has been trending downward.  The national average authorized ROE for vertically integrated electric utilities was 9.53% in 2021 and 9.47% in the first six months of 2022 as reported by Regulatory Research Associates (RRA), a subsidiary of S&P Global.  An excerpt of the RRA Report is provided in Exhibit BSL-2.  These averages reflect the actual decisions from rate cases in Georgia as well as decisions by other state regulatory commissions in general rate cases.  As discussed later, this is a reasonable basis for assessing the trend in authorized ROEs.
qhow does Georgia power Company’s requested return on equity compare to the national average return on equity for ELECTRIC utilities?
AGPC’s requested 11% ROE is 153 basis points higher than the national average authorized ROE for other vertically integrated electric utilities of 9.47% during the first six months of 2022.  
qhow would Georgia power COmpany’s projected revenue deficiency be affected if the commission set Georgia Power Company’s return on equity at the RRA national average for 2022?
AGPC’s projected revenue deficiency would decrease by $391 million.  The details of this calculation are shown in Exhibit BSL-3.  
QSHOULD THE COMMISSION consider DETERMINATIONS made in other regulatory jurisdictions?
AYes.  This Commission should be informed by the determinations made by other state regulatory commissions.  This is because the trend in authorized ROEs indicates that, in general, utilities’ financial and credit risks are lower than in the past due, in part, to the implementation of a plethora of cost recovery mechanisms and other enhancements that have reduced regulatory lag. The national authorized average ROE is an important consideration when determining the ROE for GPC, however, as demonstrated previously, my review of Mr. Coyne’s quantitative analysis supports a 9.83% ROE. 
4.  Capital Structure
qwhat is georgia power Company’s proposed capital structure?
AGPC’s proposed capital structure includes 44% debt and 56% equity, which is an excessive equity ratio.
qwhat is the basis for your statement that georgia power Company’s proposed equity ratio is excessive?
AGPC’s proposed equity ratio is excessive because it results in unnecessarily higher costs to ratepayers.  Table 2 summarizes the average equity ratio of each vertically integrated electric investor-owned utility in the most recent rate cases decided during the period 2016 through 2022 to date.
Table 2
Average Authorized Financial Equity Ratios
2016 - 2022
Year
Average Common Equity Ratio
2016
50.43%
2017
50.94%
2018
49.83%
2019
51.99%
2020
50.99%
2021
51.23%
2022
51.58%
As shown above, the average common equity ratio in 2022 is 442 basis points lower than GPC’s proposed 56% common equity ratio.  GPC’s proposed equity ratio is excessive, as compared to the national average ratio and considering GPC’s requested 11% ROE.  For example, in February 2022 Southwestern Public Service Company was authorized a 54.72% common equity ratio; however, the authorized return on equity was 9.35%, or 165 basis points lower than GPC’s requested ROE.
qhow does georgia power Company’s proposed common equity ratio compare to the average common equity ratio for the companies in the proxy group?
AThe average common equity ratio for the companies in Mr. Coyne’s proxy group is 52.44%.  The average common equity ratio includes companies with credit ratings that are lower than GPC’s (A-).  If we remove companies with a lower credit, the average common equity ratio is 53%, which is significantly below GPC’s proposed 56% equity ratio.  Exhibit BSL-4 provides each proxy group company’s credit rating and common equity ratio.  A company’s common equity ratio contributes to its credit score, therefore a company with a credit rating that is similar to GPC’s should have a common equity ratio that is supportive of its rating and a good proxy for GPC’s common equity ratio.  As demonstrated in Exhibit BSL-4, GPC’s proposed common equity ratio is significantly above the average common equity ratio of its peers and should be rejected.
qare there any consequences of using more equity and less debt to finance the utility’s rate base?
AYes.  GPC’s higher percentage of equity and lower percentage of debt in its capital structure lowers its financial risk.  Furthermore, common equity is more expensive than debt.  In this case, GPC is proposing a 56% common equity ratio, but the proposed cost of debt is 3.53%, which is 747 basis points lower than the proposed 11% return on equity.  A utility with too much equity in its capital structure has a higher cost of capital than a utility with a more balanced common equity ratio.  All else being equal, the higher the overall common equity ratio, the greater the benefits to GPC’s shareholders and executives and the higher the rates all GPC retail customers will bear.  GPC should not be rewarded for its overly conservative use of debt and high equity ratio. 
qwhat is the impact on georgia Power Company’s revenue requirement if its common equity ratio is reduced to the average common equity ratio of the companies in the proxy group with the same credit rating?
AIf GPC’s financial common equity ratio is reduced to 53%, its revenue requirement would be $108 million lower.  The detailed calculations are shown in Exhibit BSL-5.
qwhat is the impact on georgia Power Company’s revenue requirement if its return on equity is corrected and its common equity ratio is reduced to your corrected average common equity ratio?
AIf GPC’s ROE is reduced to my corrected ROE of 9.83% and its common equity ratio is reduced to 53% (which, as explained earlier, is GPC’s proxy group, excluding companies with credit rating below A-) its revenue requirement would be $392 million lower in 2023.  The detailed calculations are shown in Exhibit BSL-6.
qwhat do you recommend regarding georgia Power Company’s common equity ratio?
AI recommend that GPC’s capital structure should be more in line with the average equity ratio for companies in Mr. Coyne’s proxy group that have similar credit ratings as GPC.  Accordingly, I recommend that GPC’s equity ratio not exceed 53%.  

5.  Conclusion
qWhat findings should the commission make based on the issues addressed in your testimony?
AThe Commission should make the following findings:  
GPC’s return on equity should be no higher than 9.83%.
GPC’s common equity ratio should be no higher than the average common equity ratio for companies in the proxy group with similar credit ratings as GPC, or 53%.
qDoes this conclude your direct testimony?
AYes.
APPENDIX A
Qualifications of Billie S. LaConte
qplease state your name and business address. 
ABillie S. LaConte.  My business mailing address is 12647 Olive Blvd., Suite 585, St. Louis, Missouri 63141.  
qwhat is your occupation and by whom are you employed?  
AI am an energy advisor and am currently employed by J. Pollock, Incorporated as Associate Consultant.  
qplease state your educational background and experience.  
AI have a Bachelor of Arts Degree in Mathematics from Boston University and a Master’s degree in Business Administration from Washington University.    
Upon graduation in May 1995, I joined Drazen Consulting Group, Inc. (DCGI).  DCGI was incorporated in 1995 assuming the utility rate and economic consulting activities of Drazen Associates, Inc., active since 1937.  I joined J.Pollock in May 2015. 
During my tenure at DCGI and J.Pollock my work has focused on revenue requirement issues, cost of capital (return on equity and capital structure), cost allocation, rate design, sales and price forecasts, power cost forecasting, electric restructuring issues, integrated resource plans, formula rate plans, asset management agreements and contract interpretation.  
I have been engaged in a wide range of consulting assignments including energy and regulatory matters in both the United States and several Canadian provinces.  This has included advising clients on economic and strategic issues concerning the natural gas pipeline, oil pipeline, electric, wastewater and water utilities.  I have prepared cost allocation and rate design studies to provide timely support to clients engaged in settlement negotiations in electric and gas utilities, provided power cost forecasting studies to assist clients in project planning and negotiated contracts with electric utilities for standby services and interruptible rates.  I have also prepared studies on electric and gas utilities’ performance-based rates (PBR) and benchmarking programs to evaluate their success and to provide recommendations on methods to be used.  I worked on contract interpretation to resolve contract disputes for several clients.  I have provided financial and cost of service analysis for natural gas pipelines certificate approval from the Federal Energy and Regulatory Commission (FERC) and the Canadian National Energy Board (NEB).  Additionally, I completed the Corporate Credit Rating Analysis course presented by Moody’s Analytics.  
I have worked on various projects located in many states and several Canadian provinces including Alberta, British Columbia, Saskatchewan, Nova Scotia and Quebec.  I have testified before the state regulatory commissions of Arkansas, Florida, Georgia, Iowa, Louisiana, Michigan, Minnesota, Missouri, New Mexico, Pennsylvania, Texas and South Carolina, and the provincial regulatory boards of Alberta and Nova Scotia.  I similarly have appeared before the St. Louis Metropolitan Sewer District Commission.

APPENDIX C
Return on Equity Methodologies

Single Stage Discounted Cash Flow

The discounted cash flow model is used by investors to determine the present value of a stock, based on future cash flows (dividends), which are discounted by the stock’s known return and its forecast growth.  
The formula is:
 
Where:
P = current stock price
D = dividend yield
r = rate of return
g = growth rate
We can re-arrange the formula thus:

In other words, the expected return equals (1) the current dividend rate, plus (2) the expected growth in dividends.  The expected growth in dividends is also measured by the expected growth in earnings.
The stock prices are based on the average closing prices for the past 30 days. The 30-day average is used to ensure that the results reflect stock prices over a period of time that is not overly reliant on any particular events affecting stock prices on a given day and that represent capital market conditions over the past month.
 The growth rates are the forecast earnings per share growth rates for the next five years from Value Line, Yahoo! Finance, and Zacks.  The dividends are forecast  figures, also from Value Line.  These have been adjusted to reflect any quarterly adjustments during the year. 
Multi-Stage Discounted Cash Flow Methodology

A multi-stage DCF analysis uses three separate growth estimates or stages.  The first stage measures the near-term growth rate based on the analysts’ forecast earnings growth used in my constant growth DCF analysis.  The second stage (intermediate-term) growth rates are linear interpolations of the first and third stage growth rates.  The third stage (long term) is the forecast of the long-term growth rate of gross domestic product (GDP).  Using these inputs, the model calculates the required internal rate of return to meet these dividend growth rates, or the ROE.  I calculated three multi-stage ROE analyses using a low, mean, and high first year growth rate that is based on the analysts’ forecast estimates of growth, similar to the constant growth DCF analysis.
The multi-stage method is used because analysts’ growth rates for the first stage may not be sustainable over the long term.  The multi-stage model recognizes short-term growth (whether it be higher or lower than the long term), but also accounts for a more realistic, long-term growth rate.  Analysts’ growth rates should be viewed in conjunction with other growth estimates to achieve a reasonable forecast of expected earnings.
The long-term growth in GDP is used because the underlying assumption is that mature, established companies can grow at a rate that is similar to, or lower than, the GDP growth rate.  While some companies in the economy will grow faster than GDP for a while, this cannot happen consistently over a long period. 
Capital Asset Pricing Model
The CAPM is a Risk Premium method that is used to estimate the ROE.  It states that the expected return of a security equals the risk-free rate plus a risk premium.  Simply put, investors require a premium over the risk-free rate if they are going to invest in a riskier security.  The formula for the CAPM is:
Expected ROE = Risk-Free Rate + β*Market Risk Premium 
The equity risk premium for a particular stock is the MRP times the stock’s beta (β).  The MRP is the difference between the return on the market on average (i.e., the S&P 500) and the risk-free rate.  Thus, it is the premium that reflects the risk on an average stock.  Beta is the price volatility of that stock relative to the market as a whole.  Thus, the risk premium for a specific stock equals the average MRP times the beta.  Since utility stocks are lower risk than the average stock, the risk premium for a utility stock is lower than the average MRP.  Multiplying the beta times the MRP gives the appropriate risk premium for the company (or group of comparable companies) being studied.
The risk-free rate is the projected yield on 30-year U.S. Treasury bonds.  This rate is considered to be risk-free because the return is guaranteed by the U.S. government.
Two MRP estimates may be used, including the historical MRP estimate and a projected MRP.  The historical MRP is based on historical data dating back to 1996. The projected MRP is based on the projected median three-to-five year price appreciation of the 1,700 stocks from Value Line and the projected median dividend yield over the next 12 months for all dividend paying stocks.  The forecast annual return is based on the forecast annual growth rate of the stocks plus the forecast median dividend produces a projected annual return.  The projected risk-free rate is deducted from the projected annual return to determine the projected MRP.
Beta measures the volatility of a security in comparison to the market as a whole.  A beta equal to 1.00 means that a stock’s price fluctuates exactly as the market as a whole.  A beta higher than 1.00 implies that the stock’s price is more volatile than the market; a beta less than 1.00 implies the stock’s price is less volatile than the market. The standard formula for estimating beta is the covariance between a security’s return and the return of the market divided by the variance of the market returns over a specified period.  Beta is based on the betas provided in Value Line.Beta measures the amount of compensation an investor needs to take on additional risk, as compared to the risk-free rate.  Value Line’s method to estimate beta is based on “a regression analysis of the relationship between weekly percentage changes in the price of a stock and weekly percentage changes in the NYSE Composite Index over a period of five years.  Value Line then adjusts these Betas to account for their long-term tendency to converge toward 1.00.”
The risk-free rate is based on the projected return on long-term treasuries.  This rate is considered risk-free because the security is backed by the U.S. government.
Risk Premium Method
The Risk Premium method estimates the ROE for a utility as the sum of a bond yield plus a risk premium yield.  The bond yield is the projected return on the long-term government bond plus the historical risk premium.  The risk premium is a measure of the additional return an investor requires due to the additional risk of the security.  The risk premium is the measure of the difference between the historical authorized return on equity regulated electric, natural gas or water utilities and the historical yield on 30-year Treasury bonds.

 1   Return on Equity
 2              •   The 11% ROE recommended by GPC’s ROE witness, Mr. James
 3                  M. Coyne, is based on improper application of widely used and
 4                  accepted methods, as well the Risk Premium and Expected
 5                  Earnings methods, which are not widely used.
 6              •   Correcting Mr. Coyne’s ROE analyses reveals that GPC’s cost of
 7                  equity does not exceed 9.83%.
 8              •   Mr. Coyne further inflates his derived ROEs by including a flotation
 9                  cost adjustment. It is unnecessary to include flotation costs
10                  because GPC does not directly incur these costs and Mr. Coyne’s
11                  flotation cost adjustment is not based on the flotation costs incurred
12                  by GPC’s parent company, Southern Company.
13              •   GPC’s proposed 11% cost of equity is excessive relative to the
14                  returns authorized by other state regulatory commissions
15                  nationwide in rate case decisions in 2021 (9.53%) and 2022 year to
16                  date (9.47%) for vertically integrated electric investor-owned
17                  utilities. In fact, authorized ROEs have averaged below 10% since
18                  2013.

19   Capital Structure
20              •   GPC’s proposed 56% common equity ratio is clearly excessive. Its
21                  proposed cost of common equity of 11% is 747 basis points more
22                  expensive than its long-term debt cost (3.53%). The excessive
23                  equity ratio results in a higher cost of capital and higher rates than
24                  a utility with a more leveraged capital structure.
25              •   On average, the companies in the proxy group with credit ratings
26                  similar to Georgia Power’s A- credit rating collectively had an
27                  average 53% common equity ratio in 2021, which is 300 basis
28                  points lower than the equity ratio GPC is proposing in this case.
29              •   GPC’s capital structure is inefficient because it fails to employ an
30                  appropriate amount of leverage. Accordingly, for ratemaking
31                  purposes, the Commission should adjust GPC’s common equity
32                  ratio so that it is more in line with the average of other vertically
33                  integrated electric investor-owned utilities and should not exceed
34                  53%.

 8   Q    WHAT IS A PROXY GROUP?

 9   A    A proxy group is a group of companies involved in operations similar to the utility being

10        analyzed.


11   Q    WHY IS A PROXY GROUP RELEVANT IN DETERMINING AN APPROPRIATE

12        RETURN ON EQUITY?

13   A    A proxy group is relevant because it provides a group of companies that are

14        comparable in risk to GPC, hence estimating the cost of equity for the proxy group

15        represents the economic opportunity costs that have an impact on the ROE for GPC.

 1       Q      IS IT A COMMON PRACTICE TO ALSO USE THE LONG-TERM HISTORICAL

 2              MARKET RISK PREMIUM TO ESTIMATE THE CAPITAL ASSET PRICING MODEL

 3              RETURN ON EQUITY?

 4       A      Yes. A long-term estimate of the historical MRP is a commonly used method which is

 5              based on actual, historical MRPs over several decades and provides a reliable estimate

 6              of the expected MRP. Long-term data provides the MRP throughout many economic

 7              cycles, providing a reasonable expectation of future performance.


 8       Q      WHY      DOES      LONG-TERM         HISTORICAL        DATA      PROVIDE        A   GOOD

 9              APPROXIMATION OF FUTURE RISK PREMIUMS?

10       A      Dr. Roger A. Morin, whom Mr. Coyne quotes in his testimony, states in New Regulatory

11              Finance “it is reasonable to believe that long-run average realized returns provide an

12              unbiased estimate of what were expected returns.”8 Further, Dr. Morin states “a

13              regulatory body should rely on the results of both historical and prospective studies in

14              arriving at an appropriate risk premium...”9


15       Q      HAVE YOU CALCULATED THE RETURN ON EQUITY USING THE CAPITAL

16              ASSET PRICING MODEL AND LONG-TERM HISTORICAL MARKET RISK

17              PREMIUM?

19              Yearbook (formerly published by Duff & Phelps).10 Using the projected 3.4% long-term

20              risk-free rate, Mr. Coyne’s 0.87 average beta and the 7.40% historical MRP, the

21              estimated ROE is 9.84%.

 1    Flaws With Mr. Coyne’s Risk Premium Analyses

 2    Q        DO YOU HAVE ANY COMMENTS REGARDING THE RISK PREMIUM METHOD?

 3    A        Yes. The risk premium is not as widely accepted as the DCF and CAPM methodologies

 4             when estimating the ROE for a regulated utility. Mr. Coyne’s Risk Premium method

 5             analyzes the relationship between historical authorized ROEs and the risk-free rate.

 6             The Risk Premium method is sometimes used by Commissions as a “check” on the

 7             reasonableness of the DCF and CAPM results. Therefore, while the Risk Premium

 8             method may not factor in directly when estimating the ROE, it may contribute by

 9             measuring the reasonableness of the DCF and CAPM results.


10    Q        IS MR. COYNE’S RISK PREMIUM ANALYSIS VALID?

11    A        No. Mr. Coyne’s Risk Premium method estimates the ROE based on the historical

12             relationship between allowed ROEs in electric utility rate cases and the risk-free rate at

13             the time the ROEs were authorized, from 1992 through February 2022. Using this data,

14             Mr. Coyne created a regression analysis to estimate the risk premium.11 Mr. Coyne’s

15             regression analysis purports to demonstrate that there is an inverse relationship

16             between equity risk premium and interest rates. However, his regression analysis does

17             not take into account other factors that can affect the equity risk premium (e.g., different

18             Federal monetary and fiscal policies, and economic risk, such as employment,

19             consumption and growth). These factors can have an impact on authorized ROEs due

20             to their effect on market risk, which may cause regulators to adjust their authorized

21             ROEs. The change in interest rates is one of many factors that may affect a utility’s

22             authorized ROE.


     11   Direct Testimony of James M. Coyne at 35.

                                                                          2. Evaluation of Mr. Coyne’s
                                                                                         ROE Analysis
                                                 J.POLLOCK
                                                 INCORPORATED

                                                                            Billie S. LaConte
                                                                            Direct
                                                                            Page 11

 1   Q    HAVE YOU REVISED MR. COYNE’S RISK PREMIUM ANALYSIS?

 2   A    Yes, using data provided by Mr. Coyne, I used the long-term average equity risk

 3        premium of 6.06% and long-term risk-free rate of 3.4% to derive an estimated ROE of

 4        9.46%. The long-term risk premium estimate is calculated using authorized ROEs and

 5        the long-term risk-free rate at the time the ROE was authorized. The long-term risk

 6        premium estimate recognizes that the risk premium can fluctuate depending on market

 7        conditions and investor expectations. Therefore, using the average risk premium over

 8        this time-period is a reasonable method to estimate GPC’s cost of equity.


 9   Q    HOW DO THE RESULTS OF YOUR RISK PREMIUM ANALYSIS COMPARE TO

10        YOUR CORRECTED RETURN ON EQUITY?

11   A    While the 9.46% risk premium ROE is slightly below my revised average 9.83% ROE,

12        it provides support that my revised ROE is not too low and provides a reasonable

13        estimate of GPC’s ROE.


14   Flaws with Expected Earnings

15   Q    PLEASE DESCRIBE THE EXPECTED EARNINGS METHODOLOGY.

16   A    The Expected Earnings methodology estimates the cost of equity using projected

17        returns on book equity.


18   Q    WHAT IS MR. COYNE’S ESTIMATED RETURN ON EQUITY USING HIS EXPECTED

19        EARNINGS METHODOLOGY?

20   A    Mr. Coyne’s Expected Earnings analysis results in a 11.03% and a 11.08% ROE, based

21        on the mean and median of the results, respectively.


                                                                 2. Evaluation of Mr. Coyne’s
                                                                                ROE Analysis
                                          J.POLLOCK
                                          INCORPORATED

                                                                                  Billie S. LaConte
                                                                                  Direct
                                                                                  Page 12

 1    Q      WHY DO YOU DISAGREE WITH THE EXPECTED EARNINGS METHODOLOGY?

 2    A      The Expected Earnings methodology is not a reliable method to estimate the ROE

 3           because it represents a forecast return on book equity and not a required return or cost

 4           of equity.


 5    Q      WHAT DO YOU MEAN BY THE ESTIMATED EARNINGS METHOD REPRESENTS

 6           A FORECAST RETURN ON EQUITY AND NOT A REQUIRED RETURN OR COST

 7           OF EQUITY?

 8    A      This methodology uses forecasted returns on book equity. This is not a reasonable

 9           proxy for investors’ expected market returns. It is a book accounting return and does

10           not reflect investors’ market expectations. FERC rejected this methodology in 2019,

11           explaining “Because an investor cannot purchase a utility’s common stock at book

12           value and must instead pay the prevailing market price for common equity, the utility’s

13           expected earned return on book value is indicative of neither what an investor can

14           expect to earn on an investment in the utility’s common stock nor what return an

15           investor requires to invest in the utility’s common stock.”12

16                   As such, Mr. Coyne’s Expected Earnings method is not a reliable proxy for the

17           estimated ROE for GPC and should be rejected.


18    Flotation Costs

19    Q      WHAT ARE FLOTATION COSTS?

20    A      Flotation costs include two components. The first component is the actual cost paid by


     12Association of Businesses Advocating Tariff Equity et al. v. Midcontinent Independent System
      Operator, Inc. et al, Docket No. EL 14-12-003 and EL 15-45-000, Opinion No. 569 at 104 (Nov. 21,
      2019).

                                                                       2. Evaluation of Mr. Coyne’s
                                                                                      ROE Analysis
                                               J.POLLOCK
                                               INCORPORATED

                                                                                    Billie S. LaConte
                                                                                    Direct
                                                                                    Page 13

 1             a company to the underwriter for issuing stock. The second is indirect and represents

 2             the potential dilutive impact due to the issuance of new stock.


 3    Q        HOW      DO    FLOTATION        COSTS     AFFECT     THE     RETURN      ON     EQUITY

 4             DETERMINATION?

 5    A        Flotation costs increase the ROE.         For example, Mr. Coyne made an upward

 6             adjustment of eight basis points.13


 7    Q        SHOULD GEORGIA POWER COMPANY’S RETURN ON EQUITY INCLUDE A

 8             FLOTATION COST ADJUSTMENT?

 9    A        No. First, Mr. Coyne’s estimate of flotation costs was based on the companies in his

10             proxy group, not on any actual flotation costs incurred by GPC. This is because GPC

11             is a regulated utility that does not issue stock and, therefore, does not incur flotation

12             costs.   The flotation costs are incurred by GPC’s parent company, the Southern

13             Company. Therefore, a flotation cost adjustment is not necessary.


14    Q        IF GEORGIA POWER COMPANY IS RESPONSIBLE FOR THE SOUTHERN

15             COMPANY’S FLOTATION COSTS, SHOULD THE COMMISSION APPROVE MR.

16             COYNE’S ADJUSTMENT?

17    A        No. As noted above, Mr. Coyne’s adjustment is not based on actual flotation costs

18             incurred by GPC and, therefore, is unnecessary and unwarranted. If the Commission

19             allows GPC to recover flotation costs, it should be based on a reasonable projection of

20             flotation costs that GPC’s parent company will incur during the ARP period.


     13   Direct Testimony of James M. Coyne at 41-42.

                                                                        2. Evaluation of Mr. Coyne’s
                                                                                       ROE Analysis
                                                 J.POLLOCK
                                                 INCORPORATED

                                                                                Billie S. LaConte
                                                                                Direct
                                                                                Page 14

 1   Summary of the Flaws With Mr. Coyne’s ROE Analysis

 2   Q    PLEASE SUMMARIZE YOUR CRITIQUE OF MR. COYNE’S RETURN ON EQUITY

 3        ANALYSES.

 4   A    Mr. Coyne’s recommended ROE of 11% does not recognize GPC’s reduced risk due

 5        to the regulatory mechanisms it has in place which reduce regulatory lag and income

 6        variability, as discussed more fully in my next section. Further, his reasoning for

 7        adjusting GPC’s ROE due to flotation costs is inaccurate and should be rejected.

 8                        Mr. Coyne relies on four methods to estimate an ROE for GPC. His

 9        CAPM analysis includes incorrect estimates of the projected MRP and excludes the

10        historical MRP, which results in overstating the ROE.

11                 The Risk Premium analysis uses a regression analysis that relies solely on the

12        relationship between interest rates and the equity risk premium. It does not include

13        other factors that could impact the risk premium, such as Federal monetary and fiscal

14        policies. Ignoring other factors that affect the equity risk premium produces inaccurate

15        ROE estimates and the results should be rejected.

16                 The Expected Earnings methodology is not a common method used to estimate

17        the cost of equity for a regulated utility. The utility’s expected return on book value is

18        indicative of neither what an investor can expect to earn on an investment in the utility’s

19        common stock, nor what return an investor requires to invest in the utility’s common

20        stock. Therefore, it should be rejected.

21                 Lastly, the flotation cost adjustment is misplaced because GPC does not issue

22        stock.

                                                                                   ROE Analysis
                                            J.POLLOCK
                                            INCORPORATED

                                                                                  Billie S. LaConte
                                                                                  Direct
                                                                                  Page 15

                    3. FACTORS IMPACTING THE RETURN ON EQUITY


 1   Q     WHAT FACTORS SHOULD THE COMMISSION CONSIDER WHEN DETERMINING

 2         AN APPROPRIATE RETURN ON EQUITY FOR GEORGIA POWER COMPANY?

 3   A     First and foremost, a fair and reasonable ROE should be based on results derived from

 4         accepted methodologies, such as the DCF and CAPM methods, and using the Risk

 5         Premium method as a check on the reasonableness of the DCF and CAPM results.

 6         Further, the inputs and assumptions used in applying the accepted methodologies

 7         should consider current financial and economic realities, such as:

 8             •   GPC’s financial and business risk; and
 9             •   The ROEs authorized by state regulatory commissions.

10         I discuss each of these considerations in detail below.


11   Financial Risk
12   Q     WHAT IS FINANCIAL RISK?

13   A     Financial risk represents variability in income. Quantifying financial risk includes an

14         analysis of a utility’s financial statements and cash flow/leverage analyses. To the

15         extent such variability is small, or has been reduced by other means, the risk to GPC’s

16         shareholders is lower.


17   Q     WHAT FACTORS CAN INFLUENCE THE LEVEL OF FINANCIAL RISK FOR A

18         PARTICULAR UTILITY?

19   A     Financial risk is highly influenced by the regulatory climate. All other things being equal,

20         a utility is less risky if it is regulated by a Commission that:


                                                                      3. Factors Impacting the ROE

                                              J.POLLOCK
                                              INCORPORATED

                                                                              Billie S. LaConte
                                                                              Direct
                                                                              Page 16
 1           •   authorizes rates for a multi-year period;
 2           •   authorizes separate piecemeal cost recovery clauses and revenue
 3               decoupling that adjusts rates automatically outside of base rate cases; and
 4           •   employs other constructive ratemaking practices.

 5   Q   WHAT DO YOU MEAN BY PIECEMEAL COST RECOVERY CLAUSES?

 6   A   Piecemeal (or single-issue) ratemaking refers to a utility being allowed to implement

 7       adjustment clauses that permit it to change rates outside of a base rate case, while

 8       ignoring the utility’s earnings. Utilities are able to mitigate risk by implementing an

 9       adjustment clause that allows rates to change on an expedited basis, outside the

10       context of a traditional rate case in which all costs and revenues would be considered.


11   Q   DOES GEORGIA POWER COMPANY CURRENTLY HAVE ANY ADJUSTMENT

12       CLAUSES IN PLACE THAT REDUCE ITS VARIABILITY IN INCOME AND LOWER

13       ITS FINANCIAL RISK?

14   A   Yes. GPC has numerous adjustment clauses in place that reduce its financial risk,

15       such as Fuel Cost Recovery, Environmental Compliance Cost Recovery, Demand Side

16       Management rider, the Nuclear Construction Cost Recovery, and an Interim Fuel Rider.

17       GPC’s cash flow is improved due to its adjustment clauses and positively affects its

18       credit quality, which reduces its financial risk.


19   Q   HOW DOES LOWER RISK IMPACT GEORGIA POWER COMPANY’S EXPECTED

20       COST OF CAPITAL?

21   A   Lower risk leads to lower financial volatility. GPC has several tools available to reduce

22       its regulatory lag, including the aforementioned adjustment clauses. Therefore,

23       investors’ required return for GPC will be lower. Hence, these risk-reducing measures

24       support a reduction to GPC’s requested ROE of 11%.

                                                                3. Factors Impacting the ROE

                                            J.POLLOCK
                                            INCORPORATED

                                                                                     Billie S. LaConte
                                                                                     Direct
                                                                                     Page 17

 1    Q         DOES THE PROPOSED ALTERNATE RATE PLAN AFFECT GEORGIA POWER

 2              COMPANY’S RISK PROFILE?

 3    A         Yes. The proposed ARP is a three-year plan in which rates are based on test-year

 4              ending July 31, 2023, modified for a levelization adjustment. The ARP provides GPC

 5              with rate stability and predictability, reduces regulatory lag by allowing the Company

 6              more-timely recovery of costs, and is considered favorable by the credit rating

 7              agencies.14


 8    Q         HOW WILL THE PROPOSED THREE-YEAR ALTERNATE RATE PLAN AFFECT

 9              GEORGIA POWER COMPANY’S RISK PROFILE?

10    A         The proposed three-year ARP will lower GPC’s risk because it will reduce its business

11              risk (regulatory lag) and its financial risk (revenue volatility). The ARP has lower risk

12              than a traditional ratemaking plan because rates will be adjusted to recover GPC’s

13              levelized revenue deficiency over the three-year period. Under normal ratemaking

14              conditions, GPC would file a rate case every year to increase its rates. Having an ARP

15              lowers the risk faced by GPC because it provides predictable and reliable rates,

16              reduces regulatory lag and also reduces revenue volatility.


17    ROEs Authorized By State Regulatory Commissions

18    Q         WHAT RETURNS ON EQUITY HAVE STATE REGULATORY COMMISSIONS

19              RECENTLY AUTHORIZED FOR REGULATED ELECTRIC INVESTOR-OWNED

20              UTILITIES?

21    A         From roughly 2010 to 2021, average authorized ROEs ranged from 9.53% to 10.42%.

 1   Q   SHOULD THE COMMISSION CONSIDER DETERMINATIONS MADE IN OTHER

 4       regulatory commissions. This is because the trend in authorized ROEs indicates that,

 5       in general, utilities’ financial and credit risks are lower than in the past due, in part, to

 6       the implementation of a plethora of cost recovery mechanisms and other

 8       is an important consideration when determining the ROE for GPC, however, as

                                   4. CAPITAL STRUCTURE

 3       excessive equity ratio.


 4   Q   WHAT IS THE BASIS FOR YOUR STATEMENT THAT GEORGIA POWER

 5       COMPANY’S PROPOSED EQUITY RATIO IS EXCESSIVE?

 6   A   GPC’s proposed equity ratio is excessive because it results in unnecessarily higher

 7       costs to ratepayers. Table 2 summarizes the average equity ratio of each vertically

 8       integrated electric investor-owned utility in the most recent rate cases decided during

 9       the period 2016 through 2022 to date.

 4    Q         HOW DOES GEORGIA POWER COMPANY’S PROPOSED COMMON EQUITY

 5              RATIO COMPARE TO THE AVERAGE COMMON EQUITY RATIO FOR THE

 6              COMPANIES IN THE PROXY GROUP?

 7    A         The average common equity ratio for the companies in Mr. Coyne’s proxy group is

 8              52.44%.16 The average common equity ratio includes companies with credit ratings

 9              that are lower than GPC’s (A-). If we remove companies with a lower credit, the

10              average common equity ratio is 53%, which is significantly below GPC’s proposed 56%

11              equity ratio. Exhibit BSL-4 provides each proxy group company’s credit rating and

12              common equity ratio. A company’s common equity ratio contributes to its credit score,

13              therefore a company with a credit rating that is similar to GPC’s should have a common

14              equity ratio that is supportive of its rating and a good proxy for GPC’s common equity

15              ratio. As demonstrated in Exhibit BSL-4, GPC’s proposed common equity ratio is

16              significantly above the average common equity ratio of its peers and should be rejected.

                                                                                  4. Capital Structure

                                                  J.POLLOCK
                                                 INCORPORATED

                                                                             Billie S. LaConte
                                                                             Direct
                                                                             Page 22

 1   Q   ARE THERE ANY CONSEQUENCES OF USING MORE EQUITY AND LESS DEBT

 2       TO FINANCE THE UTILITY’S RATE BASE?

 3   A   Yes. GPC’s higher percentage of equity and lower percentage of debt in its capital

 4       structure lowers its financial risk. Furthermore, common equity is more expensive than

 5       debt. In this case, GPC is proposing a 56% common equity ratio, but the proposed

 6       cost of debt is 3.53%, which is 747 basis points lower than the proposed 11% return

 7       on equity. A utility with too much equity in its capital structure has a higher cost of

 8       capital than a utility with a more balanced common equity ratio. All else being equal,

 9       the higher the overall common equity ratio, the greater the benefits to GPC’s

10       shareholders and executives and the higher the rates all GPC retail customers will bear.

11       GPC should not be rewarded for its overly conservative use of debt and high equity

12       ratio.


13   Q   WHAT IS THE IMPACT ON GEORGIA POWER COMPANY’S REVENUE

14       REQUIREMENT IF ITS COMMON EQUITY RATIO IS REDUCED TO THE AVERAGE

15       COMMON EQUITY RATIO OF THE COMPANIES IN THE PROXY GROUP WITH THE

16       SAME CREDIT RATING?

17   A   If GPC’s financial common equity ratio is reduced to 53%, its revenue requirement

18       would be $108 million lower. The detailed calculations are shown in Exhibit BSL-5.


19   Q   WHAT IS THE IMPACT ON GEORGIA POWER COMPANY’S REVENUE

20       REQUIREMENT IF ITS RETURN ON EQUITY IS CORRECTED AND ITS COMMON

21       EQUITY RATIO IS REDUCED TO YOUR CORRECTED AVERAGE COMMON

22       EQUITY RATIO?

23   A   If GPC’s ROE is reduced to my corrected ROE of 9.83% and its common equity ratio

                                              APPENDIX C
                                 Return on Equity Methodologies

     Single Stage Discounted Cash Flow

 1         The discounted cash flow model is used by investors to determine the present value

 2         of a stock, based on future cash flows (dividends), which are discounted by the stock’s

 3         known return and its forecast growth.

 4         The formula is:

                                                        D
 5                                             𝑃 =
                                                     r−g

 6         Where:
 7                  P = current stock price
 8                  D = dividend yield
 9                  r = rate of return
10                  g = growth rate

11         We can re-arrange the formula thus:

                                                        D
12                                                 𝑟=     +𝑔
                                                        P

13         In other words, the expected return equals (1) the current dividend rate, plus (2) the

14         expected growth in dividends. The expected growth in dividends is also measured by

15         the expected growth in earnings.

16                  The stock prices are based on the average closing prices for the past 30 days.

17         The 30-day average is used to ensure that the results reflect stock prices over a period

18         of time that is not overly reliant on any particular events affecting stock prices on a

19         given day and that represent capital market conditions over the past month.


                                                                                      Appendix C
                                              J.POLLOCK
                                              INCORPORATED

                                                                            Billie S. LaConte
                                                                            Direct
                                                                            Page 34

 1           The growth rates are the forecast earnings per share growth rates for the next

 2   five years from Value Line, Yahoo! Finance, and Zacks. The dividends are forecast

 3   figures, also from Value Line. These have been adjusted to reflect any quarterly

 4   adjustments during the year.

 5   Multi-Stage Discounted Cash Flow Methodology

 6   A multi-stage DCF analysis uses three separate growth estimates or stages. The first

 7   stage measures the near-term growth rate based on the analysts’ forecast earnings

 8   growth used in my constant growth DCF analysis. The second stage (intermediate-

 9   term) growth rates are linear interpolations of the first and third stage growth rates.

10   The third stage (long term) is the forecast of the long-term growth rate of gross

11   domestic product (GDP).      Using these inputs, the model calculates the required

12   internal rate of return to meet these dividend growth rates, or the ROE. I calculated

13   three multi-stage ROE analyses using a low, mean, and high first year growth rate that

14   is based on the analysts’ forecast estimates of growth, similar to the constant growth

15   DCF analysis.

16          The multi-stage method is used because analysts’ growth rates for the first

17   stage may not be sustainable over the long term. The multi-stage model recognizes

18   short-term growth (whether it be higher or lower than the long term), but also accounts

19   for a more realistic, long-term growth rate. Analysts’ growth rates should be viewed in

20   conjunction with other growth estimates to achieve a reasonable forecast of expected

21   earnings.

22          The long-term growth in GDP is used because the underlying assumption is

23   that mature, established companies can grow at a rate that is similar to, or lower than,

24   the GDP growth rate. While some companies in the economy will grow faster than

25   GDP for a while, this cannot happen consistently over a long period.

                                                                                Appendix C
                                      J.POLLOCK
                                      INCORPORATED

                                                                            Billie S. LaConte
                                                                            Direct
                                                                            Page 35
 1   Capital Asset Pricing Model

 2   The CAPM is a Risk Premium method that is used to estimate the ROE. It states that

 3   the expected return of a security equals the risk-free rate plus a risk premium. Simply

 4   put, investors require a premium over the risk-free rate if they are going to invest in a

 5   riskier security. The formula for the CAPM is:

 6          Expected ROE = Risk-Free Rate + β*Market Risk Premium

 7   The equity risk premium for a particular stock is the MRP times the stock’s beta (β).

 8   The MRP is the difference between the return on the market on average (i.e., the S&P

 9   500) and the risk-free rate. Thus, it is the premium that reflects the risk on an average

10   stock. Beta is the price volatility of that stock relative to the market as a whole. Thus,

11   the risk premium for a specific stock equals the average MRP times the beta. Since

12   utility stocks are lower risk than the average stock, the risk premium for a utility stock

13   is lower than the average MRP.        Multiplying the beta times the MRP gives the

14   appropriate risk premium for the company (or group of comparable companies) being

15   studied.

16          The risk-free rate is the projected yield on 30-year U.S. Treasury bonds. This

17   rate is considered to be risk-free because the return is guaranteed by the U.S.

18   government.

19          Two MRP estimates may be used, including the historical MRP estimate and a

20   projected MRP. The historical MRP is based on historical data dating back to 1996.

21   The projected MRP is based on the projected median three-to-five year price

22   appreciation of the 1,700 stocks from Value Line and the projected median dividend

23   yield over the next 12 months for all dividend paying stocks. The forecast annual return

24   is based on the forecast annual growth rate of the stocks plus the forecast median

25   dividend produces a projected annual return. The projected risk-free rate is deducted

26   from the projected annual return to determine the projected MRP.
                                                                                 Appendix C
                                      J.POLLOCK
                                      INCORPORATED

                                                                                         Billie S. LaConte
                                                                                         Direct
                                                                                         Page 36

 1                      Beta measures the volatility of a security in comparison to the market as a

 2              whole. A beta equal to 1.00 means that a stock’s price fluctuates exactly as the market

 3              as a whole. A beta higher than 1.00 implies that the stock’s price is more volatile than

 4              the market; a beta less than 1.00 implies the stock’s price is less volatile than the

 5              market. The standard formula for estimating beta is the covariance between a security’s

 6              return and the return of the market divided by the variance of the market returns over

 7              a specified period. Beta is based on the betas provided in Value Line.         Beta

 8              measures the amount of compensation an investor needs to take on additional risk, as

 9              compared to the risk-free rate. Value Line’s method to estimate beta is based on “a

10              regression analysis of the relationship between weekly percentage changes in the price

11              of a stock and weekly percentage changes in the NYSE Composite Index over a period

12              of five years. Value Line then adjusts these Betas to account for their long-term

13              tendency to converge toward 1.00.”17

14                      The risk-free rate is based on the projected return on long-term treasuries. This

15              rate is considered risk-free because the security is backed by the U.S. government.

16              Risk Premium Method

17              The Risk Premium method estimates the ROE for a utility as the sum of a bond yield

18              plus a risk premium yield. The bond yield is the projected return on the long-term

19              government bond plus the historical risk premium. The risk premium is a measure of

20              the additional return an investor requires due to the additional risk of the security. The

21              risk premium is the measure of the difference between the historical authorized return

22              on equity regulated electric, natural gas or water utilities and the historical yield on 30-

23              year Treasury bonds.


     17   Value Line Publishing, Using Beta, Andrew J. Cueter, (Oct. 2, 2012).

                                                                                              Appendix C
                                                   J.POLLOCK
                                                   INCORPORATED

                                                                                                                     Exhibit BSL-1

of 2022 versus 55 in full-year 2021.
