===== Direct Testimony of Roger Tutterow.docx =====
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF
ROGER TUTTEROW, PH.D.
ON BEHALF OF 
GEORGIA ASSOCIATION OF MANUFACTURERS
DOCKET NO. 44280


Q. PLEASE STATE YOUR NAME, TITLE AND BUSINESS ADDRESS.

A. My name is Roger Tutterow.  I am Professor of Economics at Kennesaw State University, 560 Parliament Garden Way, Kennesaw, GA 30144.

Q. PLEASE SUMMARIZE YOUR EDUCATIONAL AND PROFESSIONAL BACKGROUND.

A. I received a Bachelor of Science in Decision Sciences from Berry College, a Master of Arts in Economics and a Doctor of Philosophy in Economics, both from Georgia State University. Subsequent to my graduation, I taught at West Virginia University in Morgantown, West Virginia before accepting a position at Kennesaw State University. At Kennesaw State University, I served as a tenured professor in the Department of Economics & Finance. From 1995 to 2005, I served as Chair of that department. In 2005, I moved to Mercer University where I served as Dean of the Stetson School of Business during 2005 and 2006 and as a tenured Professor of Economics from through 2014. In 2014, I returned to Kennesaw State University as a tenured Professor of Economics. I also serve as Director of the Econometric Center and hold the Henssler Financial Endowed Chair. I have taught a variety of courses in economic theory, finance theory and quantitative analysis at the undergraduate and graduate level. In addition, I conduct research, speak to professional groups and serve as a consultant to businesses. I also have provided expert testimony on economics, financial and statistical matters in state and federal court, before the Georgia General Assembly and before this Commission in the 2004, 2007, 2010, 2013 and 2019 Georgia Power Company Rate Cases.

Q.IN ADDITION TO THESE QUALIFICATIONS, HAVE YOU SERVED IN ANY OTHER CAPACITIES THAT WOULD GIVE YOU SPECIAL EXPERTISE AND INSIGHT WITH REGARD TO THE GEORGIA ECONOMY?

A.Yes.  From 2003 until 2011, I served on the Council of Economic Advisors to Governor Perdue.  From 2011 through 2019, I served in that capacity for Governor Deal.  I currently serve on the Council for Governor Kemp. In 2010, I was named to serve on the Special Council on Tax Reform and Fairness for Georgians, which was charged with conducting a comprehensive review of Georgia’s tax code and recommending changes to the General Assembly. 

Q. WHAT ARE THE PURPOSES OF YOUR TESTIMONY? 

A.The purposes of my testimony are:
(1)To describe the importance of manufacturing to the Georgia economy;
(2)To address the impact of energy costs on manufacturing and outline the regional and global competitive pressures that impact manufacturing and distinguish manufacturing from other classes of customers served by Georgia Power Company (“Georgia Power”);
(3)To discuss the economic changes that have occurred since my last testimony before the Commission;
(4)Relative to this rate case, to emphasize the importance of a Commission decision that recognizes the importance of manufacturing to the Georgia economy and policies that encourage continued growth and investment by manufacturers in the state; and 
(5)To support the retention of any existing rate design or other mechanisms that promote the goal of a healthy manufacturing base in Georgia, as well as the adoption of positions and proposals that will further that goal in light of the harshly competitive environment posed by surrounding states and other countries as locations for potential manufacturing investment.

I.THE IMPORTANCE OF MANUFACTURING TO GEORGIA

Q.WHY DOES MANUFACTURING MATTER TO GEORGIA?

A.As I have argued in the past, it remains critical to recognize the importance of manufacturing when considering matters of public policy.  Manufacturing matters to Georgia for several reasons: (1) it creates wealth for local communities; (2) it remains a significant contributor to Georgia employment; (3) it pays premium compensation to its employees; (4) it plays a disproportionate and important role in the economies of Georgia’s smaller communities and local areas; and (5) it produces the largest positive multiplier or “spillover” effects that support related industries and other sectors of the economy.

Q.PLEASE DESCRIBE WHAT YOU MEAN BY CREATING WEALTH FOR LOCAL COMMUNITIES.

A.When assessing the impact of a business or sector on a community, it is crucial to recognize the role of that business in “wealth creation”; that is, the degree to which that business or sector creates wealth in the community rather than redistributing wealth that is already present. For this reason, economic developers have long recognized the export-based model of economic growth.  Under the export-based growth model, the ability of a region to grow is linked to the presence of industries that export their products outside that region. In its crudest form, one could argue that, in the absence of the ability to export products outside a region, the inhabitants are selling goods and services to each other and thus only redistributing the collective wealth of the community.

Q.AREN’T THERE SOME SERVICES THAT ARE EXPORTED OUT OF STATE?

A.Yes. Major urban areas that are hubs for professional services do, in fact, export those services to some customers outside the region. One example would be a law firm that serves clients from other states.  Likewise, an information technology firm that sells software to businesses in other communities, states or countries also “creates wealth.”  However, manufactured products are distinctive because of the degree to which they are sold outside their region, or even country, of production. For example, at the national level, goods comprised 69% of U.S. exports in 2021. Over 52% of these exported goods were manufactured capital and consumer goods. The remainder, which include agricultural products and industrial supplies, are products that also include a manufacturing component.

Q.HOW MUCH DOES MANUFACTURING CONTRIBUTE TO GEORGIA EMPLOYMENT?

A.Manufacturing employment remains significant in Georgia.  As of August 2022, the manufacturing sector provided roughly 412,900 jobs in Georgia – or about 8.6% of all non-agricultural employment.  In terms of output, the role of manufacturing in the Georgia economy is even more pronounced.  For 2021, the manufacturing sector accounted for $64.9 billion, or 9.4%, of Georgia’s Gross Domestic Product. Further, if one factors out the public sector, then manufacturing accounted for 10.6% of the private industry component of Georgia’s GDP – or roughly $1 in every $9.47 of Georgia’s private economic output.   

Q.HOW DOES GEORGIA’S MANUFACTURING OUTPUT COMPARE WITH OTHER STATES?

A.Georgia is the 11th largest state in the nation in terms of the value of its manufacturing output.  By way of comparison, the value of manufacturing’s output in Georgia is larger than the manufacturing output of the smallest 33 states combined. 


Q.WHAT HAS BEEN THE EXPERIENCE OF GEORGIA’S MANUFACTURING BASE OVER THE LAST 15 YEARS?

A.Manufacturing’s share of nonagricultural employment in Georgia stands at 8.6%.  Georgia lost a significant number of manufacturing jobs during the “Great Recession” of 2008-09, but this subsequently stabilized at roughly the same level from 2010 to 2019.  Manufacturing’s share of employment fell again, modestly, during the COVID-19 pandemic, but again has been restored to its prior levels. This recent shift to manufacturing expanding more in line with broad employment is likely, to some degree, the effect of shifts in state policy including the tax treatment of energy and the recognition of manufacturing’s importance in decisions by this Commission.  It is important that the Commission continue this recognition and not make decisions that would impair this stabilization or to discourage future investment in manufacturing. 


Q.TO WHAT EXTENT DOES MANUFACTURING PAY PREMIUM COMPENSATION TO ITS EMPLOYEES?

A.Manufacturing is distinguished from many other economic sectors because of the higher-than-average compensation it provides. For example, during 2021, average weekly wages in manufacturing were approximately 1.9% higher than the statewide average for non-manufacturing employment, but importantly, outside of the Atlanta Metropolitan Statistical Area (“MSA”), manufacturing wages exceed non-manufacturing wages by 16.6%.  Thus, the premium of compensation that is paid by manufacturers is even greater – and significantly so – in the less urban regions of the state. In many rural communities, a local manufacturer is both the largest and best compensating employer.  

However, even comparing average weekly wage data understates the premium in total compensation paid by manufacturing firms because it does not capture the value of employer–provided fringe benefit packages. At the national level, employers’ actual contributions to discretionary fringe benefit programs were 27.4% higher in goods-producing firms than for service-producing firms. 

Q.CAN YOU DISCUSS THE DISPROPORTIONATE IMPACT THAT MANUFACTURING HAS ON GEORGIA’S MORE RURAL COMMUNITIES?

A.Manufacturing plays a disproportionately large role in the rural economies of Georgia.  In August 2022, manufacturing represented 5.9% of non-agricultural employment in the Atlanta MSA and 12.9% of employment outside of the Atlanta MSA.  In other words, manufacturing employment comprises twice as large a percentage of employment outside the 28-county Atlanta MSA as it does inside the Atlanta MSA. 
Q.WHAT DO YOU MEAN BY THE “MULTIPLIER EFFECT” AND WHY IS IT CRITICAL FOR UNDERSTANDING THE IMPORTANCE OF MANUFACTURING?

A.When evaluating the importance of various sectors of the economy, it is important to recognize the broad impacts of spending in one sector upon activity in other sectors. The indirect and induced spending associated with manufacturing creates a “multiplier effect” of the employment directly associated with manufacturing.

Q.CAN YOU DESCRIBE THE INDIRECT AND INDUCED EFFECTS?

A.Sure.  When a manufacturing firm purchases products, services or raw materials from local suppliers, employment by the supplier enterprises is enhanced. This is known as the indirect effect.  Further, when the employees of a manufacturer or its suppliers spend their wages in their community, that spending also supports employment at a variety of businesses. This is known as the induced effect. 

The size of the “multiplier effect” in manufacturing is confirmed by data from the U.S. Department of Commerce. Based on its 2021 Input-Output Accounts Data and 2020 Regional Data, the manufacturing sector has a total requirements multiplier of 2.31. In other words, every dollar of final demand for manufactured products generates an additional $1.31 in demand for products that are required to produce the original manufactured product. 

Of the fifteen sectors in the Department of Commerce classification, the manufacturing sector had the highest multiplier effect. 

Of course, the multiplier effects of manufacturing spill over broadly into most industries in the economy. Of the $1.31 in additional product demand associated with the multiplier for manufactured products, over $0.74 of that demand is for products outside the manufacturing sectors. The professional and business services, mining, agriculture and wholesale trade sectors benefit the most from manufacturing’s multiplier effect.  For example, for each dollar of final demand for manufacturing products, there is an associated demand of over $0.14 in demand for professional and business services and over $0.13 of activity in wholesale trade. Finally, these multipliers reflect linkages between the final demand for manufactured products and the associated demand for products from other industries. However, they do not capture the effect of the induced spending of employees at the manufacturing and associated businesses in their communities. Given the competitive compensation paid by the manufacturing sector, that induced spending is likely quite significant. 

II.THE IMPACT OF ENERGY COSTS ON MANUFACTURERS

Q.DO INCREASES IN ENERGY COSTS IMPACT THE ABILITY OF MANUFACTURERS TO CONTINUE PRODUCTION AND COMPETE EFFECTIVELY? 

A. Yes. Higher electricity rates raise the cost to businesses of producing goods and services. Funds available to finance capital investment are lowered as well.  For industrial customers, electricity is not consumed as a final product itself, but rather, is an input into the process through which they produce goods. To use the syntax of microeconomics, energy is a “factor of production” rather than consumption good. As with other factors of production, higher energy rates will increase the cost to industrial customers of producing goods.  This increase in cost likely will lead to a reduction in competitiveness, lower production and employment or both, depending on the ability of the business to pass on the higher production cost.  In a competitive global marketplace, manufacturers find it difficult to pass along increased costs of production such as the cost of electricity.

Q.WHY CAN’T MANUFACTURERS SIMPLY PASS ALONG THESE COSTS IN THE PRICE OF THE PRODUCTS THEY SELL?

A.In the current competitive environment for manufacturers, it is not easy or even possible to do in many cases.  Global competition, especially from low production cost areas of the world, serves as a strong constraint on the ability of manufacturers to pass along cost increases to customers. Customers are more likely to press manufacturers for lower prices than to pay more for the products they produce.  Manufacturers, then, are constantly challenged to lower their costs of products to compensate for higher raw material, labor, and energy costs.  There are practical limits, including regulatory restrictions, on their ability to do so.  When manufacturers cannot meet these pressures, they generally lose customers to a lower cost producer, in many cases from another region or country.

Q.CAN HIGHER ENERGY COSTS IMPACT A MANUFACTURER’S DECISION AS TO WHERE TO LOCATE PRODUCTION?

A.Yes.  Facing the pressures of surviving in the current environment, manufacturers need to maintain a competitive cost structure in order to succeed.  If the costs of production in a particular region, or state, become less competitive than readily available alternatives, a manufacturer might need to relocate production to lower cost states or countries.  Energy costs certainly can be a driving factor in this regard.

Further, if a manufacturer with multiple production facilities decides to expand production, there will be a clear preference for expanding activity in the facilities with the lower production cost. Those locations within the United States with lower production cost are the more likely beneficiaries of any shift in location. 

Q.HOW WOULD YOU DESCRIBE THE REGIONAL ENVIRONMENT IN TERMS OF COMPETITION FOR MANUFACTURING?

A.There is significant competition for manufacturing investment and jobs from surrounding states. Many industrial customers have production facilities in other states in the region. Since labor flows easily between states, labor differentials will be smaller and energy costs are even more likely to be a factor in domestic location decisions.

With regard to local competition, what matters are the rates charged by electric utilities in similarly situated locations. In the case of Georgia, the most natural comparables would be the rates charged in other Southeastern states including Alabama, North Carolina, South Carolina and Tennessee.  To the extent the cost of electricity in Georgia is greater than in neighboring states, it acts as a disincentive to invest and create jobs in the state.

The importance of energy costs for regional locations decisions has long been recognized by economic developers. While incentives are sometimes employed to attract new enterprises, they are less likely to be used to retain existing enterprises. Thus, recognizing the role of base energy prices in the competitive position of existing enterprises is important for retaining, and encouraging the expansion of, existing industry.  In a very real sense, Georgia competes with its closest neighboring states to retain and expand industry and manufacturing and the myriad of benefits they bring to the economy and well-being of all Georgia citizens. 

That energy costs affect location decisions for goods producing industries was recognized by the Special Council on Tax Reform and Fairness for Georgians on which I served in 2010. The council acknowledged its importance, and the uniqueness of the challenge faced by manufacturing and agricultural businesses, in recommending that energy used in manufacturing be exempted from sales taxes. While that recommendation was translated into policy, the removal of the sales tax from energy only reduced an existing competitive disadvantage faced by Georgia producers. 

Q.ARE INDUSTRIAL AND OTHER CUSTOMER CLASSES EQUALLY IMPACTED BY HIGHER ELECTRICITY RATES?

A.No. In general, while higher electricity rates will increase the cost of production for industrial customers or other customer classes, the magnitude of that effect will vary across sectors of the economy and across businesses within each sector.  For reasons upon which I will further elaborate, the impact of higher electric rates on manufacturers is more adverse than other customer classes.

Q. WHAT FACTORS DETERMINE THE MAGNITUDE OF THE EFFECT OF HIGHER ELECTRICITY RATES ON BUSINESSES?

A. Higher electricity rates will impact all businesses primarily through changing their cost structure. The magnitude of the effect of an increase in electricity rates on the cost structure is determined by the proportion of the business’s operating cost that is represented by the cost of electricity as well as their ability to substitute among energy sources. For most industrial customers, energy and labor are generally recognized as the two largest costs for conversion of raw materials into final products.  For some industrial customers, energy is the largest cost of conversion. 

Equally important is the effect of a rate increase on the competitive environment in which the customers operate. Specifically, if an increase in electricity rates leads to a comparable increase in production cost for all competitors in a market area, then the effect on the competitive position of a single business is mitigated. If a customer’s competitors are in areas not subject to the rate increase, then an increase in costs associated with a rate increase will diminish the customer’s competitive position. These competitors can be global or regional.

Q.PLEASE ELABORATE ON THE ISSUE OF REGIONAL COMPETITION.

A.Manufacturers generally will invest capital in locations where they can achieve the lowest costs of production.  This is particularly the case for manufacturers that operate in several states within a region.  As noted above, a manufacturer’s decision as to where to place, consolidate, or curtail production can be significantly influenced by the cost of energy.

Q.HOW DO INDUSTRIAL AND OTHER CUSTOMERS COMPARE IN THIS REGARD? 

A.One of the challenges facing industrial customers, and one which differentiates their class from other classes of customers, is that they face competition regionally, nationally and globally rather than just locally. Thus, many of the industrial customers’ competitors will not be subject to the same increase in electricity rates and resultant increase in production costs.  As such, when an industrial customer receives a rate increase, it will hurt their competitive position versus its competitors who are operating outside the regulated area. 

It is understood that no business would welcome a rate increase. However, a rate increase generally has less effect on electricity customer who compete only locally because their competitors are likely to be subject to the same increase.

Many industrial businesses have broad geographic markets and multiple production facilities.  These production facilities within a company compete among themselves in the sense of having production levels varied in response to cost considerations, including the cost of electricity.  They also compete for new investment on the basis of production costs.

Thus, an increase in electricity rates would likely have a greater impact on production, facility location and investment for expansion decisions of industrial class customers than for business that compete only locally.  Thus, the outcome of these decisions impact job retention and job creation.

Further, the importance of initially selecting or remaining in a specific geographic location, and hence being subject to a specific utility rate structure, is not the same for all customer classes. Specifically, for businesses who compete only locally, close proximity to their customers and the cost of real estate drive location decisions more than the cost of electricity.

For example, for these businesses, being located in close geographic proximity to their customers is the crucial consideration. Since customers tend to patronize commercial businesses that are close to their residence or employment, the business location is a determinant of the relevant customer market. For manufacturers, however, the decision as to where to locate or expand a production facility is most likely based on the cost of production, of which energy cost is a substantial component. 

Businesses that depend on geographic proximity to customers will be less likely to reduce or cease operations in response to an increase in their electricity rates.  As has been evident by the regional and international migration of manufacturing firms in past decades, the manufacturing sector has demonstrated the ability to relocate in response to changes in the cost of production.

III.THE ECONOMIC ENVIRONMENT SINCE 2019

Q.DR. TUTTEROW, YOU LAST TESTIFIED BEFORE THIS COMMISSION IN 2019, CORRECT?

A.That is correct.  I testified in the last Georgia Power rate case in November 2019. 

Q.AND YOUR TESTIMONY AT THAT TIME, LIKE NOW, SUPPORTED THE IMPORTANCE OF MANUFACTURING AND DEMONSTRATED THE UNIQUE CHARACTERISTICS OF THE MANUFACTURING CLASS OF CUSTOMERS?

A.Yes.

Q.HAVE THERE BEEN ANY ECONOMIC DEVELOPMENTS SINCE 2019 THAT YOU BELIEVE TO BE RELEVANT TO THE COMMISSION’S CONSIDERATION IN THIS CASE?

A.Yes. When I testified in 2019, the national economy had been expanding for over ten years, the longest economic expansion since the end of the second World War. However, there were some indications that the pace of economic growth was slowing. Indeed, increases in the value of the U.S. dollar against the currencies of major trading partners was adversely affecting the trade sector. The strong dollar was making U.S. exports more expensive on foreign markets. That effect is most pronounced in the manufacturing sector.

In early 2020, climate was disrupted by the Covid-19 pandemic. Both mandatory and voluntary changes in corporate and household behaviors sent the economy into a brief, but steep, recession. As measured by Gross Domestic Product, economic output fell at an 18.2% annual rate in the first half of 2020. For the two months of March and April 2020, industrial production fell by 16.5% while non-farm employment dropped by 14.4%.

The economy began recovering in May 2020. Output, as measured by gross domestic product, surpassed its pre-pandemic peak during 2nd Quarter of 2021. Industrial production surpassed its pre-pandemic high in November 2021. Non-farm payroll finally surpassed its pre-recession high in August 2022. 

While surpassing pre-pandemic peaks is certainly commendable, it is also worth noting that economic activity has been slowing in recent quarters. Industrial production has decreased modestly in three of the past five quarters. Further, the Institute of Supply Management’s monthly PMI (Purchasing Manager’s Index) index has fallen to 50.9 as of September 2022. This index was above 60 for most of 2021. As 50 is the threshold between expansion and contraction in the manufacturing sector, the PMI is signaling that the manufacturing sector is coming under pressure of late. 

Finally, inflation pressures are pervasive through the economy. While it is common to think of inflation in terms of the prices that consumers pay, it is also manifest as an increase in the prices of inputs used in producing goods. When these higher input prices are combined with upward pressure on wages, it is clear that many businesses will face challenges maintaining profit margins. Of course, in the case of manufacturing, the cost of energy is also an important cost of producing final products.

Q.  ARE THERE REASONS TO BE OPTIMISTIC ABOUT THE STATE’S FUTURE?

A.Yes, looking forward, I am optimistic about the Georgia economy and believe manufacturing can play a key role in its future performance. Competitive energy rates will play an important role in where that production locates.  Georgia also remains committed to recruiting new corporate residents and retaining those already here. Energy cost will be important for those decisions as well. Further, infrastructure investments, including enhancements to the transportation system, such as the deepening of the port in Savannah, should also support further growth.  

IV.THE ROLE OF THE COMMISSION

Q.WHAT ROLE DOES THE COMMISSION PLAY IN TERMS OF ENHANCING GEORGIA AS A COMPETITIVE STATE FOR MANUFACTURING INVESTMENT?

A.The Commission plays a very important role.  The rates and terms under which manufacturers receive electric service are regulated by this Commission.  The financial impacts of this Commission’s decision are clear.  As described in other parts of my testimony, the rates and terms of service for energy service have a direct impact on the competitiveness of manufacturers and the economic decisions they make.


Q.HOW IMPORTANT IS THE COMMISSION’S DECISION IN THIS CASE FROM THE PERSPECTIVE OF INDUSTRIAL CUSTOMERS?

A.It is very important.  I know that GAM appreciates the pro-business decisions this Commission has made in the past.  To the degree that they support employment growth, the decisions benefit all Georgians, not only business entities. I encourage the Commission to adopt an order that is consistent with the policies of growth and enhancing manufacturing in the state of Georgia.  We remain in challenging economic times and many manufacturers are facing uncertain futures.  In my judgment, this is an important window in which to make decisions that will aggressively improve the competitive position of manufacturers from an energy cost perspective.  That is a role that this Commission can play in making Georgia a competitive state for manufacturing investment.

QDOES THIS CONCLUDE YOUR TESTIMONY?

A.Yes.

 8   I.   THE IMPORTANCE OF MANUFACTURING TO GEORGIA


 9   Q.   WHY DOES MANUFACTURING MATTER TO GEORGIA?


10   A.   As I have argued in the past, it remains critical to recognize the importance of
11        manufacturing when considering matters of public policy. Manufacturing matters
12        to Georgia for several reasons: (1) it creates wealth for local communities; (2) it
13        remains a significant contributor to Georgia employment; (3) it pays premium
14        compensation to its employees; (4) it plays a disproportionate and important role in
15        the economies of Georgia’s smaller communities and local areas; and (5) it
16        produces the largest positive multiplier or “spillover” effects that support related
17        industries and other sectors of the economy.


18   Q.   PLEASE DESCRIBE WHAT YOU MEAN BY CREATING WEALTH FOR
19        LOCAL COMMUNITIES.


20   A.   When assessing the impact of a business or sector on a community, it is crucial to
21        recognize the role of that business in “wealth creation”; that is, the degree to which
22        that business or sector creates wealth in the community rather than redistributing
23        wealth that is already present. For this reason, economic developers have long
24        recognized the export-based model of economic growth. Under the export-based
     _______________________________________________________________________
                       Direct Testimony of Roger Tutterow, Ph.D.
                    On Behalf of Georgia Association of Manufacturers
                               and Georgia Industrial Group
                                    Docket No. 44280
                                      Page 3 of 17

 1        growth model, the ability of a region to grow is linked to the presence of industries
 2        that export their products outside that region. In its crudest form, one could argue
 3        that, in the absence of the ability to export products outside a region, the inhabitants
 4        are selling goods and services to each other and thus only redistributing the
 5        collective wealth of the community.


 6   Q.   AREN’T THERE SOME SERVICES THAT ARE EXPORTED OUT OF
 7        STATE?


 8   A.   Yes. Major urban areas that are hubs for professional services do, in fact, export
 9        those services to some customers outside the region. One example would be a law
10        firm that serves clients from other states. Likewise, an information technology firm
11        that sells software to businesses in other communities, states or countries also
12        “creates wealth.” However, manufactured products are distinctive because of the
13        degree to which they are sold outside their region, or even country, of production.
14        For example, at the national level, goods comprised 69% of U.S. exports in 2021.
15        Over 52% of these exported goods were manufactured capital and consumer goods.
16        The remainder, which include agricultural products and industrial supplies, are
17        products that also include a manufacturing component.


18   Q.   HOW MUCH DOES MANUFACTURING CONTRIBUTE TO GEORGIA
19        EMPLOYMENT?


20   A.   Manufacturing employment remains significant in Georgia. As of August 2022,
21        the manufacturing sector provided roughly 412,900 jobs in Georgia – or about 8.6%
22        of all non-agricultural employment. In terms of output, the role of manufacturing
23        in the Georgia economy is even more pronounced. For 2021, the manufacturing
24        sector accounted for $64.9 billion, or 9.4%, of Georgia’s Gross Domestic Product.
25        Further, if one factors out the public sector, then manufacturing accounted for
     _______________________________________________________________________
                       Direct Testimony of Roger Tutterow, Ph.D.
                    On Behalf of Georgia Association of Manufacturers
                               and Georgia Industrial Group
                                    Docket No. 44280
                                      Page 4 of 17

 1        10.6% of the private industry component of Georgia’s GDP – or roughly $1 in every
 2        $9.47 of Georgia’s private economic output.


 3   Q.   HOW DOES GEORGIA’S MANUFACTURING OUTPUT COMPARE
 4        WITH OTHER STATES?


 5   A.   Georgia is the 11th largest state in the nation in terms of the value of its
 6        manufacturing output. By way of comparison, the value of manufacturing’s output
 7        in Georgia is larger than the manufacturing output of the smallest 33 states
 8        combined.


 9   Q.   WHAT        HAS       BEEN       THE       EXPERIENCE           OF      GEORGIA’S
10        MANUFACTURING BASE OVER THE LAST 15 YEARS?


11   A.   Manufacturing’s share of nonagricultural employment in Georgia stands at
12        8.6%. Georgia lost a significant number of manufacturing jobs during the “Great
13        Recession” of 2008-09, but this subsequently stabilized at roughly the same level
14        from 2010 to 2019. Manufacturing’s share of employment fell again, modestly,
15        during the COVID-19 pandemic, but again has been restored to its prior levels. This
16        recent shift to manufacturing expanding more in line with broad employment is
17        likely, to some degree, the effect of shifts in state policy including the tax treatment
18        of energy and the recognition of manufacturing’s importance in decisions by this
19        Commission. It is important that the Commission continue this recognition and not
20        make decisions that would impair this stabilization or to discourage future
21        investment in manufacturing.

 1   Q.   TO WHAT EXTENT DOES MANUFACTURING PAY PREMIUM
 2        COMPENSATION TO ITS EMPLOYEES?


 3   A.   Manufacturing is distinguished from many other economic sectors because of the
 4        higher-than-average compensation it provides. For example, during 2021, average
 5        weekly wages in manufacturing were approximately 1.9% higher than the statewide
 6        average for non-manufacturing employment, but importantly, outside of the Atlanta
 7        Metropolitan Statistical Area (“MSA”), manufacturing wages exceed non-
 8        manufacturing wages by 16.6%. Thus, the premium of compensation that is paid
 9        by manufacturers is even greater – and significantly so – in the less urban regions
10        of the state. In many rural communities, a local manufacturer is both the largest and
11        best compensating employer.


12        However, even comparing average weekly wage data understates the premium in
13        total compensation paid by manufacturing firms because it does not capture the
14        value of employer–provided fringe benefit packages. At the national level,
15        employers’ actual contributions to discretionary fringe benefit programs were
16        27.4% higher in goods-producing firms than for service-producing firms.


17   Q.   CAN YOU DISCUSS THE DISPROPORTIONATE IMPACT THAT
18        MANUFACTURING              HAS       ON     GEORGIA’S          MORE        RURAL
19        COMMUNITIES?


20   A.   Manufacturing plays a disproportionately large role in the rural economies of
21        Georgia. In August 2022, manufacturing represented 5.9% of non-agricultural
22        employment in the Atlanta MSA and 12.9% of employment outside of the Atlanta
23        MSA. In other words, manufacturing employment comprises twice as large a
24        percentage of employment outside the 28-county Atlanta MSA as it does inside the
25        Atlanta MSA.

                    On Behalf of Georgia Association of Manufacturers
                               and Georgia Industrial Group
                                    Docket No. 44280
                                      Page 6 of 17

 1   Q.   WHAT DO YOU MEAN BY THE “MULTIPLIER EFFECT” AND WHY IS
 2        IT CRITICAL FOR UNDERSTANDING THE IMPORTANCE OF
 3        MANUFACTURING?


 4   A.   When evaluating the importance of various sectors of the economy, it is important
 5        to recognize the broad impacts of spending in one sector upon activity in other
 6        sectors. The indirect and induced spending associated with manufacturing creates
 7        a “multiplier effect” of the employment directly associated with manufacturing.


 8   Q.   CAN YOU DESCRIBE THE INDIRECT AND INDUCED EFFECTS?


 9   A.   Sure. When a manufacturing firm purchases products, services or raw materials
10        from local suppliers, employment by the supplier enterprises is enhanced. This is
11        known as the indirect effect. Further, when the employees of a manufacturer or its
12        suppliers spend their wages in their community, that spending also supports
13        employment at a variety of businesses. This is known as the induced effect.
14
15        The size of the “multiplier effect” in manufacturing is confirmed by data from the
16        U.S. Department of Commerce. Based on its 2021 Input-Output Accounts Data and
17        2020 Regional Data, the manufacturing sector has a total requirements multiplier
18        of 2.31. In other words, every dollar of final demand for manufactured products
19        generates an additional $1.31 in demand for products that are required to produce
20        the original manufactured product.


21        Of the fifteen sectors in the Department of Commerce classification, the
22        manufacturing sector had the highest multiplier effect.


23        Of course, the multiplier effects of manufacturing spill over broadly into most
24        industries in the economy. Of the $1.31 in additional product demand associated
     _______________________________________________________________________
                       Direct Testimony of Roger Tutterow, Ph.D.
                    On Behalf of Georgia Association of Manufacturers
                               and Georgia Industrial Group
                                    Docket No. 44280
                                      Page 7 of 17

 1         with the multiplier for manufactured products, over $0.74 of that demand is for
 2         products outside the manufacturing sectors. The professional and business services,
 3         mining, agriculture and wholesale trade sectors benefit the most from
 4         manufacturing’s multiplier effect. For example, for each dollar of final demand for
 5         manufacturing products, there is an associated demand of over $0.14 in demand for
 6         professional and business services and over $0.13 of activity in wholesale trade.
 7         Finally, these multipliers reflect linkages between the final demand for
 8         manufactured products and the associated demand for products from other
 9         industries. However, they do not capture the effect of the induced spending of
10         employees at the manufacturing and associated businesses in their communities.
11         Given the competitive compensation paid by the manufacturing sector, that induced
12         spending is likely quite significant.


13   II.   THE IMPACT OF ENERGY COSTS ON MANUFACTURERS


14   Q.    DO INCREASES IN ENERGY COSTS IMPACT THE ABILITY OF
15         MANUFACTURERS TO CONTINUE PRODUCTION AND COMPETE
16         EFFECTIVELY?


17   A.    Yes. Higher electricity rates raise the cost to businesses of producing goods and
18         services. Funds available to finance capital investment are lowered as well. For
19         industrial customers, electricity is not consumed as a final product itself, but rather,
20         is an input into the process through which they produce goods. To use the syntax
21         of microeconomics, energy is a “factor of production” rather than consumption
22         good. As with other factors of production, higher energy rates will increase the cost
23         to industrial customers of producing goods. This increase in cost likely will lead to
24         a reduction in competitiveness, lower production and employment or both,
25         depending on the ability of the business to pass on the higher production cost. In a


     _______________________________________________________________________
                       Direct Testimony of Roger Tutterow, Ph.D.
                    On Behalf of Georgia Association of Manufacturers
                               and Georgia Industrial Group
                                    Docket No. 44280
                                      Page 8 of 17

 1        competitive global marketplace, manufacturers find it difficult to pass along
 2        increased costs of production such as the cost of electricity.


 3   Q.   WHY CAN’T MANUFACTURERS SIMPLY PASS ALONG THESE COSTS
 4        IN THE PRICE OF THE PRODUCTS THEY SELL?


 5   A.   In the current competitive environment for manufacturers, it is not easy or even
 6        possible to do in many cases. Global competition, especially from low production
 7        cost areas of the world, serves as a strong constraint on the ability of manufacturers
 8        to pass along cost increases to customers. Customers are more likely to press
 9        manufacturers for lower prices than to pay more for the products they produce.
10        Manufacturers, then, are constantly challenged to lower their costs of products to
11        compensate for higher raw material, labor, and energy costs. There are practical
12        limits, including regulatory restrictions, on their ability to do so.            When
13        manufacturers cannot meet these pressures, they generally lose customers to a
14        lower cost producer, in many cases from another region or country.


15   Q.   CAN HIGHER ENERGY COSTS IMPACT A MANUFACTURER’S
16        DECISION AS TO WHERE TO LOCATE PRODUCTION?


17   A.   Yes. Facing the pressures of surviving in the current environment, manufacturers
18        need to maintain a competitive cost structure in order to succeed. If the costs of
19        production in a particular region, or state, become less competitive than readily
20        available alternatives, a manufacturer might need to relocate production to lower
21        cost states or countries. Energy costs certainly can be a driving factor in this regard.


22        Further, if a manufacturer with multiple production facilities decides to expand
23        production, there will be a clear preference for expanding activity in the facilities


     _______________________________________________________________________
                       Direct Testimony of Roger Tutterow, Ph.D.
                    On Behalf of Georgia Association of Manufacturers
                               and Georgia Industrial Group
                                    Docket No. 44280
                                      Page 9 of 17

 1        with the lower production cost. Those locations within the United States with lower
 2        production cost are the more likely beneficiaries of any shift in location.


 3   Q.   HOW WOULD YOU DESCRIBE THE REGIONAL ENVIRONMENT IN
 4        TERMS OF COMPETITION FOR MANUFACTURING?


 5   A.   There is significant competition for manufacturing investment and jobs from
 6        surrounding states. Many industrial customers have production facilities in other
 7        states in the region. Since labor flows easily between states, labor differentials will
 8        be smaller and energy costs are even more likely to be a factor in domestic location
 9        decisions.


10        With regard to local competition, what matters are the rates charged by electric
11        utilities in similarly situated locations. In the case of Georgia, the most natural
12        comparables would be the rates charged in other Southeastern states including
13        Alabama, North Carolina, South Carolina and Tennessee. To the extent the cost of
14        electricity in Georgia is greater than in neighboring states, it acts as a disincentive
15        to invest and create jobs in the state.


16        The importance of energy costs for regional locations decisions has long been
17        recognized by economic developers. While incentives are sometimes employed to
18        attract new enterprises, they are less likely to be used to retain existing enterprises.
19        Thus, recognizing the role of base energy prices in the competitive position of
20        existing enterprises is important for retaining, and encouraging the expansion of,
21        existing industry.    In a very real sense, Georgia competes with its closest
22        neighboring states to retain and expand industry and manufacturing and the myriad
23        of benefits they bring to the economy and well-being of all Georgia citizens.


     _______________________________________________________________________
                       Direct Testimony of Roger Tutterow, Ph.D.
                    On Behalf of Georgia Association of Manufacturers
                               and Georgia Industrial Group
                                    Docket No. 44280
                                      Page 10 of 17

 1        That energy costs affect location decisions for goods producing industries was
 2        recognized by the Special Council on Tax Reform and Fairness for Georgians on
 3        which I served in 2010. The council acknowledged its importance, and the
 4        uniqueness of the challenge faced by manufacturing and agricultural businesses, in
 5        recommending that energy used in manufacturing be exempted from sales taxes.
 6        While that recommendation was translated into policy, the removal of the sales tax
 7        from energy only reduced an existing competitive disadvantage faced by Georgia
 8        producers.


 9   Q.   ARE INDUSTRIAL AND OTHER CUSTOMER CLASSES EQUALLY
10        IMPACTED BY HIGHER ELECTRICITY RATES?


11   A.   No. In general, while higher electricity rates will increase the cost of production for
12        industrial customers or other customer classes, the magnitude of that effect will
13        vary across sectors of the economy and across businesses within each sector. For
14        reasons upon which I will further elaborate, the impact of higher electric rates on
15        manufacturers is more adverse than other customer classes.


16   Q.   WHAT FACTORS DETERMINE THE MAGNITUDE OF THE EFFECT OF
17        HIGHER ELECTRICITY RATES ON BUSINESSES?


18   A.   Higher electricity rates will impact all businesses primarily through changing their
19        cost structure. The magnitude of the effect of an increase in electricity rates on the
20        cost structure is determined by the proportion of the business’s operating cost that
21        is represented by the cost of electricity as well as their ability to substitute among
22        energy sources. For most industrial customers, energy and labor are generally
23        recognized as the two largest costs for conversion of raw materials into final
24        products. For some industrial customers, energy is the largest cost of conversion.


     _______________________________________________________________________
                       Direct Testimony of Roger Tutterow, Ph.D.
                    On Behalf of Georgia Association of Manufacturers
                               and Georgia Industrial Group
                                    Docket No. 44280
                                      Page 11 of 17

 1        Equally important is the effect of a rate increase on the competitive environment in
 2        which the customers operate. Specifically, if an increase in electricity rates leads to
 3        a comparable increase in production cost for all competitors in a market area, then
 4        the effect on the competitive position of a single business is mitigated. If a
 5        customer’s competitors are in areas not subject to the rate increase, then an increase
 6        in costs associated with a rate increase will diminish the customer’s competitive
 7        position. These competitors can be global or regional.


 8   Q.   PLEASE ELABORATE ON THE ISSUE OF REGIONAL COMPETITION.


 9   A.   Manufacturers generally will invest capital in locations where they can achieve the
10        lowest costs of production. This is particularly the case for manufacturers that
11        operate in several states within a region. As noted above, a manufacturer’s decision
12        as to where to place, consolidate, or curtail production can be significantly
13        influenced by the cost of energy.


14   Q.   HOW DO INDUSTRIAL AND OTHER CUSTOMERS COMPARE IN THIS
15        REGARD?


16   A.   One of the challenges facing industrial customers, and one which differentiates
17        their class from other classes of customers, is that they face competition regionally,
18        nationally and globally rather than just locally. Thus, many of the industrial
19        customers’ competitors will not be subject to the same increase in electricity rates
20        and resultant increase in production costs. As such, when an industrial customer
21        receives a rate increase, it will hurt their competitive position versus its competitors
22        who are operating outside the regulated area.


     _______________________________________________________________________
                       Direct Testimony of Roger Tutterow, Ph.D.
                    On Behalf of Georgia Association of Manufacturers
                               and Georgia Industrial Group
                                    Docket No. 44280
                                      Page 12 of 17

 1        It is understood that no business would welcome a rate increase. However, a rate
 2        increase generally has less effect on electricity customer who compete only locally
 3        because their competitors are likely to be subject to the same increase.


 4        Many industrial businesses have broad geographic markets and multiple production
 5        facilities. These production facilities within a company compete among themselves
 6        in the sense of having production levels varied in response to cost considerations,
 7        including the cost of electricity. They also compete for new investment on the basis
 8        of production costs.


 9        Thus, an increase in electricity rates would likely have a greater impact on
10        production, facility location and investment for expansion decisions of industrial
11        class customers than for business that compete only locally. Thus, the outcome of
12        these decisions impact job retention and job creation.


13        Further, the importance of initially selecting or remaining in a specific geographic
14        location, and hence being subject to a specific utility rate structure, is not the same
15        for all customer classes. Specifically, for businesses who compete only locally,
16        close proximity to their customers and the cost of real estate drive location decisions
17        more than the cost of electricity.


18        For example, for these businesses, being located in close geographic proximity to
19        their customers is the crucial consideration. Since customers tend to patronize
20        commercial businesses that are close to their residence or employment, the business
21        location is a determinant of the relevant customer market. For manufacturers,
22        however, the decision as to where to locate or expand a production facility is most
23        likely based on the cost of production, of which energy cost is a substantial
24        component.


     _______________________________________________________________________
                       Direct Testimony of Roger Tutterow, Ph.D.
                    On Behalf of Georgia Association of Manufacturers
                               and Georgia Industrial Group
                                    Docket No. 44280
                                      Page 13 of 17

 1          Businesses that depend on geographic proximity to customers will be less likely to
 2          reduce or cease operations in response to an increase in their electricity rates. As
 3          has been evident by the regional and international migration of manufacturing firms
 4          in past decades, the manufacturing sector has demonstrated the ability to relocate
 5          in response to changes in the cost of production.

Direct Testimony of Jeffry Pollock
1.  INTRODUCTION, QUALIFICATIONS AND SUMMARY
QPlease state your name and business address.
AJeffry Pollock; 12647 Olive Blvd., Suite 585, St. Louis, MO 63141.
QWhat is your occupation and by who are you employed?
AI am an energy advisor and President of J. Pollock, Incorporated.
QPlease state your educational background and experience.
AI have a Bachelor of Science Degree in Electrical Engineering and a Masters in Business Administration from Washington University in St. Louis, Missouri.  Since graduation in 1975, I have been engaged in a variety of consulting assignments, including energy procurement and regulatory matters in both the United States and several Canadian provinces.  My qualifications are documented in Appendix A.  A list of my appearances is provided in Appendix B to this testimony.  
QON WHOSE BEHALF ARE YOU TESTIFYING IN THIS PROCEEDING? 
AI am testifying on behalf of the Georgia Association of Manufacturers (GAM).  The members of GAM are customers of Georgia Power Company (GPC).  They consume large quantities of electricity, primarily for manufacturing under a variety of tariffs.  GAM members purchase nearly 11% of GPC’s retail energy sales.  
QWHAT ISSUES ARE YOU ADDRESSING IN YOUR TESTIMONY? 
AThis is the first of many rate increases that the Commission will decide over the next three years, which I will discuss initially.  I am also addressing:
The proposed Alternate Rate Plan (ARP);
Cost recovery for regulatory assets;
Class revenue allocation; and
Other tariff terms and conditions.
qAre any other witnesses appearing on behalf of GEORGIA ASSoCIATION OF MANUFACTURERS?
AYes.  My colleague, Ms. Billie S. LaConte, will address the issues raised by GPC witness, James M. Coyne, in determining the return on equity (ROE) and capital structure.  Dr. Roger Tutterow will address the state of manufacturing in Georgia.
QAre you sponsoring any exhibits with your testimony? 
AYes.  I’m sponsoring Exhibits JP-1 through JP-9.   These exhibits were prepared by me or under my supervision and direction.  
qDoes the fact that you are not addressing all issues in this proceeding constitute an endorsement of the positions of Georgia Power Company, or other parties, on these issues?
ANo.  The fact that I am not addressing every issue should not be interpreted as an endorsement of GPC’s or other parties’ proposals in this proceeding.
Summary
qPlease summarize your findings and recommendations.
AMy findings and conclusions are as follows:
This Case in Perspective
GPC is on the precipice of a “perfect storm” of at least seven rate increases that could result in cumulative rate increases of up to $2.9 billion over the next three years.
Even before the first of three step increases is implemented under GPC’s proposed ARP, customers taking service under the Real Time Pricing (RTP) rates have already experienced double-digit rate increases.  
Higher RTP prices have generated $565 million of contributions to fixed costs during the current ARP through July 2022.  Thus far in 2022, the fixed cost contributions through July 2022 alone ($191 million) exceed the $95 million of fixed cost contributions included in GPC’s test-year revenue requirement.  
To offset future rate increases, the Commission should identify alternatives to traditional ratemaking — securitization in particular — that would allow the Company to recover its capital costs while minimizing rate impacts on customers.  
Securitization is an accepted practice for recovering large capital investments (retired plant) and other major unexpected costs (storm damage) to alleviate the upward rate pressures facing GPC’s customers.  The cost of securitizing utility assets is only a fraction of GPC’s pre-tax cost of capital under traditional ratemaking.  Further, securitization bonds can extend cost recovery for periods ranging from four to over 20 years, thereby further alleviating upward rate pressure.  
Alternate Rate Plan
The proposed ARP would implement three step increases effective on January 1, 2023, 2024, and 2025.  However, the vast majority of the increase would occur on January 1, 2023.  
The three step increases are based entirely on three projected test years.  Use of three projected test years requires accurate projections of future sales, revenues, investments, expenses and interest rates.  Such projections are inherently inaccurate, particularly, in this case, for the 2nd and 3rd years of the proposed ARP.
GPC’s past projections have allowed it to earn above the authorized midpoint ROE in each of the last ten years.  In six of those years, the earned ROE exceeded the upper end of the authorized ROE band.  
In light of past experience, the base rate and Environmental Compliance Cost Recovery (ECCR) increases should be implemented in three steps, and GPC should be required to update its base rate and traditional ECCR projections prior to implementing the 2nd and 3rd years of the ARP.  
Cost Recovery of Major Assets
Higher depreciation and amortization expenses are a major driver of the proposed base revenue increase.  These higher expenses are a direct result of GPC’s Integrated Resource Plan (IRP) to retire/early retire major power plants (i.e., Wansley, Scherer, Bowen Units 1-2) with over $3.1 billion of remaining (unrecovered) investment.  
GPC is also seeking recovery of the costs of the six capacity power purchase agreements (PPAs).  Excluding Bowen Units 1-2 (for which a retirement decision was deferred), the capacity of these PPAs roughly offsets the capacity of the retired/early retired plants.  
GPC is proposing to earn a return on both the retired/early retired plants and as a result of the additional sums on the six capacity PPAs.  
The simultaneous recovery of both retired/early retired plants and the six capacity PPAs effectively charges retail customers for redundant capacity. 
Retired plants no longer provide electricity to the public.  Thus, the costs of retired plants are not used and useful.  A utility should not earn a return on equity on investments in facilities that are not used and useful.  
To avoid placing unnecessary upward pressure on rates, GPC should amortize its investment in Scherer Unit Nos. 1, 2 and 3 over their current remaining lives, which range from 20-25 years.  
GPC is proposing to continue deferred recovery of coal combustion residual (CCR) costs, thereby earning a return on any amounts that have not been recovered in rates.  
GPC is projecting to incur $986 million in CCR costs during the ARP.  This is in addition to the CCR costs that have been incurred but not yet recovered in rates.  
Securitizing the remaining investments in Plant Wansley and the as-incurred/projected CCR costs would place less upward pressure on rates than under traditional ratemaking.  
Until securitization is authorized, GPC should not earn a return on equity on the regulatory assets (i.e., Plant Wansley, CCR costs).  
Class Revenue Allocation
GPC’s proposed equal percentage base rate increase would not result in moving rate groups closer to parity. 
Any base rate increase authorized by the Commission should continue the policy established in the 2010 rate case and continued in the 2013 and 2019 rate cases to continue moving each rate group closer to parity.  
Other Tariff Terms and Conditions
GPC’s proposed Rule 9 would absolve it of any liability for damages sustained by customers, even if outages or equipment failures were the result of gross negligence.  Further, GPC is proposing to limit its liability for damages to the net book value of the customer’s equipment.  
A customer’s damages from electricity outages can be much more extensive than the net book value of the equipment or personal property.  For example, outages can result in lost product, lost sales, additional maintenance expense and potentially higher replacement costs for new equipment.  
Consistent with best practices, Rule 9 should be revised so that GPC is liable for damages caused by willful misconduct and gross negligence.  The amount of damages should, at a minimum, be sufficient to replace the customer’s damaged equipment and/or personal property.  

2.  ThE Rate case in perspective
Qwhat is the company requesting in this case?
AGPC is asking the Commission to continue the process of a three-year ARP with a one-time “levelized” base rate increase taking effect on January 1, 2023 and two additional step increases for specific cost components that would become effective on January 1, 2024 and January 1, 2025.  Originally, GPC requested a one-time $738 million levelized base revenue increase.  However, in its Supplemental filing it increased the request to $762 million.  The $37.6 million additional base revenue increase reflects various items included in the final Order approved in the 2022 IRP and Demand Side Management (DSM) proceedings.
Qare these the only rate increases that Georgia Power Company will be seeking from the commission?
ANo.  As I have testified in past cases, we are on the precipice of a “perfect storm” of rate increases that will occur over the next three years.  Table 1 provides an estimate of the potential impact of the seven future rate increases.  As Table 1 reveals, GPC will be seeking cumulative rate increases of up to $2.9 billion over the next three years.

As can be seen, the proposed ARP is but one component of the upcoming rate increases.  GPC will be adjusting rates to recognize the commercial operation of Vogtle Unit 3 when it is placed in commercial operation in the spring of 2023 and further in the fall of 2024, and when fuel is loaded into Vogtle Unit 4 in the fall of 2023.  In addition, GPC reported a $948 million fuel cost recovery (FCR) under-collection in June.  The balance has continued to grow significantly despite the Interim Fuel Rider that was implemented on January 1, 2022.  Assuming gas prices remain at current high levels, the FCR rate increase could exceed $1.1 billion.
In summary, retail customers are potentially facing $2.9 billion in rate increases over the next three years.  
Qhave any retail customers already experienced substantial rate increases?
AYes.  Even before Step 1 of the proposed ARP takes effect, customers taking service under the RTP rate schedules have already experienced and will continue paying record high prices.
Chart 1
The much higher prices can be attributed to much higher replacement fuel costs (particularly natural gas) and hotter than normal weather.
Qwhat has been the impact of the high RTP Prices?
AHigher RTP prices have resulted in much higher contributions to fixed costs by RTP customers.  The fixed cost contributions consist of:
A Reliability adder that reflects the marginal cost of generation capacity;
A Transmission adder that reflects the marginal cost of transmission capacity; and
Risk adders ranging from $1 to $3 per megawatt-hour (MWh) for Day Ahead (DA) and Hour Ahead (HA) pricing.
The Reliability and Transmission adders apply when demand approaches and exceeds the projected Southern Company system peak, and the Risk adders apply to all hours.
As a result of the higher prices, RTP customers have paid $565 million in fixed cost contributions during the current ARP through July 2022.  In 2022 alone, they contributed $191 million to fixed costs through July, which is twice GPC’s projected ($95 million) test-year fixed cost contributions.  Thus, it is clear that a large segment of GPC’s retail customers has already experienced substantial rate increases, even before base rates are adjusted.  Further, GPC’s non-RTP customers have benefitted and continue to benefit from the fixed costs that RTP customers pay.  
Qrecognizing the perfect storm of upcoming rate increases and the much higher RTP prices, what steps should the commission take to protect the interests of retail customers?
AThe objective should be to minimize future rate impacts while affording GPC a reasonable opportunity to earn a reasonable return on the facilities that are used and useful in providing electricity service to the public.
Qhow can the commission achieve this objective?
AFirst, the Commission should thoroughly scrub each of the three projected test years used to support GPC’s proposed ARP.  
Second, recognizing that GPC’s proposed 11% ROE is approximately 150 basis points above the national average, the Commission should, at a minimum, establish an ROE and capital structure based on the specific corrections to Mr. Coyne’s testimony as recommended by Ms. LaConte.  
Third, the Commission should identify alternatives to traditional ratemaking that would allow the Company to recover its capital costs while minimizing rate impacts on customers.  For example, $297 million of the proposed revenue requirement reflects changes in depreciation rates.  As discussed later, GPC is proposing to accelerate the recovery of certain assets due to recent retirement decisions and extend the depreciable lives of other assets.  Accelerated/extended depreciation can alleviate rate pressure.  However, this option is not cost-free because GPC would continue to earn a return on the unamortized balance.  The only alternative that would both alleviate rate pressure and reduce the overall revenue requirement is securitization.  
Qwhat is securitization?
ASecuritization is a financing mechanism that provides timely recovery of certain regulatory assets by issuing a type of debt that would significantly lower the carrying costs of the assets relative to the costs that would be incurred using traditional ratemaking practices.  Specifically, a special purpose entity would be created whose sole purpose is to issue transition bonds that are used solely for purposes of reducing the amount of recoverable regulatory assets and any other amounts as determined by the Commission through the refinancing or retirement of utility debt or equity.  This special purpose entity would be entitled to the revenues collected by the utility through a separate cost recovery mechanism in an amount sufficient to timely recovery of the debt services associated with securitization bonds.
Under this structure, securitization bonds would be “AAA” rated and carry the lowest possible interest rate.  For example, recently issued securitization bonds have had an average interest rate of 3.6%.  This is substantially below GPC’s proposed 9.8% pre-tax cost of capital.  The much lower cost reflects the fact that there is no equity and no federal and/or state income taxes associated with securitized financing.  Further, securitization bonds issued to date have maturities ranging from four to over 20 years.  Therefore, securitization financing would be the most economical means of recovering the costs of long-term regulatory assets, such as retired coal plants and CCR obligations.
QIs securitization widely used?
AYes.  Exhibit JP-1 shows that 27 states, the District of Columbia and Puerto Rico have enacted utility securitization laws.  Thus, securitization is authorized in the majority of states.
Qhow is SECURITIZATION financing typically used?
AInitially, securitization was used to recover utility assets that became stranded when generation was no longer subject to price regulation.  More recently, securitization has become more widely used to recover retired coal plant assets, to finance major storm damage, and to recover extraordinary costs incurred during Winter Strom Uri.
QCan securitization be beneficial for customers?
AYes.  If the legislation is properly drafted, securitization can be a more cost-effective means of recovering retired assets and other obligations than under traditional ratemaking.  To demonstrate, Exhibit JP-2 provides an illustration of the impact of securitizing $1.5 billion of assets.  The illustration shows the annual revenue requirement in both nominal and net present value (NPV) dollar costs to customers under traditional ratemaking (the solid green and dashed black lines) and securitization (the orange bars).  For purposes of the illustration, I have used GPC’s proposed cost of capital, current state/federal income tax rates and both accelerated (9-year) and nominal (20-year) amortization.  Finally, I assumed an average interest rate of 3.61% with upfront costs of 2% of the securitizable amount.  The interest rate assumption was based on a securitization issued by Entergy Texas, Inc., on March 25, 2022. 
QHow does securitization compare with traditional ratemaking?
AThe vertical axis shows the annual revenue requirement.  The higher the annual revenue requirement, the higher the rate impact.  
As can be seen, the annual revenue requirement for securitizing $1.5 billion of regulatory assets is less than $150 million per year over the 14-year average life of the securitization bonds.  By contrast, the annual revenue requirements under traditional ratemaking (for either a nine or 20-year amortization period) would be higher than for securitization.  Table 2 below compares the net cost to retail customers under those options.
Table 2
Net Cost to Retail Customers

Thus, not only would securitization place less upward pressure on rates, it would be less costly for customers on both a nominal and NPV basis.
qhave credit rating agencies expressed any opinions on the use of securitized financing?
AYes.  For example, Moody’s Investors Service opined that utility cost recovery through securitization is credit positive provided that it is not over-used.  A copy of Moody’s assessment is provided in Exhibit JP-3.  The key takeaways are:
Securitization typically benefits utilities and their current customers because utilities receive an immediate source of cash and are ensured recovery of large costs in a timely manner, and because the cost of securitized debt is lower than the utility’s all-in pre-tax cost of capital, it reduces the impact on customers’ monthly bills. 
Utility cost recovery charge securitization has become more versatile and prevalent.  It is now used to recover costs associated with storm restoration and environmental costs, utility restructuring, deferred fuel costs and renewable energy projects. 
State law and financing orders strongly protect securitization assets which ultimately protect the assets backing the bonds.
Too much securitization can have negative consequences such as impacting customer bills substantially while hurting the utility’s financial flexibility and ability to raise rates for other reasons.  
Regulatory Research Associates, a subsidiary of S&P, recently observed that there has been a rekindled interest in securitization, stating:           
This financing method allows utilities to include the revenue stream to repay the bonds within customer bills as a separate, non-bypassable surcharge that is subject to true-up. Amounts collected by the company through the surcharge are remitted to a special purpose entity, which then disburses the payments to investors. 
While it is unclear if securitization requires legislation, a specific legislative mandate generally improves the rating accorded to the securitization bonds and lowers the associated cost of capital, given that a legislatively supported revenue stream may be more difficult to rescind than a stand-alone order of a state commission. In RRA's experience, no state utility regulatory commission has authorized securitization in the absence of enabling legislation.  
Securitization is viewed as an attractive option because it allows regulators to reduce the customer rate impacts related to the recovery of a particular utility asset. The carrying charge on the asset would be the lower interest rate applied to a highly rated, usually AAA, corporate bond as compared to the utility's WACC, which includes both equity and debt components, or even the interest rate applied to typical utility corporate bonds, which are generally rated BBB and carry higher interest rates. 
At the same time, securitization reduces the investment risk for the utility by providing the utility up-front recovery of its investment in what are usually non-revenue-producing assets — assets that are not dedicated to providing service to current ratepayers. The company can then redeploy those investment dollars elsewhere.  
Qis securitization possible under the current regulatory and legal environment in Georgia?
ANo.  It is my understanding that securitization would require both a change in law and regulations.  I also understand that a securitization bill has been drafted and reviewed by several parties, including GPC.  Thus, there is no reason why a securitization bill could not be introduced and enacted in the next legislative session.
QWhat can the commission do to facilitate securitization?
AThis rate case provides an opportunity to identify the capital assets that would most likely be candidates for securitized financing.  For example, as discussed later, GPC is seeking full recovery of the now-retired Plants Wansley and Boulevard.  GPC continues to defer CCR costs and is seeking to recover $986 million of additional CCR costs over the duration of the ARP.  Both the retired plants and unrecovered/expected CCR costs would be ideal candidates for securitization.  
Therefore, I recommend that the Commission quantify and set-aside the portion of costs associated with Plant Wansley and the unrecovered/expected CCR costs in this proceeding.  Setting aside these specific costs will facilitate a timely rate adjustment once GPC issues securitization bonds to finance these costs, while avoiding double-recovery.  
qhow should these costs be recovered prior to securitization?
AAs previously stated, Plant Wansley was retired and the investment is not used and useful.  Further, the CCR costs that are associated with Plant Wansley and other retired power plants are also not used and useful.  Under these circumstances, the Commission should not allow a return on the unamortized balance.  However, recognizing GPC’s need to finance regulatory assets, the Commission should authorize a return capped at GPC’s cost of long-term debt.  

3.  Alternate rate plan

As previously stated, GPC is proposing to implement a one-time levelized increase in base rates and the traditional ECCR rate.  Alternatively, GPC could have proposed three smaller base rate increases. 
QDo you have any concerns regarding Georgia Power Company’s alternate rate plan?
AYes.  The proposed ARP is based entirely on three projected test years:  calendar year 2023, calendar year 2024, and calendar year 2025.  None of these projections reflect GPC’s official corporate budgets, which were prepared last fall.  Use of three projected test years requires accurate projections of future sales, revenues, investments, expenses and interest rates.  Such projections, particularly in the 2nd and 3rd years of the proposed ARP, are inherently inaccurate.
QIs Georgia Power Company proposing to update its projections prior to implementing the 2nd and 3rd year increases under the proposed alternate rate plan?
ANo.  With the exception of the CCR and DSM rate components, which would be updated in November prior to the following rate year, the base rate and ECCR components would be fixed based on the three projected test years. 
Qwhy do you assert that Georgia Power Company’s forecasts are inherently inaccurate?
AExhibit JP-4 shows GPC’s earned ROE for the past 10 years under the then-applicable Accounting Orders.  Under these Accounting Orders, GPC was authorized to earn a mid-point ROE ranging from 10.5% to 11.15%.  The ceiling ROE ranged from 12% to 12.25%, and the ROE floor ranged from 9.5% to 10.25%.  
As can be seen in Exhibit JP-4, reliance on GPC’s projections has resulted in GPC consistently earning a ROE above the mid-point in each of the last 10 years.  Further, GPC earned above the upper end of the ROE range in six of the last 10 years.  At no time did GPC’s earnings ever fall below the ROE mid-point or, obviously, the ROE floor.
Thus, based on this past experience, GPC’s projections have either overstated future costs, or understated future sales and/or revenues.
Qwhat do you recommend?
ARecognizing the other substantial rate increases likely to occur in 2023 (as listed in Table 1), prior experience as discussed earlier, and to provide a more gradual rate path, GAM members strongly prefer a three-stepped increase over GPC’s proposed levelized increase.  GPC should be required to update its base rate and traditional ECCR projections prior to implementing the 2nd and 3rd years of the ARP.  Thus, for the years 2024 and 2025, new base rates should be set using updated test-year projections based on GPC’s then-official corporate budgets.  The updated budget projections should be filed within 60 days prior to the effective date of new rates.  This would provide the Commission and parties ample time to review the projections prior to the rates becoming effective.
qhas the commission previously approved stepped base rate increases in an alternate rate plan?
AYes.  In the last rate case, GPC also requested a levelized base rate increase.  However, the Commission approved a settlement between GPC, the Commission Staff, GAM and other parties authorizing three stepped base rate increases.  

4.  Cost Recovery of Major Assets
qare changes in the timing of cost recovery for certain major assets a driver of the proposed base revenue increase?
AYes.  As a result of the decisions in the IRP case, GPC is proposing to transfer the remaining investments of Plants Wansley and Boulevard to a regulatory asset account.  Both plants were removed from service this year.  GPC is also proposing to accelerate the recovery of plants that will be retired early, prior to the lifespans established in the last rate case.  This includes Plants Scherer Unit Nos. 1, 2, and 3 and Bowen Units 1 and 2.  In these cases, GPC would continue to earn a full return on the amortized balances of the affected assets.  
qare any other decisions made in the integrated resource planning case also affecting the proposed base revenue increase?
AYes.  During the ARP, GPC will begin incurring purchased capacity costs under the six capacity PPAs approved in the IRP.  This includes purchases from Wansley Unit No. 7, Harris Unit No. 2, Monroe Unit Nos. 1 and 2, and Dahlberg Unit Nos. 2, 6, 8, 9 and 10.  GPC will also recover an additional sum on these expenses.  The additional sum is the financial equivalent of a return on equity.
qwill the amount of capacity provided by the Power purchase AGREEMENTS approximately offset the capacity previously supplied by the retired/early retired plants?
AYes.  Table 4 summarizes the approved plant retirements and capacity additions.

qwill the proposed base rates include the recovery of both the capital costs of the retired/early retired plants and the six capacity Power purchase agreements?
AYes.  The proposed base rates include recovery of the retired/early retired plants as well as the replacement purchased power capacity.  As previously stated, GPC will earn a return on both the retired/early retired plants and the six capacity PPAs.

qdo the retired/early retired plants represent a significant investment?
AYes.  Table 5 shows that GPC has approximately $3.3 billion of investment in Plants Wansley, Bowen Unit Nos. 1 and 2, and Scherer Unit Nos. 1, 2 and 3.
