GPC is proposing to recover Plant Wansley over eight years.  This coincides with the remaining life as determined in the last rate case.  However, GPC is proposing to recover the investments in Scherer Unit Nos. 1, 2 and 3 and Bowen Unit Nos. 1 and 2 over nine years.  The Scherer units have remaining lives ranging from 20 to 25 years.  Thus, GPC’s proposal to accelerate the recovery of Scherer Unit Nos. 1, 2 and 3 will place upward pressure on rates.
qdo you have concerns with the company’s cost recovery proposals?
AYes.  First, retail customers would be charged for redundant capacity.  Second, Plant Wansley (and after 2028, Scherer Unit Nos. 1, 2 and 3) is no longer used and useful.  A utility should not expect to earn a return on investment for facilities that are no longer used and useful in providing electricity to the public.  Third, abruptly changing the depreciation schedule for Scherer Unit Nos 1, 2, and 3 is unfair to GPC's current ratepayers.  
qis there any precedent for denying a return on a plant that is not used and useful?
AYes.  In Docket No. 46449, the Public Utility Commission of Texas denied a proposal by a utility to include its investment in the then-retired Welsh Unit 2 in rate base, thereby denying a return on the unamortized balance.  In rejecting the utility’s proposed ratemaking treatment, the Commission stated:
68. Because Welsh unit 2 is no longer used and useful, SWEPCO may not include its investment associated with the plant in its rate base, and may not earn a return on that remaining investment.
69. Allowing SWEPCO a return of, but not on, its remaining investment in Welsh unit 2 balances the interests of ratepayers and shareholders with respect to a plant that no longer provides service.
70. It is reasonable for SWEPCO to recover the remaining undepreciated balance of Welsh unit 2 over the 24-year remaining lives of Welsh units 1 and 3.
qwhat do you recommend?
AFirst, as previously discussed, the remaining investment in Plant Wansley should be securitized.  This would allow GPC to immediately recover its capital without an additional return.  This is appropriate because, as previously stated, Plant Wansley is no longer used and useful.  In the interim, Plant Wansley regulatory asset should be amortized over eight years with no return on equity.
Second, to avoid placing unnecessary upward pressure on rates, GPC should amortize its investment in Scherer Unit Nos. 1, 2 and 3 over their current remaining lives, which range from 20-25 years, with a return on the unamortized balance.  However, once Scherer Unit Nos. 1, 2 and 3 have been retired, the remaining investments should be securitized.
CCR Costs
qis Georgia Power Company proposing to continue its recovery of coal combustion residual costs?
AYes.  GPC is proposing to continue the recovery of CCR costs while earning a return on the unamortized CCR obligation.  In 2021, GPC recovered $166 million of CCR costs in rates.  By 2025, GPC projects that the CCR annual revenue requirement would reach $386 million.  Thus, it is a significant and growing component of GPC’s overall revenue requirement.  But for the sheer magnitude of the CCR obligation, which GPC estimates at $6.8 billion, these costs would be expensed, and GPC would not earn a return on the unamortized CCR balance.  
qIs a return on unamortized balance the only cost associated with coal combustion residual cost recovery?
ANo.  The return applied to the unamortized CCR obligation includes a return on equity.  Further, because equity is taxable, retail customers are paying not only an additional return to shareholders, they are also funding the income tax liability associated with the shareholder return.  The inclusion of both a return on equity and associated income taxes places unnecessary upward pressure on rates.
qHow can this unnecessary upward pressure be ALLEVIATED? 
AGPC should securitize all unrecovered CCR expenses, as well as a substantial portion of the CCR expenses that will be incurred during the ARP.  As previously stated, securitization is a less expensive alternative to traditional ratemaking because it is financed solely by much lower debt cost.  Further, securitization bonds can be issued with extended maturities at a cost that is less than 50% of GPC’s pre-tax cost of capital.  Prior to securitization, the CCR regulatory asset should earn a return based on GPC’s long-term cost of debt.  

5.  Class revenue allocation
qwhat is class revenue allocation?
AClass revenue allocation is the process of determining how any base revenue change the Commission approves should be spread to each customer class the utility serves.  
qHOW IS Georgia Power Company PROPOSING TO allocate THE PROPOSED BASE REVENUE INCREASE IN THIS PROCEEDING?
AGPC is proposing to spread the proposed base revenue increase by applying an approximately equal percentage increase to current base revenues.  This is shown in Exhibit JP-5.  
qwould an equal percentage increase move rate groups closer to parity?
ANo.  As discussed later, the class cost-of-service study (CCOSS) results show that the rate groups are not currently at parity.  Some rate groups are below parity while other rate groups are above parity.  Moving rates toward parity means applying above-average base revenue increases to those rate groups that are currently below parity and below-average base revenue increases to those rate groups that are currently above parity.  
qwhat is a class cost-of-service study?
AA CCOSS is an analysis used to determine each class’s responsibility for a utility’s costs.  Thus, it determines whether the revenues a class generates cover the class’s cost of service.  A CCOSS separates the utility's total costs into portions incurred on behalf of the various customer groups.  Most of a utility's costs are incurred to jointly serve many customers.  For purposes of revenue allocation and rate design, customers are grouped into homogeneous classes according to their usage patterns and service characteristics.  The procedures typically used in a CCOSS are described in more detail in Appendix C.  
qhas georgia power filed a class cost-of-service study in this case?
AYes.  GPC filed four CCOSSs – two studies were based on calendar year 2021 (the historical test period) and two studies were based on the test year ending July 31, 2023.  One set of studies was presented by rate group and by rate schedule while the other set of studies was presented by customer class.  
qdid georgia power acknowledge an error in the filed class cost-of-service studies?
AYes.  GPC acknowledged that there was an error in the filed CCOSSs.  Specifically, the filed studies overstated the revenues from the Medium Business rate group and  understated the revenues from the Marginally Priced rate group.  A corrected CCOSS was provided.  
qdoes the corrected class cost-of-service study generally comport with accepted industry practice?
AYes.  The corrected CCOSS generally comports with accepted industry practice.  Specifically, it recognizes the different types of costs incurred by the utility, as well as the different ways electricity is delivered to, and used by, the various types of customers.  In particular:
All production and transmission plant costs and related expenses were classified to demand.  
A portion of the distribution network was classified as a customer-related cost.
For the Marginally Priced rate group, production/transmission demand allocation factors were based on the customer baseline load (CBL), which is the load that GPC must plan to serve.  
Thus, GPC’s CCOSSs are consistent with the principles of cost causation; that is, costs are allocated to rate groups based on the degree that each rate group caused GPC to incur each specific cost.  
This is in stark contrast to theoretical studies that allocate production plant costs on energy usage on the mistaken belief that some investment is incurred to save fuel costs or that individual power plants benefit certain customer classes more than others and, therefore, should be allocated differently, rather than as a unified generation fleet.  A more in-depth assessment of GPC’s CCOSSs and these other competing allocation theories is provided in Appendix C.  
qare there differences between the two sets of cost studies?
AYes.  The CCOSSs used the same methodologies except for the allocation of production and transmission plant and related expenses.  GPC’s preferred CCOSS allocates production and 20% of transmission plant and related expenses using the twelve coincident peak (12CP) method.  The remaining transmission costs were allocated to classes using the four coincident peak (4CP) method.  
qplease describe the 12cp and 4Cp methods.
AUnder 12CP, each class’s share of allocated costs is based on the demand that occurs coincident with the system peak in all 12 months.  GPC also filed a second CCOSS 
using the 4CP method.  Like 12CP, the 4CP method allocates costs based on the demand that occurs coincident with the system peak during the summer months, June through September.  
qwhat is the rationale for using a specific demand allocation method, such as 12cp or 4CP.  
AThe choice of an allocation method should reflect the load characteristics of the utility.  For example, 4CP is appropriate for a utility that is primarily summer peaking, while 12CP is appropriate for a utility that has no discernable peak periods.  The rationale for using 12CP and 4CP is discussed in more detail in Appendix C.  
qis georgia power company a summer peaking utility?
AYes.  Chart 2 below compares the monthly system peaks as a percent of GPC’s annual system peak.  The red bars denote when the annual system peak occurs.  The red/blue bars show the months when the system demand is close to the annual system peak.  The other months are shown in the blue bars.  
Chart 2
Monthly Peak Demands as a Percent of
GPC’s Historical and Test Year Annual System Peak

As Chart 2 demonstrates, GPC is summer-peaking.  However, GPC’s winter peaks are growing faster than its summer peaks, and as a consequence, GPC will have both summer and winter peaks in future test years.  
qare there differences in the share of production/transmission costs allocated to each rate group under the 12CP and 4CP methods?
AYes.  Exhibit JP-6 provides a comparison of the allocation factors for each rate group under 12CP (column 1) and 4CP (column 3).  Recognizing the convergence between the summer and winter peaks, I have also quantified the allocation factors based on an average of the four summer and two winter system peaks (column 2).  As can be seen, each method allocates a different share of production/transmission costs to rate groups.  
qwhich of the allocation methods shown in exhibit JP-6 best fits georgia power company’s system load characteristics?
ACurrently, the 4CP method best reflects GPC’s load characteristics.  However, in the future the average of the four summer/two winter CP method will become a better fit as the summer and winter peaks converge.  As can be seen in Exhibit JP-6, the allocation factors under this approach more closely resemble 4CP than the 12CP. demand allocation factors.  
In any event, the 12CP method is not reflective of GPC’s load characteristics.  Thus, the results of GPC’s preferred CCOSS are not an accurate reflection of the parity ratios.  The additional flaws with the 12CP method are discussed in Appendix C.  
qDespite the differences in the various allocation methodologies shown in exhibit jp-5, do the results of the corrected class Cost-of-service studies confirm that certain rate groups are either above or below parity?
AYes.  Exhibits JP-7 and JP-8 present the corrected CCOSS results at present rates under the 12CP/4CP and 4CP methods described above.  The results are summarized in Table 6 below.  If the rates were at parity, each rate group would have a parity ratio (or relative rate of return) of 100%.  As Table 6 demonstrates, the Medium and Large Business groups are well above parity, while the Domestic and Government/Institution groups are well below parity.

qReferring to exhibits jp-7 and jp-8, please explain the terms rate of return, relative rate of return, and subsidy.
AThe rate of return (column 3) is the net operating income (i.e., revenues less allocated expenses – column 2) divided by the allocated rate base (column 1).  The relative rate of return or parity ratio (column 4) measures each rate group’s rate of return (column 3) as a percent of the 6.31% Total Retail rate of return (line 9, column 3).  The subsidy (column 5), measures the difference between actual revenues and the allocated costs.  Thus, a negative amount means that a class is providing revenues less than its allocated costs, while a positive amount means that a class is providing revenues above its allocation costs.  
qdo you have any further observations about the corrected class cost-of-service study results?
AYes.  The results for the Marginally Priced rate group are significantly affected by the test-year contribution to fixed costs by RTP customers.  This is because RTP customers are included in this class, and the contributions to fixed costs is a large portion of the revenue stream from RTP sales.  As discussed previously (in Section 2), fixed cost contributions from RTP sales during the test-year were only $95 million, which was only 50% of the ($191 million) of fixed cost contributions in the first seven months of 2022.  Thus, the net revenues and rates of return from the Marginally Priced rate group are clearly under-stated.  This group is clearly much more profitable to GPC than is indicated in the corrected CCOSSs.  
qdoes the commission have to approve a specific class cost-of-service study to determine an appropriate class revenue allocation?
ANo.  Despite the different methodologies, the CCOSS results are relatively consistent.  The rate groups that are below parity under GPC’s preferred CCOSS are also below parity under each of the alternative CCOSSs, and vice versa.  
qwould rates move closer to parity under georgia power company’s proposed class revenue allocation?
ANo.  The movement to parity is shown in Exhibit JP-9 using GPC’s preferred CCOSS for illustrative purposes.  As can be seen, GPC’s equal percent base increases applied to each rate group would, for the most part, result in higher subsidies.  Thus, rates would not move closer to parity.  
Qhas the Commission endorsed moving rates closer to PARITY?
AYes.  In the 2010, 2013, and 2019 rate cases, the Commission approved class revenue allocations that moved rates closer to parity.  In each case the Commission approved parity adjustments ranging from 75% to 125% of the system average base revenue increase.  For example, in 2010 the Commission found:  
…the allocation of costs and rate design contemplated and specified in the Stipulation are reasonable and in the best interests of all customer groups, and represent a balanced and fair settlement of the positions advocated by the parties in this docket.
Furthermore, in the 2013 rate case the Commission stated: 
The resulting rate increases will be allocated by rate group using the revenue distribution method recommended by Staff Witness Watkins, with some exceptions and with the spread adjusted for balancing. (Panel Rebuttal Testimony, p.19). The exceptions are based on testimony by the Company and several intervenors with regard to further movements towards parity and the importance of the RTP rate to remain as currently designed and for further RTP tariff availability. (Panel Rebuttal Testimony, p 19-20). In order to further address rate parity issues, in particular with respect to the TOU-MB rate, that group will receive a rate increase of 80% of what would otherwise be allocated Watkins' proposed revenue distribution method.
In the most recent (2019) rate case, the Commission stated:
The Proposed Agreement provides that beginning in 2021, with the adjustment to traditional base rate tariff, the rate increase shall be allocated by rate group, except as otherwise provided in Proposed Agreement, with adjustments for balancing.  Rates in Medium Business and Large Business rate groups will receive 85% of the overall base rate increase with the resulting revenue deficiency being spread to the other rate groups excluding the Small Business, Lighting and Marginal rate groups which will receive the overall base rate increase (Agreement Provision 17).   The Commission finds this provision of the Proposed Agreement to be just and reasonable, and is hereby approved.
qshould rates continue to move toward parity?
AYes.  Base revenues should reflect the actual cost of providing service to each rate group as closely as practicable.  Cost-based rates are fair (because each class’s rates reflect its cost to serve, no more and no less); they are efficient (because, when coupled with a cost-based rate design, customers are provided with the proper incentive to minimize their costs, which will, in turn, minimize the costs to the utility); they enhance revenue stability (because changes in revenues due to changes in sales will translate into offsetting changes in costs); and they encourage conservation (because cost-based rates will send the proper price signals to customers, thereby allowing customers to make rational consumption decisions). 
q         are there any Other compelling reasons to set rates closer to cost in this proceeding?
A          Yes.  As Dr. Tutterow testifies, manufacturing is essential to creating wealth for local communities.  However, economic activity has been slowing, industrial production has decreased modestly in three of the past five quarters, and inflation pressures are persuasive through the economy.  Many businesses will face challenges because in a competitive global marketplace, it is difficult to pass along higher production costs, such as the cost of electricity.  Further, as previously discussed, much higher RTP prices have already resulted in higher electricity costs.  For all of these additional reasons, minimizing base rate increases to the Medium and Large Business and Marginally Priced classes is critical to retaining and potentially increasing manufacturing jobs (and the attendant economic benefits) in Georgia.  
Qwhat do you recommend?
ABased on the CCOSS results shown in Table 6 and Exhibit JP-9, I recommend that the Commission continue to move rate groups closer to parity.  Table 7 summarizes my recommended target parity adjustments.

Acknowledging gradualism, the target parity adjustments constrained to between 0.77 times and 1.28 times the system average revenue increase excluding FCR. Rate groups with current parity ratios above 100% would receive below system-average increases, while rate groups with current parity ratios below 100 would receive above system-average increases.  
qshould the same parity adjustments be applied in determining the 2024 and 2025 base rate increases under Georgia association of manufacturers’ recommended alternate rate plan?
AYes.  
qif the commission approves lower base revenue increases than Georgia Power company is proposing, how should these lower increases be allocated by rate group?
AThe same parity adjustments should be applied. 

6. Other Tariff Terms and Conditions
qis georgia power Company proposing any changes in its tariff rules? 
AYes. GPC is proposing Rule 9 be added to its Rules and Regulations for Electric Service, Contract and Enforcement Regulations, on January 1, 2023.  Proposed Rule 9 states:
Without restricting the limitations of liability provided elsewhere within these rules, to the extent a cognizable claim arises, the customer waives any right to consequential, special, indirect, treble, exemplary, incidental, punitive, loss of business reputation, interruption of electric service or loss of use (including loss of revenue, profits, or capital costs) damages in connection with any outage, surge, voltage fluctuation, disturbance or other variation or failure of electric service, or the Company’s equipment, even where such variation, interruption, or failure of electric service was reasonably foreseeable, contemplated, or avoidable.  To the extent the Company is liable under an agreement, and to the extent allowed by applicable law, the Company’s liability is expressly limited to proven direct damages.  The Company’s liability for damage to personal property will be limited to the depreciated value of such personal property.
 qWHat is the reason for having A LIMITATION OF LIABILITY PROVISION?
ALimitation of liability provisions are designed to protect the utility from the risks of incurring costs caused by equipment failures and/or outages. Through Rule 9, GPC aims to limit its liability to compensate the customer based on net book value of the property damaged by an equipment failure or outage, even when damages are due to gross negligence.
 qWHAT IS NET BOOK VALUE?
 ANet book value is an asset's original cost minus its accumulated depreciation.
qWHAT ARE YOUR CONCERNS WITH THE PROPOSED RULE 9 LANGUAGE? 
ARule 9 absolves GPC of responsibility for any damages, even if equipment failures or outages were the result of gross negligence.  Further, limiting damages to the net book value of the customer’s damaged equipment is not a sufficient remedy.  
qWHAT IS GROSS NEGLIGENCE?
ABlack’s Law Dictionary defines gross negligence as:  A conscious, voluntary act or omission in reckless disregard of a legal duty and of the consequences to another party, who may typically recover exemplary damages. 
qHow would the proposed rule 9 absolve georgia Power Company of liability due to gross negligence? 
ARule 9 would waive GPC's liability, "even where such variation, interruption, or failure of electric service was reasonably foreseeable, contemplated, or avoidable." This language demonstrates GPC’s intent to leave customers defenseless against gross negligence through Rule 9.
 qshould georgia power Company be absolved of any responsibility due to gross negligence?
ANo.  GPC should be held accountable for its decisions and actions so that it does not prioritize its self-interest over the safety and well-being of customers.  Further, it is well within the Company’s direct control to avoid actions that could be deemed to be willful misconduct or grossly negligent.  Otherwise, customers would be placed in an unfairly vulnerable position.
qWhy is limiting COMPENSATION for damages BASED ON the NET BOOK VALUE of the customer’s damaged equipment concerning?
ANet book value decreases an asset's worth through accumulated depreciation, which is the depreciation or amortization expense per year multiplied by the number of years. If GPC were to damage a fully depreciated asset, the asset's owner could not claim any compensation under Rule 9. 
qIS THE VALUE OF DAMAGED property or equipment THE ONLY DAMAGES A CUSTOMER MAY INCUR AS A RESULT OF EQUIPMENT FAILURES OR SERVICE OUTAGES?
ANo.  Any service disruption (for whatever reason) can be costly to a customer.  For example, product can be lost, order fulfillment delayed, and equipment may require additional maintenance and/or replacement.  Some processes may require many hours or days to restore production to pre-outage levels.  
qWHY ARE YOU ASSERTING THAT THE PROPOSED RULE 9 IS NOT CONSISTENT WITH INDUSTRY BEST PRACTICES?
AI conducted a survey of electric utility tariffs to determine whether other utilities have a similar rule to Rule 9. Through this survey, I came to the conclusion that GPC's Rule 9 proposal is overly protective.  
First, several utilities have specific language addressing gross negligence.  For example:
El Paso Electric Company states: "the Company will be liable to the Customer for any damages occasioned by such suspension only when the damages are the result of gross negligence or willful misconduct on the part of the Company.”  
Public Service Company of Oklahoma (PSO) states: “PSO will not be liable for any damages, whether direct or consequential, including without limitation, loss of profits, loss of revenue, or loss of production capacity occasioned by fluctuations or interruptions unless it be shown PSO has not made reasonable provisions to supply steady and continuous electric service, consistent with the customer’s class of service or in compliance with Oklahoma Corporation Commission Electric Utility Rules.”
PECO Energy Company states: "the liability of the Company to customers or other persons for damages, direct or consequential, including damage to computers and other electronic equipment and appliances, loss of business, or loss of production caused by any interruption, reversal, spike, surge or variation in supply or voltage, transient voltage, or any other failure in the supply of electricity shall in no event, unless caused by the willful and/or wanton misconduct of the Company, exceed an amount in liquidated damages equivalent to the greater of $1000 or two times the charge to the customer for the service affected during the period in which such interruption, reversal, spike, surge or variation in supply or voltage, transient voltage, or any other failure in the supply of electricity occurs.”
Duke Energy Florida states: “The Customer shall indemnify, hold harmless and defend the Company from and against any and all liability, proceedings, suits, costs or expense for loss, damage, death or injury to persons or property, in any manner directly or indirectly connected with, or growing out of the use or disposition of electricity by the Customer at or on the Customer’s side of the Point of Delivery, unless such loss, damage, death or injury shall result from the sole negligence of the Company.
Further, to the extent the utility is found to have been grossly negligent, the damages paid are not limited to the net book value of the customer’s equipment.  For example:
CenterPoint Energy Houston Electric states:  “Company will not be liable for any damages, whether direct or consequential, including, without limitation, loss of profits, loss of revenue, or loss of production capacity, occasioned by fluctuations or interruptions unless it be shown that Company has not made reasonable provision to supply steady and continuous Delivery Service, consistent with the Retail Customer’s class of service, and in the event of a failure to make such reasonable provisions, whether as a result of negligence or otherwise, Company’s liability shall be limited to the cost of necessary repairs of physical damage proximately caused by the service failure to those electrical delivery facilities of Retail Customer which were then equipped with the protective safeguards recommended or required by the then current edition of the National Electrical Code.”
El Paso Electric Company states, “in the event of the Company’s gross negligence or willful misconduct, the Company’s liability under this paragraph will be limited to the cost of necessary repair or replacement of tangible property damaged or destroyed by the interruption or fluctuation.”
While the above examples specify a broad range of damage, the vast majority of tariffs are not specific about the amount of any damages. Therefore, the damages can be determined by litigation on a situational basis.
qWHAT DO YOU RECOMMEND?
AI recommend that Rule 9 be revised to require that GPC be liable for damages from equipment failures or outages that are demonstrated to be the result of GPC’s gross negligence.  Further, the compensation to customers for such outages should be sufficient to at least replace any damaged equipment.  Alternatively, the Rule should not limit the amount of the damages, consistent with industry practice.  

7. Conclusion
qwhat findings should the commission make on the issues addressed in your testimony?
AThe Commission should make the following findings:
Securitization is a lower cost option than traditional recovery of retired assets and CCR costs.  
The remaining costs of Plant Wansley and the CCR costs incurred through 2022 but not yet recovered in rates, and up to 50% of the CCR costs GPC expects to occur in the ARP, should be identified and separated from the costs recovered in base rates and the ECCR. 
The base rate increases should be implemented in three steps. 
To alleviate rate pressure and to mitigate the amount of redundant capacity recovered in rates, GPC should amortize its remaining investment in Scherer Units 1, 2, and 3 over their current remaining lives.  
The authorized base rate increases should be spread to customer groups in a manner that continues the movement toward parity.  
The proposed Rule 9 should be modified so that GPC will be liable for damages that are determined to be the result of willful misconduct and gross negligence, and the amount of damages either should not be specified, or should be sufficient to at least replace the customer’s damaged equipment and/or personal property. 
qDoes this conclude your direct testimony?
AYes.

APPENDIX C
Assessment of Georgia Power Company’s
Class Cost-of-Service Study
Procedures for Conducting a Class Cost-of-Service Study
QWHAT PROCEDURES ARE USED IN A COST-OF-SERVICE STUDY?
AThe basic procedure for conducting a CCOSS is fairly simple.  First, we identify the different types of costs (functionalization), determine their primary causative factors (classification), and then apportion each item of cost among the various rate classes (allocation).  Adding up the individual pieces gives the total cost for each class. 
Identifying the utility’s different levels of operation is a process referred to as functionalization.  The utility’s investments and expenses are separated into production, transmission, distribution, and other functions.  To a large extent, this is done in accordance with the Uniform System of Accounts developed by FERC.  
Once costs have been functionalized, the next step is to identify the primary causative factor (or factors).  This step is referred to as classification.  Costs are classified as demand-related, energy-related or customer-related.  Demand (or capacity) related costs vary with peak demand, which is measured in kilowatts (kW).  This includes production, transmission, and some distribution investment and related fixed Operation and Maintenance (O&M) expenses.  As explained later, peak demand determines the amount of capacity needed for reliable service.  Energy-related costs vary with the production of energy, which is measured in kilowatt-hours (kWh).  Energy-related costs include fuel and variable O&M expense.  Customer-related costs vary directly with the number of customers and include expenses such as meters, service drops, billing, and customer service.  
Each functionalized and classified cost must then be allocated to the various customer classes.  This is accomplished by developing allocation factors that reflect the percentage of the total cost that should be paid by each class.  The allocation factors should reflect cost causation; that is, the degree to which each class caused the utility to incur the cost.  
qwhat key principles are recognized in a class cost-of-service study?
AA properly conducted CCOSS recognizes two key cost-causation principles.  First, customers are served at different delivery voltages.  This affects the amount of investment the utility must make to deliver electricity to the meter.  Second, since cost causation is also related to how electricity is used, both the timing and rate of energy consumption (i.e., demand) are critical.  Because electricity cannot be stored for any significant time period, a utility must acquire sufficient generation resources and construct the required transmission facilities to meet the maximum projected demand, including a reserve margin as a contingency against forced and unforced outages, severe weather, and load forecast error.  Customers that use electricity during the critical peak hours cause the utility to invest in generation and transmission facilities.  
QWHAT factors CAUSE the per-unit costs to DIFFER AMONG CUSTOMER CLASSES?
AFactors that affect the per-unit cost include whether a customer’s usage is constant or fluctuating (load factor), whether the utility must invest in transformers and distribution systems to provide the electricity at lower voltage levels, the amount of electricity that a customer uses, and the quality of service (e.g., firm or non-firm).  In general, industrial consumers are less costly to serve on a per-unit basis because they:  
operate at higher load factors; 
take service at higher delivery voltages; and 
use more electricity per customer.  
Further, non-firm service is a lower quality of service than firm service.  Thus, non-firm service is less costly per unit than firm service for customers that otherwise have the same characteristics.  This explains why some customers pay lower average rates than others.
For example, the difference in the losses incurred to deliver electricity at the various delivery voltages is a reason why the per-unit energy cost to serve is not the same for all customers.  More losses occur to deliver electricity at distribution voltage (either primary or secondary) than at transmission voltage, which is generally the level at which industrial customers take service.  This means that the cost per kWh is lower for a transmission customer than a distribution customer.  The cost to deliver a kWh at primary distribution, though higher than the per-unit cost at transmission, is lower than the delivered cost at secondary distribution.  
In addition to lower losses, transmission customers do not use the distribution system.  Instead, transmission customers construct and own their own distribution systems.  Thus, distribution system costs are not allocated to transmission level customers who do not use that system.  Distribution customers, by contrast, require substantial investments in these lower voltage facilities to provide service.  Secondary distribution customers require more investment than primary distribution customers.  This results in a different cost to serve each type of customer.  
Two other cost drivers are efficiency and size.  These drivers are important because most fixed costs are allocated on either a demand or customer basis.  
Efficiency can be measured in terms of load factor.  Load factor is the ratio of average demand (i.e., energy usage divided by the number of hours in the period) to peak demand.  A customer that operates at a high load factor is more efficient than a lower load factor customer because it requires less capacity for the same amount of energy.  For example, assume that two customers purchase the same amount of energy, but one customer has an 80% load factor and the other has a 40% load factor.  The 40% load factor customers would have twice the peak demand of the 80% load factor customers, and the utility would therefore require twice as much capacity to serve the 40% load factor customer as the 80% load factor.  Said differently, the fixed costs to serve a high load factor customer are spread over more kWh usage than for a low load factor customer.  
qdoes Georgia Power Company’s class cost-of-service study generally follow the above procedures?
AYes.  In particular, GPC classifies all production and transmission plant and related operating expenses as demand-related costs, and it also recognizes that a portion of the distribution network is a customer-related cost.  The classification of these costs not only comport with generally accepted cost of service principles, they are consistent with cost causation and system planning criteria.  
For example, GPC classified a portion of its distribution network investment as customer-related.  This is consistent with the purpose of the distribution system, which is to deliver power from the transmission grid to the customer, where it is eventually consumed.  Certain investments (e.g., meters, service drops) must be made just to attach a customer to the system.  These investments are customer-related.  
Classification of Distribution Network Costs
QWHAT DO YOU MEAN BY THE DISTRIBUTION NETWORK?
AThe distribution "network" consists of GPC’s investment in poles, towers, fixtures, overhead lines, and line transformers.  These investments are booked to FERC Accounts 364, 365, 366, 367, and 368.  
qwhy is it appropriate to classify a portion of the DISTRIBUTION network INVESTMENTS AS A CUSTOMER-RELATED COST?
AClassifying a portion of the distribution network as a customer-related cost recognizes the reality that every utility must provide a physical connection and voltage support to allow electricity to be delivered to each and every customer regardless of the customer’s peak demand or energy consumed.  Further, that path and the required voltage support must be in place if the utility is to meet its obligation to provide service upon demand.
Absent a connection to the system, a customer cannot take power.  Further, the connecting facilities must be sized to provide voltage support before any power or energy can be consumed.  These prerequisites (i.e., a grid connection with facilities sized to provide voltage support) are clearly related to the existence of the customer.  
Further, it is a recognized and accepted practice.  For example, the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commission Manual states that:
Distribution plant Accounts 364 through 370 involve demand and customer costs.  The customer component of distribution facilities is that portion of costs which varies with the number of customers.  Thus, the number of poles, conductors, transformers, services, and meters are directly related to the number of customers on the utility’s system.   
For this reason, many utilities and state regulatory commissions have approved CCOSSs in which a significant portion of the distribution network is classified as a customer-related cost.  
Classification and Allocation of Production Plant
qyou previously stated that Georgia Power Company classified all production plant as a demand-related cost.  is this practice consistent with cost causation?
AYes.  Classifying all production plant as a demand-related cost is consistent with system planning principles.  It recognizes a utility’s obligation to provide reliable service at the lowest reasonable cost.  It also recognizes that once an investment is made to meet peak demand, that same investment can be used to meet demand throughout the year.  The latter is a bi-product of meeting peak demand.  Meeting peak demand is the cost-causer.  
Further, proposals to classify production plant as energy-related are both overly simplistic and fraught with internal inconsistencies.  For example, advocates of energy-based allocations often cite the lower cost of operating base load units as a reason to allocate the higher capital costs on an energy basis.  However, such advocates often fail to allocate the lower fuel costs to those customer classes that purportedly benefit more from base load capacity.  The reality is that all classes are served from system resources.  Any methodology that is premised on an assumption that each customer class is served from a different mix of resources that does not also address the allocation of both capital costs and operating costs is, at best, an over-simplification of the planning process and is inherently flawed.  
qare there any aspects of Georgia Power Company’s class cost-of-service study that do not reflect cost-causation principles?
AYes.  GPC’s preferred CCOSS allocates production and a portion of transmission costs using the 12CP method.  The 12CP method allocates costs to customer classes based on their contribution to each of the 12 monthly system peaks.  The 12CP method gives equal weight to all 12 monthly peaks.  That is, March and April (which are always low demand months) are treated the same as July and August (which are always high demand months).  This equal weighting fails to recognize that GPC has pronounced peaks in the summer.  
qDOES THE 12CP METHOD VIOLATE THE PRINCIPLE OF COST CAUSATION?  
AYes.  Utility system load characteristics are an important factor in determining the specific method which should be employed to allocate demand-related costs. If a utility is to fulfill its obligation to provide continuous service, it must install sufficient generation capacity to meet the expected peak demands of its customers and to provide an adequate reserve margin.  
Thus, consistent with cost causation, a utility that has a high summer peak demand relative to the demands in other seasons should allocate production plant costs relative to each customer class's contribution to that summer peak (or peaks).  Summer peak demands are still the primary driver in determining how much capacity is needed by GPC to provide reliable service.  
Thus, either the 4CP or Annual System Peak (1CP) methods would explicitly recognize the seasonal (summer-peaking) nature of GPC's load and would be more appropriate and better reflect cost causation than the 12CP method.  
qGeorgia Power Company is experiencing a growing convergence between its summer and winter system peaks.  Does this justify using the 12CP method?
ANo.  Despite the recent winter peaks, which are due to extreme cold weather, GPC is still projecting to be a summer-peaking utility on a weather-normalized basis.  Thus, GPC continues to plan resources to meet the summer peak because the summer peaks are higher and more prolonged in nature than the recently experienced winter peaks.  I would also observe that the entire southeast region remains summer-peaking.  
qis there an allocation methodology that would give some weight to the growing winter peak demand?
AYes.  As an alternative to 12CP, a methodology similar to what Santee Cooper uses (i.e., the average of the summer and winter peaks) would give explicit weight to both the growing winter peak demand as well as the summer peak demand, which is still the primary planning criteria.  Based on GPC’s test-year load characteristics, the highest peak demands are projected to occur in the months January, June, July, August, September, and December.  Thus, a method that reflects both seasonal peak demands would give weight to GPC’s growing winter peak.  
Marginal Cost Rates 
qHOW DOES Georgia Power Company HANDLE marginal cost rates, such as real time pricing, IN its class COST-OF-SERVICE STUDY? 
AGPC's CCOSS includes both embedded costs and revenues, as well as marginal costs and revenues for RTP customers. Thus, GPC's CCOSS is a hybrid study which incorporates both embedded and marginal costing principles due to the inclusion of marginal cost-based rates (i.e., RTP). GPC's hybrid treatment parallels the design of RTP in which a portion of the service (i.e., the CBL) is priced under a standard tariff, which reflects average or embedded costs, while the incremental load is priced to reflect marginal costs. In GPC's CCOSS, embedded production and transmission costs are allocated to the CBL portion of the RTP loads using the same allocation methods as apply to all non-RTP classes. For the incremental loads, GPC allocates distribution costs for service provided at distribution voltage, and it directly assigns the marginal or incremental costs to the RTP classes. The latter step is accomplished by assuming that the test period incremental cost of serving RTP customers is equal to the expected incremental revenue, which is based on the revenue GPC expects to receive during the test period from customers on RTP rates. 
qIS IT REASONABLE TO ALLOCATE EMBEDDED COSTS ONLY TO THE Customer baseline load PORTION OF REAL TIME PRICING?
AYes.  Embedded production and transmission costs are not allocated to the incremental RTP load because the incremental load is capable of responding to price under appropriate circumstances.  Further, real time prices recover marginal production, transmission, and system energy costs.  Therefore, it would be inappropriate to allocate embedded production, transmission, and system energy costs to the incremental RTP load.  To do so would charge RTP customers twice for these services.
qSHOULD An EMBEDDED class COST-OF-SERVICE STUDY BE USED to determine THE cost of providing REAL TIME PRICING service?
ANo. An embedded CCOSS should not be used to measure the cost of providing RTP service.  This is because hourly (RTP) prices are based on marginal costs.  An embedded CCOSS allocates average, rather than marginal costs, to customer classes.  Thus, using an embedded CCOSS to determine the cost of providing RTP service would create an economic mismatch because the revenues are based on marginal cost, while the allocated costs are based on average costs.  Incremental RTP load should be priced using marginal cost principles, and not allocated embedded costs.  
qdoes Georgia Power Company’s treatment of the rEAL TIME PRICING rates in its class cost-of-service study recognize that rEAL TIME PRICING is a marginal cost-based rate?
AYes.  GPC's adjustments appropriately recognize that a full allocation of embedded costs should only apply to the CBL component of the RTP usage, while incremental costs should be directly assigned to the marginal loads.  The Commission should reject any attempt to allocate full embedded costs to loads which are priced through marginal cost methodologies.  
Why Production Plant Costs Are Not Energy-Related  
QWHY DO SOME ANALYSTS RECOMMEND ALLOCATING A PORTION OF PRODUCTION PLANT COSTS ON AN ENERGY BASIS?  
AEnergy-based allocation methods are based on a theory that the “extra” investment associated with certain types of generation (i.e., units that are operated as base load and/or load following) provides fuel cost savings.  Since fuel costs are typically allocated on an energy (or kWh) basis, the assumption is that this investment is also driven by kWh sales.  This theory is referred to as Capital Substitution.  Capital Substitution overlooks four realities:
The need for new capacity is driven by both projected peak demands and reserve requirements to ensure that electricity is reliable.  Using 12CP to allocate a portion of production plant that GPC considers demand-related does not recognize the peak demands that drive capacity needs.  
Fuel savings are not a cost driver.  All new plants save fuel costs because of improvements in generation technology, not because they are more capital intensive.  Although the choice of plant technology is determined by economics, the objective is to provide reliable service at the lower overall cost and not solely to lower fuel costs.  
Combined cycle gas turbines (CCGTs) have become the technology of choice not because they have lower fuel costs but because they can provide flexible load following capabilities needed to balance loads and resources in real time and meet operating reserve requirements.  
An energy allocation assumes all hours are critical to the choice of generation.  However, not all production from a specific plant determines the type of capacity to install.  Thus, allocating investment to all hours is contrary to cost causation.  
QHOW IS ALLOCATING INVESTMENT TO ALL HOURS CONTRARY TO COST CAUSATION?  
AThe following simplified example of how an energy allocation is contrary to cost causation.  Let us suppose two drivers are required to rent cars from a fleet that contains only two types of cars, “Car P” and “Car B”:

Car P

Car B has a high fixed charge and gets high mileage (like a base load plant), while Car P has a low fixed charge but gets poor mileage (like a peaker).  The graph below shows total cost of both cars over a range of miles driven.  

The total cost is also calculated in the following table.  As can be seen, the break-even point between Car P and Car B is 1,000 miles.

That is, Car B has a lower total cost per mile than Car P if it is operated more than 1,000 miles.  If one customer needed to drive 1,500 miles and a second customer needed to drive a car 4,500 miles, both customers would choose to drive Car B.  In other words, the decision to drive Car B was based on whether the car would be driven at least 1,000 miles.  It doesn’t matter how many miles over 1,000 it will be driven.
The same break-even construct applies to electric utilities.  For example, assuming the break-even point between Base Load and Peaking capacity is 1,000 hours per year, it doesn’t matter whether the Base Load plant will operate 5,000 hours, 6,000 hours, of 8,000 hours per year.  Thus, load duration can affect the decision of whether to install Base Load or Peaking capacity.  However, once the decision is made, duration beyond the break-even point is irrelevant.  
Energy-based allocation methods ignore this fundamental planning construct because investment would be allocated to all kWh usage.  This is at odds with Capital Substitution because the extra investment in base load generation can be justified by load duration up to the economic break-even point between Base Load and Peaking capacity.  That is, once a utility decides that additional production capacity is needed to meet peak demand, if that new capacity is expected to run only a limited number of hours, total costs are minimized by the choice of a peaker.  On the other hand, if it is projected that a unit will run for a sufficient number of hours, then a load following or base load unit will be more economical.  
Therefore, annual energy usage does not cause plant investment.  However, load duration up to the break-even point may influence plant investment decisions.  Beyond the break-even point, energy utilization is no longer a factor in the decision to select base load capacity or peaking capacity.  
QWHY ELSE ARE ENERGY-BASED ALLOCATION METHODS WRONG?  
AEnergy-based allocation methods also suffer from the lack of symmetry in how plant and fuel costs are allocated (i.e., the “Fuel Symmetry” problem) and average demand is double-counted (i.e., the Double-Counting” problem).  
The fuel symmetry problem was one of the primary reasons cited by the Public Utility Commission of Texas in rejecting every type of energy-based allocation method proposed in rate cases throughout the 1980s and 1990s (see for example Docket No. 5560; Docket No. 5700; Docket Nos. 7460 and 7172; Docket No. 8032).  
For example, in Docket No. 7460, the Commission adopted the Hearing Examiner’s Report, which cited the apparent lack of “fuel symmetry” in rejecting capital substitution, and energy-based allocation method.  
The Examiner’s find that the most important flaw in Dr. Johnson’s capital substitution methodology is the lack of symmetry, both as to fuel and as to operations and maintenance expense.  To the extent that relative class energy consumption becomes the primary factor in apportioning capacity costs as between customer classes, as is the case with Dr. Johnson’s proposal, the high load factor classes, which will bear the higher cost responsibility for base load units will not also receive the benefit of the lower operating costs and lower fuel costs associated with those units.
QWHAT IS THE DOUBLE-COUNTING PROBLEM INHERENT IN AN ENERGY-BASED ALLOCATION METHOD?  
ADouble-counting can occur when plant-related costs are properly allocated partially on a coincident peak basis and an average demand (or energy) basis.  Average demand is annual energy consumption divided by 8,760 hours.  It is also a component of coincident peak demand.  This is illustrated in the following figure for a hypothetical summer peaking utility.  

Average demand is equivalent to the black shaded area of the chart.  Coincident peak demand is represented by the combined black and blue shaded areas.  Double-counting occurs because coincident peak demand incorporates average demand.  
By allocating some plant-related costs relative to average demand and some relative to coincident peak demand, energy usage is counted twice in the allocation process: once by itself and a second time as a subset of coincident peak demand.  If you presume that base load units are built to meet average year-round demand, then it follows that the only time load-following (e.g., intermediate and peaking) units would be needed is when system demands exceed the average demand.  Capital Substitution proponents would allocate the cost of this additional capacity relative to coincident peak demand (i.e., the entire bar including both the black and blue portions of the bars), rather than just the excess demand (i.e., the blue portion of the bar).  
QHAS THE DOUBLE-COUNTING PROBLEM BEEN CITED AS A CRITICAL FLOW IN ENERGY-BASED ALLOCATION METHODOLOGIES?  
AYes.  For example, both the Iowa Utilities Board and the Public Utility Commission of Texas have cited the double-counting problem in numerous cases.  Specifically, the Public Utility Commission of Texas states:
As to double-counting energy, the flaw in Dr. Johnson’s proposal is the fact that the allocator being used to allocate peak demand, and 50% of the intermediate demand, includes with it an energy component.  Dr. Johnson has elected to use a 4CP demand allocator, but such an allocator, because it looks at peak energy usage, necessarily includes within that peak usage average usage, or energy. 
* * *
A substantial portion of average demand is being utilized in two different allocators, and this “double dipping” is taking place.

                       DIRECT TESTIMONY OF JEFFRY POLLOCK


                1. INTRODUCTION, QUALIFICATIONS AND SUMMARY

 1   Q   PLEASE STATE YOUR NAME AND BUSINESS ADDRESS.

 2   A   Jeffry Pollock; 12647 Olive Blvd., Suite 585, St. Louis, MO 63141.


 3   Q   WHAT IS YOUR OCCUPATION AND BY WHO ARE YOU EMPLOYED?

 4   A   I am an energy advisor and President of J. Pollock, Incorporated.


 5   Q   PLEASE STATE YOUR EDUCATIONAL BACKGROUND AND EXPERIENCE.

 6   A   I have a Bachelor of Science Degree in Electrical Engineering and a Masters in

 7       Business Administration from Washington University in St. Louis, Missouri. Since

 8       graduation in 1975, I have been engaged in a variety of consulting assignments,

 9       including energy procurement and regulatory matters in both the United States and

10       several Canadian provinces. My qualifications are documented in Appendix A. A list

11       of my appearances is provided in Appendix B to this testimony.


12   Q   ON WHOSE BEHALF ARE YOU TESTIFYING IN THIS PROCEEDING?

13   A   I am testifying on behalf of the Georgia Association of Manufacturers (GAM). The

14       members of GAM are customers of Georgia Power Company (GPC). They consume

15       large quantities of electricity, primarily for manufacturing under a variety of tariffs. GAM

16       members purchase nearly 11% of GPC’s retail energy sales.

 1   Q   WHAT ISSUES ARE YOU ADDRESSING IN YOUR TESTIMONY?

 2   A   This is the first of many rate increases that the Commission will decide over the next

 3       three years, which I will discuss initially. I am also addressing:

 4          •   The proposed Alternate Rate Plan (ARP);
 5          •   Cost recovery for regulatory assets;
 6          •   Class revenue allocation; and
 7          •   Other tariff terms and conditions.

 8   Q   ARE ANY OTHER WITNESSES APPEARING ON BEHALF OF GEORGIA

 9       ASSOCIATION OF MANUFACTURERS?

10   A   Yes. My colleague, Ms. Billie S. LaConte, will address the issues raised by GPC

11       witness, James M. Coyne, in determining the return on equity (ROE) and capital

12       structure. Dr. Roger Tutterow will address the state of manufacturing in Georgia.


13   Q   ARE YOU SPONSORING ANY EXHIBITS WITH YOUR TESTIMONY?

14   A   Yes. I’m sponsoring Exhibits JP-1 through JP-9. These exhibits were prepared by

15       me or under my supervision and direction.


16   Q   DOES THE FACT THAT YOU ARE NOT ADDRESSING ALL ISSUES IN THIS

17       PROCEEDING CONSTITUTE AN ENDORSEMENT OF THE POSITIONS OF

18       GEORGIA POWER COMPANY, OR OTHER PARTIES, ON THESE ISSUES?

19   A   No. The fact that I am not addressing every issue should not be interpreted as an

20       endorsement of GPC’s or other parties’ proposals in this proceeding.

 1   Summary

 2   Q   PLEASE SUMMARIZE YOUR FINDINGS AND RECOMMENDATIONS.

 3   A   My findings and conclusions are as follows:

 4       This Case in Perspective
 5          •   GPC is on the precipice of a “perfect storm” of at least seven rate
 6              increases that could result in cumulative rate increases of up to $2.9
 7              billion over the next three years.
 8          •   Even before the first of three step increases is implemented under
 9              GPC’s proposed ARP, customers taking service under the Real Time
10              Pricing (RTP) rates have already experienced double-digit rate
11              increases.
12          •   Higher RTP prices have generated $565 million of contributions to fixed
13              costs during the current ARP through July 2022. Thus far in 2022, the
14              fixed cost contributions through July 2022 alone ($191 million) exceed
15              the $95 million of fixed cost contributions included in GPC’s test-year
16              revenue requirement.
17          •   To offset future rate increases, the Commission should identify
18              alternatives to traditional ratemaking — securitization in particular —
19              that would allow the Company to recover its capital costs while
20              minimizing rate impacts on customers.
21          •   Securitization is an accepted practice for recovering large capital
22              investments (retired plant) and other major unexpected costs (storm
23              damage) to alleviate the upward rate pressures facing GPC’s
24              customers. The cost of securitizing utility assets is only a fraction of
25              GPC’s pre-tax cost of capital under traditional ratemaking. Further,
26              securitization bonds can extend cost recovery for periods ranging from
27              four to over 20 years, thereby further alleviating upward rate pressure.
28       Alternate Rate Plan
29          •   The proposed ARP would implement three step increases effective on
30              January 1, 2023, 2024, and 2025. However, the vast majority of the
31              increase would occur on January 1, 2023.
32          •   The three step increases are based entirely on three projected test
33              years. Use of three projected test years requires accurate projections
34              of future sales, revenues, investments, expenses and interest rates.
35              Such projections are inherently inaccurate, particularly, in this case, for
36              the 2nd and 3rd years of the proposed ARP.
                                                               1 . Introduction, Qualifications
                                                                                 and Summary
                                          J.POLLOCK
                                          INCORPORATED

                                                                          Jeffry Pollock
                                                                          Direct
                                                                          Page 4

 1      •   GPC’s past projections have allowed it to earn above the authorized
 2          midpoint ROE in each of the last ten years. In six of those years, the
 3          earned ROE exceeded the upper end of the authorized ROE band.
 4      •   In light of past experience, the base rate and Environmental Compliance
 5          Cost Recovery (ECCR) increases should be implemented in three
 6          steps, and GPC should be required to update its base rate and
 7          traditional ECCR projections prior to implementing the 2nd and 3rd
 8          years of the ARP.

 9   Cost Recovery of Major Assets
10      •   Higher depreciation and amortization expenses are a major driver of the
11          proposed base revenue increase. These higher expenses are a direct
12          result of GPC’s Integrated Resource Plan (IRP) to retire/early retire
13          major power plants (i.e., Wansley, Scherer, Bowen Units 1-2) with over
14          $3.1 billion of remaining (unrecovered) investment.
15      •   GPC is also seeking recovery of the costs of the six capacity power
16          purchase agreements (PPAs). Excluding Bowen Units 1-2 (for which a
17          retirement decision was deferred), the capacity of these PPAs roughly
18          offsets the capacity of the retired/early retired plants.
19      •   GPC is proposing to earn a return on both the retired/early retired plants
20          and as a result of the additional sums on the six capacity PPAs.
21      •   The simultaneous recovery of both retired/early retired plants and the
22          six capacity PPAs effectively charges retail customers for redundant
23          capacity.
24      •   Retired plants no longer provide electricity to the public. Thus, the costs
25          of retired plants are not used and useful. A utility should not earn a
26          return on equity on investments in facilities that are not used and useful.
27      •   To avoid placing unnecessary upward pressure on rates, GPC should
28          amortize its investment in Scherer Unit Nos. 1, 2 and 3 over their current
29          remaining lives, which range from 20-25 years.
30      •   GPC is proposing to continue deferred recovery of coal combustion
31          residual (CCR) costs, thereby earning a return on any amounts that
32          have not been recovered in rates.
33      •   GPC is projecting to incur $986 million in CCR costs during the ARP.
34          This is in addition to the CCR costs that have been incurred but not yet
35          recovered in rates.


                                                           1 . Introduction, Qualifications
                                                                             and Summary
                                      J.POLLOCK
                                      INCORPORATED

                                                                           Jeffry Pollock
                                                                           Direct
                                                                           Page 5

 1      •   Securitizing the remaining investments in Plant Wansley and the as-
 2          incurred/projected CCR costs would place less upward pressure on
 3          rates than under traditional ratemaking.
 4      •   Until securitization is authorized, GPC should not earn a return on equity
 5          on the regulatory assets (i.e., Plant Wansley, CCR costs).

 6   Class Revenue Allocation
 7      •   GPC’s proposed equal percentage base rate increase would not result
 8          in moving rate groups closer to parity.
 9      •   Any base rate increase authorized by the Commission should continue
10          the policy established in the 2010 rate case and continued in the 2013
11          and 2019 rate cases to continue moving each rate group closer to parity.

12   Other Tariff Terms and Conditions
13      •   GPC’s proposed Rule 9 would absolve it of any liability for damages
14          sustained by customers, even if outages or equipment failures were the
15          result of gross negligence. Further, GPC is proposing to limit its liability
16          for damages to the net book value of the customer’s equipment.
17      •   A customer’s damages from electricity outages can be much more
18          extensive than the net book value of the equipment or personal property.
19          For example, outages can result in lost product, lost sales, additional
20          maintenance expense and potentially higher replacement costs for new
21          equipment.
22      •   Consistent with best practices, Rule 9 should be revised so that GPC is
23          liable for damages caused by willful misconduct and gross negligence.
24          The amount of damages should, at a minimum, be sufficient to replace
25          the customer’s damaged equipment and/or personal property.

                          2. THE RATE CASE IN PERSPECTIVE

 1   Q   WHAT IS THE COMPANY REQUESTING IN THIS CASE?

 2   A   GPC is asking the Commission to continue the process of a three-year ARP with a

 3       one-time “levelized” base rate increase taking effect on January 1, 2023 and two

 4       additional step increases for specific cost components that would become effective on

 5       January 1, 2024 and January 1, 2025. Originally, GPC requested a one-time $738

 6       million levelized base revenue increase.        However, in its Supplemental filing it

 7       increased the request to $762 million. The $37.6 million additional base revenue

 8       increase reflects various items included in the final Order approved in the 2022 IRP

 9       and Demand Side Management (DSM) proceedings.


10   Q   ARE THESE THE ONLY RATE INCREASES THAT GEORGIA POWER COMPANY

11       WILL BE SEEKING FROM THE COMMISSION?

12   A   No. As I have testified in past cases, we are on the precipice of a “perfect storm” of

13       rate increases that will occur over the next three years. Table 1 provides an estimate

14       of the potential impact of the seven future rate increases. As Table 1 reveals, GPC

15       will be seeking cumulative rate increases of up to $2.9 billion over the next three years.

                                          Table 1
                                   Upcoming Rate increases
                                                                          Amount

              Spring 2023      Fuel Cost Recovery                             $1,100
                               Vogtle 3-4 Up to Certified Amount
              Fall 2023                                                         $600
                               Net of FCR Decrease

              Jan. 2024        ARP Step 2                                       $107

 1             As can be seen, the proposed ARP is but one component of the upcoming rate

 2             increases. GPC will be adjusting rates to recognize the commercial operation of

 3             Vogtle Unit 3 when it is placed in commercial operation in the spring of 2023 and

 4             further in the fall of 2024, and when fuel is loaded into Vogtle Unit 4 in the fall of 2023.

 5             In addition, GPC reported a $948 million fuel cost recovery (FCR) under-collection in

 6             June.1 The balance has continued to grow significantly despite the Interim Fuel Rider

 7             that was implemented on January 1, 2022. Assuming gas prices remain at current

 8             high levels, the FCR rate increase could exceed $1.1 billion.

 9                     In summary, retail customers are potentially facing $2.9 billion in rate increases

10             over the next three years.

11       Q     HAVE ANY RETAIL CUSTOMERS ALREADY EXPERIENCED SUBSTANTIAL

12             RATE INCREASES?

13       A     Yes. Even before Step 1 of the proposed ARP takes effect, customers taking service

14             under the RTP rate schedules have already experienced and will continue paying

15             record high prices.

 1       The much higher prices can be attributed to much higher replacement fuel costs

 3   Q   WHAT HAS BEEN THE IMPACT OF THE HIGH RTP PRICES?

 4   A   Higher RTP prices have resulted in much higher contributions to fixed costs by RTP

 5       customers. The fixed cost contributions consist of:

 6          •   A Reliability adder that reflects the marginal cost of generation capacity;
 7          •   A Transmission adder that reflects the marginal cost of transmission
 8              capacity; and
 9          •   Risk adders ranging from $1 to $3 per megawatt-hour (MWh) for Day
10              Ahead (DA) and Hour Ahead (HA) pricing.


                                                               2. The Rate Case in Perspective
                                          J.POLLOCK
                                          INCORPORATED

                                                                                Jeffry Pollock
                                                                                Direct
                                                                                Page 9


 1       The Reliability and Transmission adders apply when demand approaches and

 2       exceeds the projected Southern Company system peak, and the Risk adders apply to

 3       all hours.

 4               As a result of the higher prices, RTP customers have paid $565 million in fixed

 5       cost contributions during the current ARP through July 2022. In 2022 alone, they

 6       contributed $191 million to fixed costs through July, which is twice GPC’s projected

 7       ($95 million) test-year fixed cost contributions. Thus, it is clear that a large segment

 8       of GPC’s retail customers has already experienced substantial rate increases, even

 9       before base rates are adjusted. Further, GPC’s non-RTP customers have benefitted

10       and continue to benefit from the fixed costs that RTP customers pay.


11   Q   RECOGNIZING THE PERFECT STORM OF UPCOMING RATE INCREASES AND

12       THE MUCH HIGHER RTP PRICES, WHAT STEPS SHOULD THE COMMISSION

13       TAKE TO PROTECT THE INTERESTS OF RETAIL CUSTOMERS?

14   A   The objective should be to minimize future rate impacts while affording GPC a

15       reasonable opportunity to earn a reasonable return on the facilities that are used and

16       useful in providing electricity service to the public.


17   Q   HOW CAN THE COMMISSION ACHIEVE THIS OBJECTIVE?

18   A   First, the Commission should thoroughly scrub each of the three projected test years

19       used to support GPC’s proposed ARP.

20               Second, recognizing that GPC’s proposed 11% ROE is approximately 150

21       basis points above the national average, the Commission should, at a minimum,

22       establish an ROE and capital structure based on the specific corrections to Mr.

23       Coyne’s testimony as recommended by Ms. LaConte.

                                                                  2. The Rate Case in Perspective
                                           J.POLLOCK
                                           INCORPORATED

                                                                                    Jeffry Pollock
                                                                                    Direct
                                                                                    Page 10

 1                    Third, the Commission should identify alternatives to traditional ratemaking that

 2             would allow the Company to recover its capital costs while minimizing rate impacts on

 3             customers. For example, $297 million of the proposed revenue requirement reflects

 4             changes in depreciation rates.2 As discussed later, GPC is proposing to accelerate

 5             the recovery of certain assets due to recent retirement decisions and extend the

 6             depreciable lives of other assets. Accelerated/extended depreciation can alleviate

 7             rate pressure. However, this option is not cost-free because GPC would continue to

 8             earn a return on the unamortized balance. The only alternative that would both

 9             alleviate rate pressure and reduce the overall revenue requirement is securitization.


10       Q     WHAT IS SECURITIZATION?

11       A     Securitization is a financing mechanism that provides timely recovery of certain

12             regulatory assets by issuing a type of debt that would significantly lower the carrying

13             costs of the assets relative to the costs that would be incurred using traditional

14             ratemaking practices. Specifically, a special purpose entity would be created whose

15             sole purpose is to issue transition bonds that are used solely for purposes of reducing

16             the amount of recoverable regulatory assets and any other amounts as determined by

17             the Commission through the refinancing or retirement of utility debt or equity. This

18             special purpose entity would be entitled to the revenues collected by the utility through

19             a separate cost recovery mechanism in an amount sufficient to timely recovery of the

20             debt services associated with securitization bonds.


     2   Direct Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B.
         Robinson, Exhibit___(APA/SPA/ADH/MBR-5, Schedule 1).

                                                                     2. The Rate Case in Perspective
                                                J.POLLOCK
                                                INCORPORATED

                                                                               Jeffry Pollock
                                                                               Direct
                                                                               Page 11

 1                 Under this structure, securitization bonds would be “AAA” rated and carry the

 2       lowest possible interest rate. For example, recently issued securitization bonds have

 3       had an average interest rate of 3.6%. This is substantially below GPC’s proposed

 4       9.8% pre-tax cost of capital. The much lower cost reflects the fact that there is no

 5       equity and no federal and/or state income taxes associated with securitized financing.

 6       Further, securitization bonds issued to date have maturities ranging from four to over

 7       20 years. Therefore, securitization financing would be the most economical means of

 8       recovering the costs of long-term regulatory assets, such as retired coal plants and

 9       CCR obligations.


10   Q   IS SECURITIZATION WIDELY USED?

11   A   Yes. Exhibit JP-1 shows that 27 states, the District of Columbia and Puerto Rico have

12       enacted utility securitization laws. Thus, securitization is authorized in the majority of

13       states.


14   Q   HOW IS SECURITIZATION FINANCING TYPICALLY USED?

15   A   Initially, securitization was used to recover utility assets that became stranded when

16       generation was no longer subject to price regulation. More recently, securitization has

17       become more widely used to recover retired coal plant assets, to finance major storm

18       damage, and to recover extraordinary costs incurred during Winter Strom Uri.


19   Q   CAN SECURITIZATION BE BENEFICIAL FOR CUSTOMERS?

20   A   Yes. If the legislation is properly drafted, securitization can be a more cost-effective

21       means of recovering retired assets and other obligations than under traditional

22       ratemaking. To demonstrate, Exhibit JP-2 provides an illustration of the impact of

23       securitizing $1.5 billion of assets.     The illustration shows the annual revenue

                                                               2. The Rate Case in Perspective
                                           J.POLLOCK
                                           INCORPORATED

                                                                              Jeffry Pollock
                                                                              Direct
                                                                              Page 12

 1       requirement in both nominal and net present value (NPV) dollar costs to customers

 2       under traditional ratemaking (the solid green and dashed black lines) and

 3       securitization (the orange bars). For purposes of the illustration, I have used GPC’s

 4       proposed cost of capital, current state/federal income tax rates and both accelerated

 5       (9-year) and nominal (20-year) amortization. Finally, I assumed an average interest

 6       rate of 3.61% with upfront costs of 2% of the securitizable amount. The interest rate

 7       assumption was based on a securitization issued by Entergy Texas, Inc., on March

 8       25, 2022.


 9   Q   HOW DOES SECURITIZATION COMPARE WITH TRADITIONAL RATEMAKING?

10   A   The vertical axis shows the annual revenue requirement. The higher the annual

11       revenue requirement, the higher the rate impact.

12              As can be seen, the annual revenue requirement for securitizing $1.5 billion of

13       regulatory assets is less than $150 million per year over the 14-year average life of

14       the securitization bonds.     By contrast, the annual revenue requirements under

15       traditional ratemaking (for either a nine or 20-year amortization period) would be higher

16       than for securitization. Table 2 below compares the net cost to retail customers under

17       those options.

                                             Table 2
                                 Net Cost to Retail Customers
                     $3 Billion Securitization Vs. Traditional Ratemaking
                                           ($ Billions)
                                                 Nominal            NPV
                            Scenario              Cost              Cost
                     Traditional Ratemaking:
                          9 Year Recovery          $2.3             $1.7
                          20 Year Recovery         $3.0             $1.9
                     Securitization                $2.0             $1.5

                                          J.POLLOCK
