                                                                                  Jeffry Pollock
                                                                                  Direct
                                                                                  Page 13

 1       Thus, not only would securitization place less upward pressure on rates, it would be

 2       less costly for customers on both a nominal and NPV basis.


 3   Q   HAVE CREDIT RATING AGENCIES EXPRESSED ANY OPINIONS ON THE USE

 4       OF SECURITIZED FINANCING?

 5   A   Yes. For example, Moody’s Investors Service opined that utility cost recovery through

 6       securitization is credit positive provided that it is not over-used. A copy of Moody’s

 7       assessment is provided in Exhibit JP-3. The key takeaways are:

 8          •   Securitization typically benefits utilities and their current customers
 9              because utilities receive an immediate source of cash and are ensured
10              recovery of large costs in a timely manner, and because the cost of
11              securitized debt is lower than the utility’s all-in pre-tax cost of capital, it
12              reduces the impact on customers’ monthly bills.
13          •   Utility cost recovery charge securitization has become more versatile
14              and prevalent. It is now used to recover costs associated with storm
15              restoration and environmental costs, utility restructuring, deferred fuel
16              costs and renewable energy projects.
17          •   State law and financing orders strongly protect securitization assets
18              which ultimately protect the assets backing the bonds.
19          •   Too much securitization can have negative consequences such as
20              impacting customer bills substantially while hurting the utility’s financial
21              flexibility and ability to raise rates for other reasons.

22       Regulatory Research Associates, a subsidiary of S&P, recently observed that there

23       has been a rekindled interest in securitization, stating:

24          This financing method allows utilities to include the revenue stream to repay
25          the bonds within customer bills as a separate, non-bypassable surcharge that
26          is subject to true-up. Amounts collected by the company through the surcharge
27          are remitted to a special purpose entity, which then disburses the payments to
28          investors.
29          While it is unclear if securitization requires legislation, a specific legislative
30          mandate generally improves the rating accorded to the securitization bonds
31          and lowers the associated cost of capital, given that a legislatively supported
32          revenue stream may be more difficult to rescind than a stand-alone order of a

                                                                 2. The Rate Case in Perspective
                                           J.POLLOCK
                                           INCORPORATED

                                                                                Jeffry Pollock
                                                                                Direct
                                                                                Page 14

 1              state commission. In RRA's experience, no state utility regulatory commission
 2              has authorized securitization in the absence of enabling legislation.
 3              Securitization is viewed as an attractive option because it allows
 4              regulators to reduce the customer rate impacts related to the recovery of
 5              a particular utility asset. The carrying charge on the asset would be the
 6              lower interest rate applied to a highly rated, usually AAA, corporate bond
 7              as compared to the utility's WACC, which includes both equity and debt
 8              components, or even the interest rate applied to typical utility corporate
 9              bonds, which are generally rated BBB and carry higher interest rates.
10              At the same time, securitization reduces the investment risk for the utility
11              by providing the utility up-front recovery of its investment in what are
12              usually non-revenue-producing assets — assets that are not dedicated to
13              providing service to current ratepayers. The company can then redeploy those
14              investment dollars elsewhere.3

15   Q      IS SECURITIZATION POSSIBLE UNDER THE CURRENT REGULATORY AND

16          LEGAL ENVIRONMENT IN GEORGIA?

17   A      No. It is my understanding that securitization would require both a change in law and

18          regulations. I also understand that a securitization bill has been drafted and reviewed

19          by several parties, including GPC. Thus, there is no reason why a securitization bill

20          could not be introduced and enacted in the next legislative session.


21   Q      WHAT CAN THE COMMISSION DO TO FACILITATE SECURITIZATION?

22   A      This rate case provides an opportunity to identify the capital assets that would most

23          likely be candidates for securitized financing. For example, as discussed later, GPC

24          is seeking full recovery of the now-retired Plants Wansley and Boulevard. GPC

25          continues to defer CCR costs and is seeking to recover $986 million of additional CCR


     3S&P Capital IQ, RRA Regulatory Focus, Kan. commission OK's storm cost bond issuance for Atmos
     Energy (Oct. 14, 2022). Emphasis added.

                                                                2. The Rate Case in Perspective
                                             J.POLLOCK
                                            INCORPORATED

                                                                                     Jeffry Pollock
                                                                                     Direct
                                                                                     Page 15

 1             costs over the duration of the ARP.4 Both the retired plants and unrecovered/expected

 2             CCR costs would be ideal candidates for securitization.

 3                    Therefore, I recommend that the Commission quantify and set-aside the

 4             portion of costs associated with Plant Wansley and the unrecovered/expected CCR

 5             costs in this proceeding. Setting aside these specific costs will facilitate a timely rate

 6             adjustment once GPC issues securitization bonds to finance these costs, while

 7             avoiding double-recovery.


 8       Q     HOW SHOULD THESE COSTS BE RECOVERED PRIOR TO SECURITIZATION?

 9       A     As previously stated, Plant Wansley was retired and the investment is not used and

10             useful. Further, the CCR costs that are associated with Plant Wansley and other

11             retired power plants are also not used and useful. Under these circumstances, the

12             Commission should not allow a return on the unamortized balance.               However,

13             recognizing GPC’s need to finance regulatory assets, the Commission should

14             authorize a return capped at GPC’s cost of long-term debt.

                                3. ALTERNATE RATE PLAN


 1   Q   HOW IS GEORGIA POWER COMPANY PROPOSING TO IMPLEMENT ITS

 2       ALTERNATE RATE PLAN?

 3   A   The proposed ARP is shown in Table 3. Also shown for comparison is the annual

 4       revenue increase for the test period, as well as the three step increases under the ARP.

                                            Table 3
                                      Alternate Rate Plan
                                          ($ Millions)
                Rate             Test
              Component         Period     Levelized      1/1/23   1/1/24     1/1/25

            Base Rates              $318       $762         $478     $269        $359

            CCR                      $60        N/M          $64      $78         $47

            ECCR                     $44          $5         $33     ($38)       ($12)

            Total Excl MFF          $460                    $612     $336        $392

           Sources: APA-SPA-ADH-MBR-1, Schedules 1, 2 and 3 Supplemental.

 5       As previously stated, GPC is proposing to implement a one-time levelized increase in

 6       base rates and the traditional ECCR rate. Alternatively, GPC could have proposed

 7       three smaller base rate increases.


 8   Q   DO YOU HAVE ANY CONCERNS REGARDING GEORGIA POWER COMPANY’S

 9       ALTERNATE RATE PLAN?

10   A   Yes. The proposed ARP is based entirely on three projected test years: calendar year

11       2023, calendar year 2024, and calendar year 2025. None of these projections reflect

12       GPC’s official corporate budgets, which were prepared last fall. Use of three projected

13       test years requires accurate projections of future sales, revenues, investments,


                                                                         3. Alternate Rate Plan
                                           J.POLLOCK
                                           INCORPORATED

                                                                            Jeffry Pollock
                                                                            Direct
                                                                            Page 17

 1       expenses and interest rates. Such projections, particularly in the 2nd and 3rd years of

 2       the proposed ARP, are inherently inaccurate.


 3   Q   IS GEORGIA POWER COMPANY PROPOSING TO UPDATE ITS PROJECTIONS

 4       PRIOR TO IMPLEMENTING THE 2ND AND 3RD YEAR INCREASES UNDER THE

 5       PROPOSED ALTERNATE RATE PLAN?

 6   A   No. With the exception of the CCR and DSM rate components, which would be

 7       updated in November prior to the following rate year, the base rate and ECCR

 8       components would be fixed based on the three projected test years.


 9   Q   WHY DO YOU ASSERT THAT GEORGIA POWER COMPANY’S FORECASTS ARE

10       INHERENTLY INACCURATE?

11   A   Exhibit JP-4 shows GPC’s earned ROE for the past 10 years under the then-applicable

12       Accounting Orders. Under these Accounting Orders, GPC was authorized to earn a

13       mid-point ROE ranging from 10.5% to 11.15%. The ceiling ROE ranged from 12% to

14       12.25%, and the ROE floor ranged from 9.5% to 10.25%.

15              As can be seen in Exhibit JP-4, reliance on GPC’s projections has resulted in

16       GPC consistently earning a ROE above the mid-point in each of the last 10 years.

17       Further, GPC earned above the upper end of the ROE range in six of the last 10 years.

18       At no time did GPC’s earnings ever fall below the ROE mid-point or, obviously, the

19       ROE floor.

20              Thus, based on this past experience, GPC’s projections have either overstated

21       future costs, or understated future sales and/or revenues.

 1   Q   WHAT DO YOU RECOMMEND?

 2   A   Recognizing the other substantial rate increases likely to occur in 2023 (as listed in

 3       Table 1), prior experience as discussed earlier, and to provide a more gradual rate

 4       path, GAM members strongly prefer a three-stepped increase over GPC’s proposed

 5       levelized increase. GPC should be required to update its base rate and traditional

 6       ECCR projections prior to implementing the 2nd and 3rd years of the ARP. Thus, for the

 7       years 2024 and 2025, new base rates should be set using updated test-year projections

 8       based on GPC’s then-official corporate budgets. The updated budget projections

 9       should be filed within 60 days prior to the effective date of new rates. This would

10       provide the Commission and parties ample time to review the projections prior to the

11       rates becoming effective.


12   Q   HAS THE COMMISSION PREVIOUSLY APPROVED STEPPED BASE RATE

13       INCREASES IN AN ALTERNATE RATE PLAN?

14   A   Yes.   In the last rate case, GPC also requested a levelized base rate increase.

15       However, the Commission approved a settlement between GPC, the Commission

16       Staff, GAM and other parties authorizing three stepped base rate increases.

 1    Q      DO YOU HAVE CONCERNS WITH THE COMPANY’S COST RECOVERY

 2           PROPOSALS?

 3    A      Yes. First, retail customers would be charged for redundant capacity. Second, Plant

 4           Wansley (and after 2028, Scherer Unit Nos. 1, 2 and 3) is no longer used and useful.

 5           A utility should not expect to earn a return on investment for facilities that are no longer

 6           used and useful in providing electricity to the public. Third, abruptly changing the

 7           depreciation schedule for Scherer Unit Nos 1, 2, and 3 is unfair to GPC's current

 8           ratepayers.


 9    Q      IS THERE ANY PRECEDENT FOR DENYING A RETURN ON A PLANT THAT IS

10           NOT USED AND USEFUL?

11    A      Yes. In Docket No. 46449, the Public Utility Commission of Texas denied a proposal

12           by a utility to include its investment in the then-retired Welsh Unit 2 in rate base, thereby

13           denying a return on the unamortized balance.           In rejecting the utility’s proposed

14           ratemaking treatment, the Commission stated:

15               68. Because Welsh unit 2 is no longer used and useful, SWEPCO may not
16               include its investment associated with the plant in its rate base, and may not
17               earn a return on that remaining investment.

18               69. Allowing SWEPCO a return of, but not on, its remaining investment in Welsh
19               unit 2 balances the interests of ratepayers and shareholders with respect to a
20               plant that no longer provides service.

21               70. It is reasonable for SWEPCO to recover the remaining undepreciated
22               balance of Welsh unit 2 over the 24-year remaining lives of Welsh units 1 and
23               3.6


     6 Application of Southwestern Electric Power Company for Authority to Change Rates, Docket No. 46449,

       Order on Rehearing at 20 (Mar, 19, 2018).

 1       Q     WHAT DO YOU RECOMMEND?

 2       A     First, as previously discussed, the remaining investment in Plant Wansley should be

 3             securitized.   This would allow GPC to immediately recover its capital without an

 4             additional return. This is appropriate because, as previously stated, Plant Wansley is

 5             no longer used and useful. In the interim, Plant Wansley regulatory asset should be

 6             amortized over eight years with no return on equity.

 7                     Second, to avoid placing unnecessary upward pressure on rates, GPC should

 8             amortize its investment in Scherer Unit Nos. 1, 2 and 3 over their current remaining

 9             lives, which range from 20-25 years, with a return on the unamortized balance.

10             However, once Scherer Unit Nos. 1, 2 and 3 have been retired, the remaining

11             investments should be securitized.

12       CCR Costs

13       Q     IS GEORGIA POWER COMPANY PROPOSING TO CONTINUE ITS RECOVERY OF

14             COAL COMBUSTION RESIDUAL COSTS?

15       A     Yes. GPC is proposing to continue the recovery of CCR costs while earning a return

16             on the unamortized CCR obligation. In 2021, GPC recovered $166 million of CCR

17             costs in rates.7 By 2025, GPC projects that the CCR annual revenue requirement

18             would reach $386 million.8 Thus, it is a significant and growing component of GPC’s

19             overall revenue requirement. But for the sheer magnitude of the CCR obligation, which

20             GPC estimates at $6.8 billion, these costs would be expensed, and GPC would not

21             earn a return on the unamortized CCR balance.9


     7   Georgia Power Company’s 2021 Annual Surveillance Report, Docket No. 42516, Schedule 15, at 2.
         (Mar. 15, 2022).
     8   Direct Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B.
         Robinson, Exhibit___(APA/SPA/ADH/MBR-1, Schedule 3 ECCR) at 6.

                                                                 4. Cost Recovery of Major Assets
                                                  J.POLLOCK
                                                  INCORPORATED

                                                                              Jeffry Pollock
                                                                              Direct
                                                                              Page 24


 1   Q   IS A RETURN ON UNAMORTIZED BALANCE THE ONLY COST ASSOCIATED

 2       WITH COAL COMBUSTION RESIDUAL COST RECOVERY?

 3   A   No. The return applied to the unamortized CCR obligation includes a return on equity.

 4       Further, because equity is taxable, retail customers are paying not only an additional

 5       return to shareholders, they are also funding the income tax liability associated with the

 6       shareholder return. The inclusion of both a return on equity and associated income

 7       taxes places unnecessary upward pressure on rates.


 8   Q   HOW CAN THIS UNNECESSARY UPWARD PRESSURE BE ALLEVIATED?

 9   A   GPC should securitize all unrecovered CCR expenses, as well as a substantial portion

10       of the CCR expenses that will be incurred during the ARP. As previously stated,

11       securitization is a less expensive alternative to traditional ratemaking because it is

12       financed solely by much lower debt cost. Further, securitization bonds can be issued

13       with extended maturities at a cost that is less than 50% of GPC’s pre-tax cost of capital.

14       Prior to securitization, the CCR regulatory asset should earn a return based on GPC’s

15       long-term cost of debt.

                                          INCORPORATED

                                                                             Jeffry Pollock
                                                                             Direct

                            5. CLASS REVENUE ALLOCATION


 1   Q   WHAT IS CLASS REVENUE ALLOCATION?

 2   A   Class revenue allocation is the process of determining how any base revenue change

 3       the Commission approves should be spread to each customer class the utility serves.


 4   Q   HOW IS GEORGIA POWER COMPANY PROPOSING TO ALLOCATE THE

 5       PROPOSED BASE REVENUE INCREASE IN THIS PROCEEDING?

 6   A   GPC is proposing to spread the proposed base revenue increase by applying an

 7       approximately equal percentage increase to current base revenues. This is shown in

 8       Exhibit JP-5.


 9   Q   WOULD AN EQUAL PERCENTAGE INCREASE MOVE RATE GROUPS CLOSER

10       TO PARITY?

11   A   No. As discussed later, the class cost-of-service study (CCOSS) results show that the

12       rate groups are not currently at parity. Some rate groups are below parity while other

13       rate groups are above parity. Moving rates toward parity means applying above-

14       average base revenue increases to those rate groups that are currently below parity

15       and below-average base revenue increases to those rate groups that are currently

16       above parity.


17   Q   WHAT IS A CLASS COST-OF-SERVICE STUDY?

18   A   A CCOSS is an analysis used to determine each class’s responsibility for a utility’s

19       costs. Thus, it determines whether the revenues a class generates cover the class’s

20       cost of service. A CCOSS separates the utility's total costs into portions incurred on

21       behalf of the various customer groups. Most of a utility's costs are incurred to jointly

                                                                  5. Class Revenue Allocation
                                          J.POLLOCK
                                          INCORPORATED

                                                                                Jeffry Pollock
                                                                                Direct
                                                                                Page 26


 1       serve many customers. For purposes of revenue allocation and rate design, customers

 2       are grouped into homogeneous classes according to their usage patterns and service

 3       characteristics. The procedures typically used in a CCOSS are described in more detail

 4       in Appendix C.


 5   Q   HAS GEORGIA POWER FILED A CLASS COST-OF-SERVICE STUDY IN THIS

 6       CASE?

 7   A   Yes. GPC filed four CCOSSs – two studies were based on calendar year 2021 (the

 8       historical test period) and two studies were based on the test year ending July 31, 2023.

 9       One set of studies was presented by rate group and by rate schedule while the other

10       set of studies was presented by customer class.


11   Q   DID GEORGIA POWER ACKNOWLEDGE AN ERROR IN THE FILED CLASS COST-

12       OF-SERVICE STUDIES?

13   A   Yes. GPC acknowledged that there was an error in the filed CCOSSs. Specifically,

14       the filed studies overstated the revenues from the Medium Business rate group and

15       understated the revenues from the Marginally Priced rate group. A corrected CCOSS

16       was provided.


17   Q   DOES THE CORRECTED CLASS COST-OF-SERVICE STUDY GENERALLY

18       COMPORT WITH ACCEPTED INDUSTRY PRACTICE?

19   A   Yes.   The corrected CCOSS generally comports with accepted industry practice.

20       Specifically, it recognizes the different types of costs incurred by the utility, as well as

21       the different ways electricity is delivered to, and used by, the various types of

22       customers. In particular:


                                                                    5. Class Revenue Allocation
                                           J.POLLOCK
                                           INCORPORATED

                                                                                Jeffry Pollock
                                                                                Direct
                                                                                Page 27


 1          •   All production and transmission plant costs and related expenses were
 2              classified to demand.
 3          •   A portion of the distribution network was classified as a customer-related
 4              cost.
 5          •   For the Marginally Priced rate group, production/transmission demand
 6              allocation factors were based on the customer baseline load (CBL),
 7              which is the load that GPC must plan to serve.

 8       Thus, GPC’s CCOSSs are consistent with the principles of cost causation; that is, costs

 9       are allocated to rate groups based on the degree that each rate group caused GPC to

10       incur each specific cost.

11              This is in stark contrast to theoretical studies that allocate production plant costs

12       on energy usage on the mistaken belief that some investment is incurred to save fuel

13       costs or that individual power plants benefit certain customer classes more than others

14       and, therefore, should be allocated differently, rather than as a unified generation fleet.

15       A more in-depth assessment of GPC’s CCOSSs and these other competing allocation

16       theories is provided in Appendix C.


17   Q   ARE THERE DIFFERENCES BETWEEN THE TWO SETS OF COST STUDIES?

18   A   Yes.   The CCOSSs used the same methodologies except for the allocation of

19       production and transmission plant and related expenses. GPC’s preferred CCOSS

20       allocates production and 20% of transmission plant and related expenses using the

21       twelve coincident peak (12CP) method.         The remaining transmission costs were

22       allocated to classes using the four coincident peak (4CP) method.


23   Q   PLEASE DESCRIBE THE 12CP AND 4CP METHODS.

24   A   Under 12CP, each class’s share of allocated costs is based on the demand that occurs

25       coincident with the system peak in all 12 months. GPC also filed a second CCOSS

                                                                    5. Class Revenue Allocation
                                           J.POLLOCK
                                           INCORPORATED

                                                                                     Jeffry Pollock
                                                                                     Direct
                                                                                     Page 28
 1       using the 4CP method. Like 12CP, the 4CP method allocates costs based on the

 2       demand that occurs coincident with the system peak during the summer months, June

 3       through September.


 4   Q   WHAT IS THE RATIONALE FOR USING A SPECIFIC DEMAND ALLOCATION

 5       METHOD, SUCH AS 12CP OR 4CP.

 6   A   The choice of an allocation method should reflect the load characteristics of the utility.

 7       For example, 4CP is appropriate for a utility that is primarily summer peaking, while

 8       12CP is appropriate for a utility that has no discernable peak periods. The rationale for

 9       using 12CP and 4CP is discussed in more detail in Appendix C.


10   Q   IS GEORGIA POWER COMPANY A SUMMER PEAKING UTILITY?

11   A   Yes. Chart 2 below compares the monthly system peaks as a percent of GPC’s annual

12       system peak. The red bars denote when the annual system peak occurs. The red/blue

13       bars show the months when the system demand is close to the annual system peak.

14       The other months are shown in the blue bars.

                                          Chart 2
                         Monthly Peak Demands as a Percent of
                    GPC’s Historical and Test Year Annual System Peak
                           100%


                             90%


                             80%


                             70%


                             60%


                             50%
                                    J   F   M   A   M   J   J   A   S   O   N   D


                                                                            5. Class Revenue Allocation
                                            J.POLLOCK
                                            INCORPORATED

                                                                            Jeffry Pollock
                                                                            Direct
                                                                            Page 29

 1       As Chart 2 demonstrates, GPC is summer-peaking. However, GPC’s winter peaks are

 2       growing faster than its summer peaks, and as a consequence, GPC will have both

 3       summer and winter peaks in future test years.


 4   Q   ARE THERE DIFFERENCES IN THE SHARE OF PRODUCTION/TRANSMISSION

 5       COSTS ALLOCATED TO EACH RATE GROUP UNDER THE 12CP AND 4CP

 6       METHODS?

 7   A   Yes. Exhibit JP-6 provides a comparison of the allocation factors for each rate group

 8       under 12CP (column 1) and 4CP (column 3). Recognizing the convergence between

 9       the summer and winter peaks, I have also quantified the allocation factors based on an

10       average of the four summer and two winter system peaks (column 2). As can be seen,

11       each method allocates a different share of production/transmission costs to rate

12       groups.


13   Q   WHICH OF THE ALLOCATION METHODS SHOWN IN EXHIBIT JP-6 BEST FITS

14       GEORGIA POWER COMPANY’S SYSTEM LOAD CHARACTERISTICS?

15   A   Currently, the 4CP method best reflects GPC’s load characteristics. However, in the

16       future the average of the four summer/two winter CP method will become a better fit as

17       the summer and winter peaks converge. As can be seen in Exhibit JP-6, the allocation

18       factors under this approach more closely resemble 4CP than the 12CP. demand

19       allocation factors.

20              In any event, the 12CP method is not reflective of GPC’s load characteristics.

21       Thus, the results of GPC’s preferred CCOSS are not an accurate reflection of the parity

22       ratios. The additional flaws with the 12CP method are discussed in Appendix C.


                                                                 5. Class Revenue Allocation
                                         J.POLLOCK
                                         INCORPORATED

                                                                             Jeffry Pollock
                                                                             Direct
                                                                             Page 30

 1   Q   DESPITE THE DIFFERENCES IN THE VARIOUS ALLOCATION METHODOLOGIES

 2       SHOWN IN EXHIBIT JP-5, DO THE RESULTS OF THE CORRECTED CLASS COST-

 3       OF-SERVICE STUDIES CONFIRM THAT CERTAIN RATE GROUPS ARE EITHER

 4       ABOVE OR BELOW PARITY?

 5   A   Yes. Exhibits JP-7 and JP-8 present the corrected CCOSS results at present rates

 6       under the 12CP/4CP and 4CP methods described above. The results are summarized

 7       in Table 6 below. If the rates were at parity, each rate group would have a parity ratio

 8       (or relative rate of return) of 100%. As Table 6 demonstrates, the Medium and Large

 9       Business groups are well above parity, while the Domestic and Government/Institution

10       groups are well below parity.

11   Q   REFERRING TO EXHIBITS JP-7 AND JP-8, PLEASE EXPLAIN THE TERMS RATE

12       OF RETURN, RELATIVE RATE OF RETURN, AND SUBSIDY.

13   A   The rate of return (column 3) is the net operating income (i.e., revenues less allocated

14       expenses – column 2) divided by the allocated rate base (column 1). The relative rate

                                                                  5. Class Revenue Allocation
                                          J.POLLOCK
                                          INCORPORATED

                                                                             Jeffry Pollock
                                                                             Direct
                                                                             Page 31

 1       of return or parity ratio (column 4) measures each rate group’s rate of return (column

 2       3) as a percent of the 6.31% Total Retail rate of return (line 9, column 3). The subsidy

 3       (column 5), measures the difference between actual revenues and the allocated costs.

 4       Thus, a negative amount means that a class is providing revenues less than its

 5       allocated costs, while a positive amount means that a class is providing revenues

 6       above its allocation costs.


 7   Q   DO YOU HAVE ANY FURTHER OBSERVATIONS ABOUT THE CORRECTED

 8       CLASS COST-OF-SERVICE STUDY RESULTS?

 9   A   Yes. The results for the Marginally Priced rate group are significantly affected by the

10       test-year contribution to fixed costs by RTP customers.         This is because RTP

11       customers are included in this class, and the contributions to fixed costs is a large

12       portion of the revenue stream from RTP sales. As discussed previously (in Section 2),

13       fixed cost contributions from RTP sales during the test-year were only $95 million,

14       which was only 50% of the ($191 million) of fixed cost contributions in the first seven

15       months of 2022. Thus, the net revenues and rates of return from the Marginally Priced

16       rate group are clearly under-stated. This group is clearly much more profitable to GPC

17       than is indicated in the corrected CCOSSs.


18   Q   DOES THE COMMISSION HAVE TO APPROVE A SPECIFIC CLASS COST-OF-

19       SERVICE STUDY TO DETERMINE AN APPROPRIATE CLASS REVENUE

20       ALLOCATION?

21   A   No. Despite the different methodologies, the CCOSS results are relatively consistent.

22       The rate groups that are below parity under GPC’s preferred CCOSS are also below

23       parity under each of the alternative CCOSSs, and vice versa.


                                                                  5. Class Revenue Allocation
                                          J.POLLOCK
                                          INCORPORATED

                                                                                   Jeffry Pollock
                                                                                   Direct
                                                                                   Page 32

 1   Q         WOULD RATES MOVE CLOSER TO PARITY UNDER GEORGIA POWER

 2             COMPANY’S PROPOSED CLASS REVENUE ALLOCATION?

 3   A         No. The movement to parity is shown in Exhibit JP-9 using GPC’s preferred CCOSS

 4             for illustrative purposes. As can be seen, GPC’s equal percent base increases applied

 5             to each rate group would, for the most part, result in higher subsidies. Thus, rates

 6             would not move closer to parity.


 7   Q         HAS THE COMMISSION ENDORSED MOVING RATES CLOSER TO PARITY?

 8   A         Yes. In the 2010, 2013, and 2019 rate cases, the Commission approved class revenue

 9             allocations that moved rates closer to parity. In each case the Commission approved

10             parity adjustments ranging from 75% to 125% of the system average base revenue

11             increase.10 For example, in 2010 the Commission found:

12                 …the allocation of costs and rate design contemplated and specified in the
13                 Stipulation are reasonable and in the best interests of all customer groups, and
14                 represent a balanced and fair settlement of the positions advocated by the
15                 parties in this docket.11

16             Furthermore, in the 2013 rate case the Commission stated:

17                 The resulting rate increases will be allocated by rate group using the revenue
18                 distribution method recommended by Staff Witness Watkins, with some
19                 exceptions and with the spread adjusted for balancing. (Panel Rebuttal
20                 Testimony, p.19). The exceptions are based on testimony by the Company and
21                 several intervenors with regard to further movements towards parity and the
22                 importance of the RTP rate to remain as currently designed and for further RTP
23                 tariff availability. (Panel Rebuttal Testimony, p 19-20). In order to further
24                 address rate parity issues, in particular with respect to the TOU-MB rate, that

 1                group will receive a rate increase of 80% of what would otherwise be allocated

 3            In the most recent (2019) rate case, the Commission stated:

 4                The Proposed Agreement provides that beginning in 2021, with the adjustment
 5                to traditional base rate tariff, the rate increase shall be allocated by rate group,
 6                except as otherwise provided in Proposed Agreement, with adjustments for
 7                balancing. Rates in Medium Business and Large Business rate groups will
 8                receive 85% of the overall base rate increase with the resulting revenue
 9                deficiency being spread to the other rate groups excluding the Small Business,
10                Lighting and Marginal rate groups which will receive the overall base rate
11                increase (Agreement Provision 17). The Commission finds this provision of
12                the Proposed Agreement to be just and reasonable, and is hereby approved.13

13   Q        SHOULD RATES CONTINUE TO MOVE TOWARD PARITY?

14   A        Yes. Base revenues should reflect the actual cost of providing service to each rate

15            group as closely as practicable. Cost-based rates are fair (because each class’s rates

16            reflect its cost to serve, no more and no less); they are efficient (because, when coupled

17            with a cost-based rate design, customers are provided with the proper incentive to

18            minimize their costs, which will, in turn, minimize the costs to the utility); they enhance

19            revenue stability (because changes in revenues due to changes in sales will translate

20            into offsetting changes in costs); and they encourage conservation (because cost-

21            based rates will send the proper price signals to customers, thereby allowing customers

22            to make rational consumption decisions).


23   Q         ARE THERE ANY OTHER COMPELLING REASONS TO SET RATES CLOSER TO

24            COST IN THIS PROCEEDING?

25   A         Yes. As Dr. Tutterow testifies, manufacturing is essential to creating wealth for local


     12   Docket No. 36989, Order Adopting Settlement Agreement at 10 (Dec. 17, 2013).
     13   Georgia Power Company’s 2019 Rate Case, Docket No. 42516, Order Adopting Settlement
          Agreement as Modified at 13 (Feb. 6, 2020).

 1       communities. However, economic activity has been slowing, industrial production has

 2       decreased modestly in three of the past five quarters, and inflation pressures are

 3       persuasive through the economy. Many businesses will face challenges because in a

 4       competitive global marketplace, it is difficult to pass along higher production costs, such

 5       as the cost of electricity. Further, as previously discussed, much higher RTP prices

 6       have already resulted in higher electricity costs. For all of these additional reasons,

 7       minimizing base rate increases to the Medium and Large Business and Marginally

 8       Priced classes is critical to retaining and potentially increasing manufacturing jobs (and

 9       the attendant economic benefits) in Georgia.

 1       For example, if GPC receives a 7% base rate increase, the target base rate increases

 4       requirement.

 5              Acknowledging gradualism, the target parity adjustments constrained to

 6       between 0.77 times and 1.28 times the system average revenue increase excluding

 7       FCR. These adjustments were applied to each rate group based on the current rate of

 8       return. Rate groups with current parity ratios above 100% would receive below system-

 9       average increases, while rate groups with current parity ratios below 100 would receive

10       above system-average increases.

                              6. OTHER TARIFF TERMS AND CONDITIONS


 1   Q         IS GEORGIA POWER COMPANY PROPOSING ANY CHANGES IN ITS TARIFF

 2             RULES?

 3   A         Yes. GPC is proposing Rule 9 be added to its Rules and Regulations for Electric

 4             Service, Contract and Enforcement Regulations, on January 1, 2023. Proposed Rule

 5             9 states:

 6                 Without restricting the limitations of liability provided elsewhere within these
 7                 rules, to the extent a cognizable claim arises, the customer waives any right to
 8                 consequential, special, indirect, treble, exemplary, incidental, punitive, loss of
 9                 business reputation, interruption of electric service or loss of use (including loss
10                 of revenue, profits, or capital costs) damages in connection with any outage,
11                 surge, voltage fluctuation, disturbance or other variation or failure of electric
12                 service, or the Company’s equipment, even where such variation, interruption,
13                 or failure of electric service was reasonably foreseeable, contemplated, or
14                 avoidable. To the extent the Company is liable under an agreement, and to the
15                 extent allowed by applicable law, the Company’s liability is expressly limited to
16                 proven direct damages. The Company’s liability for damage to personal
17                 property will be limited to the depreciated value of such personal property.14

18   Q         WHAT IS THE REASON FOR HAVING A LIMITATION OF LIABILITY PROVISION?

19   A         Limitation of liability provisions are designed to protect the utility from the risks of

20             incurring costs caused by equipment failures and/or outages. Through Rule 9, GPC

21             aims to limit its liability to compensate the customer based on net book value of the

22             property damaged by an equipment failure or outage, even when damages are due to

23             gross negligence.


24   Q         WHAT IS NET BOOK VALUE?

25   A         Net book value is an asset's original cost minus its accumulated depreciation.


     14   Application, MFRs, Traditional, F1 at 1.60.

                                                                                  6. Other Tariff Terms
                                                                                        and Conditions
                                                   J.POLLOCK
                                                   INCORPORATED

                                                                                          Jeffry Pollock
                                                                                          Direct
                                                                                          Page 37

 1    Q         WHAT ARE YOUR CONCERNS WITH THE PROPOSED RULE 9 LANGUAGE?

 2    A         Rule 9 absolves GPC of responsibility for any damages, even if equipment failures or

 3              outages were the result of gross negligence. Further, limiting damages to the net book

 4              value of the customer’s damaged equipment is not a sufficient remedy.


 5    Q         WHAT IS GROSS NEGLIGENCE?

 6    A         Black’s Law Dictionary defines gross negligence as: A conscious, voluntary act or

 7              omission in reckless disregard of a legal duty and of the consequences to another party,

 8              who may typically recover exemplary damages.15


 9    Q         HOW WOULD THE PROPOSED RULE 9 ABSOLVE GEORGIA POWER COMPANY

10              OF LIABILITY DUE TO GROSS NEGLIGENCE?

11    A         Rule 9 would waive GPC's liability, "even where such variation, interruption, or failure

12              of electric service was reasonably foreseeable, contemplated, or avoidable."16             This

13              language demonstrates GPC’s intent to leave customers defenseless against gross

14              negligence through Rule 9.


15        Q     SHOULD        GEORGIA         POWER       COMPANY          BE     ABSOLVED         OF    ANY

16              RESPONSIBILITY DUE TO GROSS NEGLIGENCE?

17    A         No. GPC should be held accountable for its decisions and actions so that it does not

18              prioritize its self-interest over the safety and well-being of customers. Further, it is well

19              within the Company’s direct control to avoid actions that could be deemed to be willful


     15   West Group, Bryan A. Garner, editor-in-chief, Black’s Law Dictionary, 1st pocket ed. (1996).
     16   Application, MFRs, Traditional, F1 at 1.60.

                                                                                      6. Other Tariff Terms
                                                                                            and Conditions
                                                    J.POLLOCK
                                                    INCORPORATED

                                                                                Jeffry Pollock
                                                                                Direct
                                                                                Page 38

 1       misconduct or grossly negligent. Otherwise, customers would be placed in an unfairly

 2       vulnerable position.


 3   Q   WHY IS LIMITING COMPENSATION FOR DAMAGES BASED ON THE NET BOOK

 4       VALUE OF THE CUSTOMER’S DAMAGED EQUIPMENT CONCERNING?

 5   A   Net book value decreases an asset's worth through accumulated depreciation, which

 6       is the depreciation or amortization expense per year multiplied by the number of years.

 7       If GPC were to damage a fully depreciated asset, the asset's owner could not claim

 8       any compensation under Rule 9.


 9   Q   IS THE VALUE OF DAMAGED PROPERTY OR EQUIPMENT THE ONLY DAMAGES

10       A CUSTOMER MAY INCUR AS A RESULT OF EQUIPMENT FAILURES OR

11       SERVICE OUTAGES?

12   A   No. Any service disruption (for whatever reason) can be costly to a customer. For

13       example, product can be lost, order fulfillment delayed, and equipment may require

14       additional maintenance and/or replacement. Some processes may require many hours

15       or days to restore production to pre-outage levels.


16   Q   WHY ARE YOU ASSERTING THAT THE PROPOSED RULE 9 IS NOT CONSISTENT

17       WITH INDUSTRY BEST PRACTICES?

18   A   I conducted a survey of electric utility tariffs to determine whether other utilities have a

19       similar rule to Rule 9. Through this survey, I came to the conclusion that GPC's Rule 9

20       proposal is overly protective.

21             First, several utilities have specific language addressing gross negligence. For

22       example:


                                                                            6. Other Tariff Terms
                                                                                  and Conditions
                                           J.POLLOCK
                                           INCORPORATED

                                                                                        Jeffry Pollock
                                                                                        Direct
                                                                                        Page 39

 1                  •   El Paso Electric Company states: "the Company will be liable to the
 2                      Customer for any damages occasioned by such suspension only when
 3                      the damages are the result of gross negligence or willful misconduct
 4                      on the part of the Company.”17
 5                  •   Public Service Company of Oklahoma (PSO) states: “PSO will not be
 6                      liable for any damages, whether direct or consequential, including
 7                      without limitation, loss of profits, loss of revenue, or loss of production
 8                      capacity occasioned by fluctuations or interruptions unless it be shown
 9                      PSO has not made reasonable provisions to supply steady and
10                      continuous electric service, consistent with the customer’s class of
11                      service or in compliance with Oklahoma Corporation Commission
12                      Electric Utility Rules.”18
13                  •   PECO Energy Company states: "the liability of the Company to
14                      customers or other persons for damages, direct or consequential,
15                      including damage to computers and other electronic equipment and
16                      appliances, loss of business, or loss of production caused by any
17                      interruption, reversal, spike, surge or variation in supply or voltage,
18                      transient voltage, or any other failure in the supply of electricity shall in
19                      no event, unless caused by the willful and/or wanton misconduct of
20                      the Company, exceed an amount in liquidated damages equivalent to
21                      the greater of $1000 or two times the charge to the customer for the
22                      service affected during the period in which such interruption, reversal,
23                      spike, surge or variation in supply or voltage, transient voltage, or any
24                      other failure in the supply of electricity occurs.”19
25                  •   Duke Energy Florida states: “The Customer shall indemnify, hold
26                      harmless and defend the Company from and against any and all liability,
27                      proceedings, suits, costs or expense for loss, damage, death or injury
28                      to persons or property, in any manner directly or indirectly connected
29                      with, or growing out of the use or disposition of electricity by the
30                      Customer at or on the Customer’s side of the Point of Delivery, unless
31                      such loss, damage, death or injury shall result from the sole
32                      negligence of the Company.20

                                                                                    6. Other Tariff Terms
                                                                                          and Conditions
                                                   J.POLLOCK
                                                   INCORPORATED

                                                                                          Jeffry Pollock
                                                                                          Direct
                                                                                          Page 40

 1              Further, to the extent the utility is found to have been grossly negligent, the damages

 2              paid are not limited to the net book value of the customer’s equipment. For example:

 3                  •   CenterPoint Energy Houston Electric states: “Company will not be liable
 4                      for any damages, whether direct or consequential, including, without
 5                      limitation, loss of profits, loss of revenue, or loss of production capacity,
 6                      occasioned by fluctuations or interruptions unless it be shown that
 7                      Company has not made reasonable provision to supply steady and
 8                      continuous Delivery Service, consistent with the Retail Customer’s class
 9                      of service, and in the event of a failure to make such reasonable
10                      provisions, whether as a result of negligence or otherwise, Company’s
11                      liability shall be limited to the cost of necessary repairs of physical
12                      damage proximately caused by the service failure to those
13                      electrical delivery facilities of Retail Customer which were then
14                      equipped with the protective safeguards recommended or required by
15                      the then current edition of the National Electrical Code.”21
16                  •   El Paso Electric Company states, “in the event of the Company’s gross
17                      negligence or willful misconduct, the Company’s liability under this
18                      paragraph will be limited to the cost of necessary repair or
19                      replacement of tangible property damaged or destroyed by the
20                      interruption or fluctuation.”22

21              While the above examples specify a broad range of damage, the vast majority of tariffs

22              are not specific about the amount of any damages. Therefore, the damages can be

23              determined by litigation on a situational basis.


24    Q         WHAT DO YOU RECOMMEND?

25    A         I recommend that Rule 9 be revised to require that GPC be liable for damages from

26              equipment failures or outages that are demonstrated to be the result of GPC’s gross

27              negligence. Further, the compensation to customers for such outages should be

28              sufficient to at least replace any damaged equipment. Alternatively, the Rule should

29              not limit the amount of the damages, consistent with industry practice.


     21   CenterPoint Energy Houston Electric, LLC, Tariff for Retail Delivery Service, Section 5.2.1, Sheet No.
          5.1 (Jan. 15, 2015).
     22   El Paso Electric Company, Rules and Regulations Regarding Electric Service, Section 2, Sheet No.
          2, 13th Revision at 10-11 (Nov. 3, 2021).

                                                                             Page 41

                                       7. CONCLUSION


 1   Q   WHAT FINDINGS SHOULD THE COMMISSION MAKE ON THE ISSUES

 2       ADDRESSED IN YOUR TESTIMONY?

 3   A   The Commission should make the following findings:

 4          •   Securitization is a lower cost option than traditional recovery of retired
 5              assets and CCR costs.
 6          •   The remaining costs of Plant Wansley and the CCR costs incurred
 7              through 2022 but not yet recovered in rates, and up to 50% of the CCR
 8              costs GPC expects to occur in the ARP, should be identified and
 9              separated from the costs recovered in base rates and the ECCR.
10          •   The base rate increases should be implemented in three steps.
11          •   To alleviate rate pressure and to mitigate the amount of redundant
12              capacity recovered in rates, GPC should amortize its remaining
13              investment in Scherer Units 1, 2, and 3 over their current remaining
14              lives.
15          •   The authorized base rate increases should be spread to customer
16              groups in a manner that continues the movement toward parity.
17          •   The proposed Rule 9 should be modified so that GPC will be liable for
18              damages that are determined to be the result of willful misconduct and
19              gross negligence, and the amount of damages either should not be
20              specified, or should be sufficient to at least replace the customer’s
21              damaged equipment and/or personal property.

22   Q   DOES THIS CONCLUDE YOUR DIRECT TESTIMONY?

23   A   Yes.
